# ZIMDNS RFC standards matrix

**Version:** 2026-10-20 (RFC-parity ledger revision), RFC-first minimal CLI design
**Product profile:** one Zig binary, local-first authoritative DNS, controlled by a CLI; no web UI or admin socket
**Status:** normative release-profile evidence map

This matrix is the source of truth for the declared first-release profile. It separates the current repository baseline from the release target and from work that is deliberately outside the profile. A behavior is user-facing `supported` only after the linked RFC requirement, implementation path, and tests pass. A `partial` row names the subset that is actually covered; it is not a claim of full RFC conformance.

Future work is tracked outside this ledger and does not change any current row or make roadmap items user-facing capabilities; this matrix is the source of truth for the declared first-release profile.

The internal quality objective is to make the declared profile more explicit, test-backed, and safe to operate than comparable deployments. That objective is not product copy, a feature-count promise, or a claim that ZIMDNS is generally superior to another DNS server.

## Status vocabulary

| Status | Meaning | Evidence needed |
|---|---|---|
| `supported` | Implemented for the declared profile and safe to advertise. | RFC section, implementation path, and passing unit/golden/integration tests; malformed-input coverage where applicable. |
| `partial` | Only the named subset is implemented. | Tests cover the named subset and the missing behavior is stated. |
| `planned` | Roadmap intent; not a user-facing capability. | A roadmap item and proposed test linkage. |
| `not-applicable` | The RFC does not define behavior for this DNS-only server. | An RFC-scope rationale; no implementation claim. |
| `out of scope` | Deliberately excluded from this profile. | Rationale and, when useful, a pointer to a later research item. |

## Core protocol and transport

| Area | RFC requirement | Current baseline | First-release target | Implementation path | Test path / evidence |
|---|---|---:|---:|---|---|
| Typed RP RDATA | [RFC 1183 §2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1183#section-2.2): type 17 RP RDATA with the two required domain names, `<mbox-dname>` and `<txt-dname>`, including root-name markers. | `supported`: typed wire parsing/encoding, strict zone-text parsing, deterministic CLI rendering, and generic authoritative delivery cover only the bounded IN profile; this is not a full RFC 1183 claim. | `supported`: authoritative IN-only type 17 data requires exactly two absolute DNS names in zone text, with `.` permitted as the root marker for either field, encodes both names on the wire, and is served through the generic authoritative path. No RP-related additional records are required or emitted by this profile; RFC 1183's optional TXT additional lookup is deferred by citation (RDATA format only), even when the `txt-dname` has an in-zone TXT record. Mailbox or mail-delivery semantics, TXT lookup/follow-up, resolver policy, DNSSEC, and non-IN classes are excluded. | [`src/dns/types.zig`](../src/dns/types.zig), [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig), [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig), [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig), [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig), and [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) | RP type-code, ordinary/compressed-name round-trip, root-field, malformed-name, truncation, and trailing-data tests in [`src/dns/types.zig`](../src/dns/types.zig) and [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig); exact-two-name absolute/root and zone-load rejection tests in [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig); deterministic `RP <mbox-dname> <txt-dname>` CLI rendering and query-type mapping in [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig) and [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig); exact type 17 authoritative wire/RDATA and zero-additional assertions in [`test/golden/golden_test.zig`](../test/golden/golden_test.zig) using [`rp-response.gdns-fixture`](../test/golden/rp-response.gdns-fixture), whose zone contains the target TXT record; and IPv4 loopback UDP/TCP no-synthesized-TXT-additional delivery tests in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig). |
| Typed SRV RDATA | [RFC 2782](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2782) ("The format of the SRV RR"; RFC 2782 has no numbered sections): type 33 SRV RDATA with unsigned 16-bit Priority, Weight, and Port fields plus the Target domain name. | `supported`: typed wire parsing/encoding with uncompressed Target, strict zone-text parsing, deterministic CLI rendering, and generic authoritative UDP/TCP delivery, with golden and loopback socket evidence; this is the bounded IN profile, not a full RFC 2782 claim. | `supported`: authoritative IN-only type 33 data accepts unsigned 16-bit Priority, Weight, and Port values and an absolute, uncompressed Target DNS name, with `.` permitted as the root Target marker. Strict four-field zone text is normalized against `$ORIGIN`, then served through the generic authoritative UDP and TCP paths. Additional synthesis is deferred by citation (RDATA format only): no A or AAAA records are synthesized, even when the Target has in-zone A and AAAA records. Target address or non-alias validation, client Priority/Weight selection, network connections, resolver behavior, DNSSEC, and non-IN classes are excluded. | [`src/dns/types.zig`](../src/dns/types.zig), [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig), [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig), [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig), [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig), and [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) | SRV type-code, numeric-boundary, normal/root Target, compressed-Target rejection, and uncompressed output from compressed-response encoding tests in [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig); strict four-field, `$ORIGIN` normalization, root-Target, and malformed zone-text tests in [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig); deterministic `SRV <priority> <weight> <port> <target>` CLI rendering and query-type mapping in [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig) and [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig); exact type-33 authoritative wire/RDATA and zero-additional assertions in [`test/golden/golden_test.zig`](../test/golden/golden_test.zig) using [`srv-response.gdns-fixture`](../test/golden/srv-response.gdns-fixture); and separate IPv4 loopback UDP/TCP delivery tests with target A/AAAA records and zero synthesized additionals in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig). |
| DNS message, names, and core RDATA | [RFC 1034 §3.1–§4.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1034), [RFC 1035 §3.2, §4.1–§4.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1035), [RFC 3597 §3–§5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3597): header/question/section parsing, DNS names, known and unknown RR wire data. | `partial`: the server parses and builds DNS messages with the RFC 1035 section 4.1 header, the question, and the answer, authority, and additional sections, and it stores and serves DNS names bounded at 63 octets a label and 255 octet a name, compared without regard to case. The RDATA of the record types the server implements is typed: it is parsed from the wire and from zone text, encoded back, and rendered by the CLI. An RR of a type this server does not implement carries its RDATA whole: on the wire the RDATA is opaque and bounded by the 16-bit RDLENGTH of RFC 1035 section 3.2.1, which allows 65535 octets (`max_opaque_rdata_len` in [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig)), and in a zone file the same octets are written and read in the RFC 3597 section 5 generic form `\# <length> <hex>`, decoded once into the zone's own storage. A zone file holds no record that no message can carry: a record whose RDATA is longer than the 64988 octets one DNS message can carry (`max_message_rdata_len` in [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig)) is refused with the zone parser's `BadRecord`. The DNSSEC types the server serves opaque (DNSKEY, RRSIG, NSEC, NSEC3, DS) are read from a zone file in the RFC 3597 section 5 generic form `\# <length> <hex>`, whose length token is the RDATA length in octets, and in the project's shorter `\# <hex>` form, and they keep the 2048-octet `max_unknown_len` bound, as does the typed RDATA that shares their bound (APL, CAA, NAPTR, TXT); TXT, OPT, TSIG, and SVCB/HTTPS keep their own 512-octet caps. The gaps are the ones the first-release target cell names. | `partial`: known RDATA and opaque unknown RDATA are supported; an RR of a type this server does not implement carries its RDATA whole, up to the 65535 octets the 16-bit RDLENGTH of RFC 1035 section 3.2.1 allows (`max_opaque_rdata_len` in [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig)), and a record whose RDATA is longer than the 2048-octet inline buffer borrows those bytes from the message it was parsed from, the way a parsed name does; the DNSSEC types served opaque (DNSKEY, RRSIG, NSEC, NSEC3, DS) and the typed RDATA that shares their bound (APL, CAA, NAPTR, TXT) keep the 2048-octet `max_unknown_len` bound and larger values are rejected, while TXT, OPT, TSIG, and SVCB/HTTPS keep their own 512-octet caps; a zone file carries the message bound, not the RDLENGTH bound, so the zone is the stricter of the two paths: RFC 1035 section 4.2.2 caps a DNS message at 65535 octets, and the answer that carries a record also carries the 12-octet header, the echoed question (a 255-octet name, QTYPE, and QCLASS), the record's own fields (a 255-octet owner name, TYPE, CLASS, TTL, and the two-octet RDLENGTH), and the 11-octet EDNS OPT record, so the longest RDATA one message can carry is 64988 octets (`max_message_rdata_len` in [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig)); a zone file record longer than that is refused with the zone parser's `BadRecord` instead of being held and never served, and a record at the bound is served byte for byte through the authoritative path; a record of a type this server does not implement is written in the RFC 3597 section 5 generic form `\# <length> <hex>`, the zone parser decodes those octets once into the zone's own storage (the arena that backs the zone records, so they stay valid for the zone's lifetime and are charged against its 16 MB `max_zone_allocation_bytes` budget); the DNSSEC types the server serves opaque (DNSKEY, RRSIG, NSEC, NSEC3, DS) are read from a zone file in the same RFC 3597 section 5 generic form `\# <length> <hex>` and in the project's shorter `\# <hex>` form, and every record is served with the RDLENGTH the data it carries has; a length token that is not the length of the hexadecimal data that follows it is read as the shorter form, so a record that is neither form is refused rather than served with a length that does not match its data; the ZONEMD digest path in [`src/zone/zonemd.zig`](../src/zone/zonemd.zig) encodes a record's canonical RDATA into a buffer of that same bound, so a zone that carries both a record at the bound and an apex ZONEMD record computes its digest; this is not a full RFC 3597 claim: the wire path still accepts the 65535 octets the RDLENGTH field allows, because a peer may send a longer RDATA in a message that carries no question, and the additions an answer makes are not part of the record's bound (a UDP answer that does not fit the negotiated payload is truncated with TC, and the RRSIG a signed zone adds, or the TSIG a signed AXFR frame adds, can still push one answer past the message size). | [`src/dns/message.zig`](../src/dns/message.zig), [`src/dns/name.zig`](../src/dns/name.zig), [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig), [`src/dns/record.zig`](../src/dns/record.zig), [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig) | [`src/dns/message.zig` tests](../src/dns/message.zig), [`src/dns/name.zig` tests](../src/dns/name.zig), [`src/dns/rdata.zig` tests](../src/dns/rdata.zig) — including the 65535-octet unknown-RDATA round trip, the refusal of an RDATA longer than the 16-bit RDLENGTH, 520-byte DNSKEY and 570-byte RRSIG preservation, and oversize rejection — the zone-file generic-form tests in [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig) ("zone parser keeps a generic record whole", "zone parser accepts a generic record at the message bound and refuses one past it", "zone parser serves a record at the message bound", "zone parser refuses an unknown type that is not in the generic form", "zone parser loads DNSSEC records in the RFC 3597 length form", "zone parser still loads the short generic form of a DNSSEC record", "zone parser refuses a DNSSEC record whose generic length disagrees with the data"), and [`test/golden/golden_test.zig`](../test/golden/golden_test.zig) opaque round-trip and bounded-length coverage, and the digest of a zone that holds a record at the message bound in [`src/zone/zonemd.zig`](../src/zone/zonemd.zig) ("a zone with a record at the message bound computes its digest"). |
| IPv6 address data (AAAA) | [RFC 3596 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3596#section-2.1)–[§2.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3596#section-2.4): type 28 AAAA RDATA, the 128-bit network-byte-order data format, and the AAAA query. [§2.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3596#section-2.5) (IP6.ARPA) and [§3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3596#section-3) (A+AAAA additional synthesis) are deferred below. RFC 3596 is **Internet Standard STD 88**; it obsoletes [RFC 1886](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1886) and [RFC 3152](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3152), which are historical predecessors of the IP6.INT-to-IP6.ARPA change and are not implementation targets in this profile. | `supported`: the AAAA record is implemented, with byte-exact golden and loopback socket evidence. `RecordType.AAAA` carries wire code 28, `RData.AAAA` holds a fixed `[16]u8`, `RData.parse` rejects any RDLENGTH other than 16, and `RData.encode` writes exactly those 16 octets; the zone parser reads an IPv6 text form, the CLI renders the address, and AAAA is served through the generic authoritative path. Out of this row's scope by citation (deferred, not missing): no code synthesizes A or AAAA glue, and no IP6.ARPA content is generated or served. Evidence is a byte-exact golden fixture plus IPv4 loopback UDP/TCP tests asserting the 16 address octets. | `supported`: authoritative IN-only type 28 stores exactly 16 octets of an IPv6 address in network byte order and is served, encoded, and re-parsed like any other typed RDATA, with a byte-exact golden fixture and IPv4 loopback UDP/TCP tests asserting the address bytes. Deferred by citation (§2.5/§3 below): no glue is synthesized even when the target name has A and AAAA records in the zone, matching the typed AFSDB, X25, ISDN, RT, SRV, and RP rows above; the IP6.ARPA reverse-lookup domain, delegated separately by BCP 49, is not served or generated. | [`src/dns/types.zig`](../src/dns/types.zig), [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig), [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig), [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig), [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) | Type-code and exact-16-octet wire round-trip tests in [`src/dns/types.zig`](../src/dns/types.zig) and [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig) ("rdata parses and encodes A and AAAA records"); IPv6 zone-text and malformed-input tests in [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig) (`bad-v6 IN AAAA 2001:db8::g`); deterministic CLI rendering in [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig). Wire evidence: byte-exact [`aaaa-response.gdns-fixture`](../test/golden/aaaa-response.gdns-fixture) plus IPv4 loopback UDP/TCP tests asserting the 16 address octets (`expectAaaaResponse` in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig)). [`srv-response.gdns-fixture`](../test/golden/srv-response.gdns-fixture) and the SRV integration zone carry `target IN AAAA 2001:db8::21` to prove that **no** A or AAAA additional record is emitted. |
| SSH key algorithms as SSHFP RR content (RFC 8709) | [RFC 8709 §8](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8709#section-8): the sole DNS content of an SSH key-exchange document — how an Ed448 public key is placed into an SSHFP RR and the code point added to the "SSHFP RR Types for public key algorithms" IANA registry. RFC 8709 is a Standards Track Proposed Standard that updates [RFC 4253](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4253); its scope is the SSH transport, not DNS server behavior. | `not-applicable`: RFC 8709 is "Ed25519 and Ed448 Public Key Algorithms for the Secure Shell (SSH) Protocol" and defines no DNS server or resolver behavior. Its one DNS obligation — an SSHFP RR must carry the SSH public key digest — is already discharged by the Typed SSHFP RDATA row below. Being a Proposed Standard rather than an Experimental or Informational memorandum does not make it a target for this server. | `not-applicable`: no target. Implementing RFC 8709 here would mean SSH key generation, negotiation, or verification, none of which is DNS-server work. | [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig) | Algorithm-6 (Ed448) acceptance is asserted by the SSHFP bound and malformed-length tests in [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig) and by the exact RFC 7479 response bytes in [`sshfp-response.gdns-fixture`](../test/golden/sshfp-response.gdns-fixture). No code implements `ssh-ed25519` or `ssh-ed448` key handling, because none belongs in a DNS server. |
| Typed SSHFP RDATA | [RFC 4255 §3.1–§3.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4255), [RFC 6594 §3.1.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6594#section-3.1.1), [RFC 6594 §3.2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6594#section-3.2.1), [RFC 7479 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7479) and [§4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7479#section-4), [RFC 8709 §8](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8709#section-8): type 44 RDATA, its decimal algorithm/fingerprint-type fields with a hexadecimal fingerprint, and the algorithm code points 4 and 6. | `supported`: typed wire parsing/encoding, strict zone-text parsing, deterministic CLI rendering, and generic authoritative delivery cover the bounded IN profile: algorithm code points 1, 2, 3, 4, and 6 with fingerprint type 1 (20 octets) or 2 (32 octets), each length enforced exactly. This is not a full SSHFP security or client-verification claim. | `supported`: authoritative IN-only type 44 data accepts algorithm code points 1 (RSA, RFC 4255 §3.1.1), 2 (DSS, RFC 4255 §3.1.1), 3 (ECDSA, RFC 6594 §3.2.1), 4 (Ed25519, RFC 7479 §4), and 6 (Ed448, RFC 8709 §8), with fingerprint type 1 (SHA-1, 20 octets) or 2 (SHA-256, 32 octets) and no other code point; the record is served through the generic authoritative UDP and TCP paths for the IN class the zone loader accepts, and the CLI renders `SSHFP <algorithm> <fingerprint type> <lowercase hexadecimal fingerprint>`. Algorithms 4 and 6 are typed SSHFP data only: the SSH key algorithm behavior and the client-side verification of RFC 8709, DNSSEC signing/validation, and SSH-client verification are not implemented. Non-IN classes are excluded. | [`src/dns/types.zig`](../src/dns/types.zig), [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig), [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig), [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig), [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig), [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig) | Type code 44 in [`src/dns/types.zig`](../src/dns/types.zig) ("SSHFP record type uses wire code 44"); SSHFP wire parse/encode round trip and the refusal of algorithms 0, 5, and 7, fingerprint types 0 and 3, and every wrong fingerprint length in [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig) ("SSHFP wire parses and encodes exact selector and fingerprint bytes", "SSHFP wire rejects truncation, unsupported selectors, and wrong lengths"); the acceptance of algorithms 1, 2, 3, 4, and 6 with both fingerprint types on the wire and in zone text, and the refusal of another class, in [`test/unit/sshfp_typed_rdata_test.zig`](../test/unit/sshfp_typed_rdata_test.zig) ("SSHFP wire accepts every algorithm code point the profile supports", "SSHFP zone text accepts the same algorithm code points", "an SSHFP record in another class is refused by the zone loader"); zone-text acceptance, malformed hex, field count, and non-decimal selector refusal in [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig); deterministic `SSHFP <algorithm> <fingerprint type> <hex>` rendering in [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig) ("query formatter prints SSHFP selectors and lowercase fingerprint"); exact type-44 authoritative wire and RDATA in [`test/golden/golden_test.zig`](../test/golden/golden_test.zig) using the RFC 7479 §2 example bytes of [`sshfp-response.gdns-fixture`](../test/golden/sshfp-response.gdns-fixture); and IPv4 loopback UDP/TCP delivery in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig) ("UDP request pipeline answers SSHFP query", "TCP request pipeline answers SSHFP query"). |
| Typed TLSA RDATA | [RFC 6698 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6698#section-2.1), [§2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6698#section-2.2), and [§7.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6698#section-7.1): type 52 TLSA RDATA fields and presentation form; [RFC 7218 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7218#section-2.1)–[§2.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7218#section-2.3) and [§3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7218#section-3.1): the field acronyms the CLI displays in place of the three integers. RFC 7218 is a Proposed Standard that updates RFC 6698 and changes no DANE protocol behavior; [RFC 7671](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7671) is an operational/client guidance boundary. | `supported`: typed record 52 authoritative data is bounded to usages 0–3, selectors 0–1, matching type 0 with nonempty association data of 1–509 bytes, and matching types 1 and 2 with exact 32- and 64-byte associations. The CLI renders the RFC 7218 §2.1–§2.3 acronyms — `PKIX-TA`/`PKIX-EE`/`DANE-TA`/`DANE-EE`, `Cert`/`SPKI`, `Full`/`SHA2-256`/`SHA2-512` — in place of the three numbers. The wire and zone-text paths are still numeric: `validTlsa` refuses usage 255, selector 255, matching type 255, and every unassigned code point, so the RFC 7218 private-use acronyms `PrivCert`, `PrivSel`, and `PrivMatch` can never reach the renderer. | `supported`: the same typed authoritative IN-only subset is served, accepts whitespace-separated strict hexadecimal zone input, and renders the RFC 7218 field acronyms plus lowercase hexadecimal association data in the CLI; exact golden response and IPv4 UDP/TCP evidence are recorded. Because the acronym display replaces the RFC 6698 §2.1 numeric presentation, `query` output is display-only and is not accepted back as zone text — `parseTlsa` still reads only the three integers. Accepting RFC 7218 acronyms as zone input (RFC 7218 §1 says software "can adopt" parsing them), the private-use 255 codes, DNSSEC signing/validation, TLS/DTLS, certificate/DER/PKIX parsing or matching, DANE client behavior, resolver policy, data beyond 509 bytes, and non-IN classes are excluded. | [`src/dns/types.zig`](../src/dns/types.zig), [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig), [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig), [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig), [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig) | Type code 52 in [`src/dns/types.zig`](../src/dns/types.zig) ("TLSA record type uses wire code 52"); typed wire parse/encode round trip, the 509-octet bound, and the refusal of usages above 3, selectors above 1, matching types above 2, and every wrong association length in [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig) ("TLSA wire parses and encodes exact selector and association bytes", "TLSA wire rejects unsupported selectors and association lengths"); strict zone-text acceptance of split hexadecimal and the exact 32- and 64-byte digests, and the refusal of malformed selectors, chunks, and lengths in [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig); the RFC 7218 acronyms of all four usages, both selectors, and all three matching types in [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig) ("query formatter prints RFC 7218 TLSA acronyms and lowercase association") and [`test/unit/tlsa_typed_rdata_test.zig`](../test/unit/tlsa_typed_rdata_test.zig) ("the CLI renders the RFC 7218 acronyms of the registry values the profile accepts"); the display-only boundary, the refusal of the private-use code point 255 on the wire and in zone text, and the refusal of another class in [`test/unit/tlsa_typed_rdata_test.zig`](../test/unit/tlsa_typed_rdata_test.zig) ("the RFC 7218 acronyms are display-only and are not accepted back as zone text", "the private-use TLSA code points are refused before the renderer sees them", "a TLSA record in another class is refused by the zone loader"); exact type-52 authoritative wire and RDATA in [`test/golden/golden_test.zig`](../test/golden/golden_test.zig) using [`tlsa-response.gdns-fixture`](../test/golden/tlsa-response.gdns-fixture); and IPv4 loopback UDP/TCP delivery in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig) ("UDP request pipeline answers TLSA query", "TCP request pipeline answers TLSA query"). |
| Typed URI RDATA | [RFC 7553 §4.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7553#section-4.1)–[§4.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7553#section-4.5), and [§6.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7553#section-6.1): type 256, presentation priority/weight/target, and the two-octet network-order fields plus raw-octet Target wire format; [RFC 1035 §5.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1035#section-5.1): master-file quoting and `\X`/`\DDD` escape forms. | `supported`: typed URI wire data, strict record-local zone decoding, deterministic CLI rendering, and authoritative delivery cover only the bounded subset; this is not full URI processing or RFC conformance. | `supported`: authoritative IN-only type 256 data with priority and weight 0–65535, a nonempty raw target of exactly 1–508 bytes under the project’s 512-octet RDATA bound, and one strict quoted target using `\X` for one non-digit byte or `\DDD` for exactly three decimal digits in the range 000–255. Target bytes are preserved on wire and escaped deterministically in CLI output; exact golden response and IPv4 UDP/TCP evidence are recorded. | RFC 1982 serial arithmetic and IXFR delta decisions | [RFC 1982 §3–§4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1982#section-3): modulo-2^32 DNS SOA serial arithmetic, including newer/older ordering and the undefined half-range; [RFC 1995 §3–§4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1995#section-3): the IXFR query carries the SOA of the client's version in its authority section, and an incremental response begins with the server's current SOA followed by the SOA of the client's version that is about to be replaced. [RFC 1995 §5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1995#section-5) sets no floor on how long an IXFR server must keep the older versions: it can not be required to hold all previous versions forever and may delete them anytime, so a server that stops recording transitions stays conforming as long as it answers such a client with the full transfer. The same section names the test that decides what the server keeps: "Information about older versions should be purged if the total length of an IXFR response would be longer than that of an AXFR response", and it says the strategy "assures that the amount of storage required is at most twice that of the current zone information". [RFC 1995 §4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1995#section-4) defines the incremental response: the list of difference sequences is preceded and followed by a copy of the server's current SOA, and each difference sequence is one update consisting of deleted RRs and added RRs, the first RR of the deleted RRs being the older SOA and the first RR of the added RRs the newer SOA, the sequences ordered oldest first. Durable on-disk storage of the delta log is local storage behavior, not an RFC wire-protocol claim. |`partial`: `compareSerial` classifies newer/equal/older/undefined serials, and `transfer.decideIxfr` shapes an IXFR answer from the zone's bounded `DeltaLog`. A running server records a serial transition: `zimdns server run` remembers the zone files it was started from, installs a SIGHUP handler that only sets a reload flag, and the serve path re-reads every one of them, parsing and building each new zone completely before swapping it in and recording the old-serial-to-new-serial transition in that zone's `DeltaLog`. The incremental answer is now the RFC 1995 section 4 difference sequence, not a serial pair: the reload computes the difference between the two zone snapshots, where both exist, and the difference is the records the old zone held and the new one does not, then the records the new zone holds and the old one did not, one record per RR, with the older SOA opening the deleted records and the newer SOA opening the added ones. A record of the difference carries its class: a record of a class other than the default IN class writes it as a `C<class>` marker in the class place of a zone file line, and the record keeps that class when the difference is turned back into DNS records. The answer is the current SOA, that difference sequence, and the current SOA again, so a client that applies the sequence reaches the new zone. The delta log is durable when the operator asks for it: the `ixfr-journal-file <path>` directive names a file in the configuration directory, every recorded transition is appended to it as one line (the zone apex, the two serials, and the difference in hex, and nothing else), and the file is read at startup and replayed into the per-zone logs, so an IXFR client at the pre-restart serial is answered with the incremental sequence exactly as it was before the restart. A file written in the old format (the apex and the two serials, no difference) is read without failing, so the server starts; its transitions cannot be turned into a difference, so they are not served incrementally and the client at that serial gets the full AXFR per RFC 1995 section 4. A record that does not parse, such as a truncated or corrupt line, is skipped and the server still starts, and a zone whose history the log cannot supply is answered with the full AXFR per RFC 1995 section 4. A difference that cannot be turned into records is dropped on its own: the line is kept and its transition is restored, but the difference is not, so the client at that serial gets the full AXFR per RFC 1995 section 4 and never an incremental answer built from records that are not records. The journal file is bounded: it is not written past 1048576 bytes (`max_journal_bytes` in [`src/zone/journal.zig`](../src/zone/journal.zig)), the same published constant the reader uses, so a long-lived server cannot fill the disk. A transition that does not fit is not recorded: the drop is counted on the journal, the server reports it to the operator on stderr as `error: the IXFR journal file is at its 1048576-byte bound; the transition for <zone> from serial <a> to <b> was not recorded`, and the server keeps serving. The in-memory log still carries the transition, so a running server answers that client incrementally until the in-memory log's own eight-transition bound evicts it; after a restart the dropped transition is not in the file, so a client at that serial is answered with the full AXFR per RFC 1995 section 4, exactly as an unknown serial is. The bound of the durable file is the project's own size limit: the file keeps the oldest transitions and refuses the newest, so a journal that reached its bound no longer carries the recent history. Without the directive the delta log stays in memory and no file is written, so an operator who did not ask for a durable log sees no change. The in-memory store now measures both sides of the section 5 test in the wire bytes of the records they carry: every difference is measured when it is stored, and the full transfer of a zone is measured by `zone_index.fullTransferBytes` in [`src/zone/index.zig`](../src/zone/index.zig), so the decision compares the two with the byte count the wire uses and not with an estimate. Both answers carry the two current SOA records, so only the other records are compared. An incremental answer that is not shorter than the full transfer is not served: the client at that serial gets the full AXFR response of RFC 1995 section 4, and the difference of the oldest version on the way is purged from the in-memory store, so the storage stays bounded the way section 5 describes. The comparison needs the size of the zone's full transfer, which the zone index hands to the per-zone log: `Zone.fromZoneFile` in [`src/zone/index.zig`](../src/zone/index.zig) measures the full transfer of the zone it loads and registers the size by apex, the signer registers it again once the zone carries the records it generated, and `DeltaLog.canServeIncremental` in [`src/zone/journal.zig`](../src/zone/journal.zig) reads the registered size, so the rule applies to a live answer. The size of a difference is measured without the two SOA records the difference carries, because both answers carry the two current SOA records and only the other records are compared. A difference that does not open with the older SOA and the newer SOA of its transition is not the transition it claims to be, so it is dropped the way a difference that cannot be turned into records is dropped: the transition stays and the client at that serial gets the full AXFR per RFC 1995 section 4. The durable file's own bound is unchanged.|`partial`: the named subset is implemented and tested — the serial arithmetic, the delta-log decision function, and the operator-reachable zone replacement that fills the log: a SIGHUP replaces the served zone from its zone file, the serial transition and the difference between the two zones are recorded, and an IXFR client at the pre-reload serial is answered with the RFC 1995 section 4 difference sequence instead of with a full AXFR, while a client at an unknown serial still gets the full transfer. The durability the deferred transfer row carried is now operator-reachable: the `ixfr-journal-file` directive names the file, the transition and its difference are appended to it, the file is replayed at startup, and a corrupt record is refused without taking the server down. A journal file in the old format is read without failing, and the transitions it cannot turn into a difference fall back to the full transfer. The journal file has a growth policy now: it stops at 1048576 bytes, a transition that does not fit is dropped and reported, and the dropped transition falls back to the full transfer after a restart. The section 5 test of the incremental answer being longer than the full transfer is now the decision the log makes: the difference and the full transfer are measured in wire bytes, an incremental answer that is not shorter is refused in favour of the full transfer, and the difference of the oldest version on the way is purged from the in-memory store. What stays open is the storage bound the section names: the per-zone log keeps the newest 32 differences, each bounded at 1024 bytes (`max_delta_bytes` and `max_stored_deltas` in [`src/zone/journal.zig`](../src/zone/journal.zig)), so a change whose difference does not fit is not recorded and its client gets the full transfer, which is safe but coarse. The wiring that makes the running server measure is in place: the zone index registers the size of the zone's full transfer when it loads the zone, the per-zone log reads that size, and a live answer applies the section 5 test. TSIG-authenticated UPDATE and TSIG-signed transfers are implemented and their rows above carry the evidence; what a secondary still lacks is the refresh side, because this server pulls a zone only at startup.|[`src/zone/journal.zig`](../src/zone/journal.zig), [`src/zone/transfer.zig`](../src/zone/transfer.zig), [`src/zone/index.zig`](../src/zone/index.zig), [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig), [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig), [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig), [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig), and [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig)|RFC 1982 equal/ordering/wrap/half-range serial tests and the bounded `DeltaLog` transition tests in [`src/zone/journal.zig`](../src/zone/journal.zig), together with the journal round trip across a reopened file, the skip of a corrupt or truncated record, the drop of a difference that cannot be turned into records, the class a difference record carries, the growth bound that stops the file and drops the transitions past it, the full-transfer fallback a dropped transition gets after a restart, the old-format file that is read but not served incrementally, and the restarted server that answers the old serial incrementally again, plus the journal tests "a journal difference that cannot be turned into records is dropped", "a difference that does not open with the two SOA records is dropped", "the difference carries the class of the record", and "a difference that is not shorter than the full transfer is purged"; `decideIxfr` incremental/fallback and client-serial extraction tests in [`src/zone/transfer.zig`](../src/zone/transfer.zig), including the difference-sequence answer, the difference applied to the old zone yielding the new zone's records, the full transfer an unknown serial still gets, "a difference longer than the full transfer is answered with the full transfer", "a difference shorter than the full transfer is answered incrementally", "the size comparison leaves the difference's own SOA records out", and "the zone's full transfer size reaches the delta log"; zone-replacement, refused-replacement, and serial-transition tests in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig); the `ixfr-journal-file` directive rejections in [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig); the restart, corrupt-journal, and no-directive tests in [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig); the SIGHUP handler test in [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig); the multi-source reload test "a reload re-reads every configured zone file" in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig), and the loopback reload and IXFR tests in [`test/integration/zone_reload_ixfr_test.zig`](../test/integration/zone_reload_ixfr_test.zig), where a reloaded zone file serves the new data over UDP, a broken zone file keeps the old zone serving, an IXFR from the pre-reload serial returns the incremental sequence, and an IXFR from an unknown serial returns the full transfer.
| EDNS(0) | [RFC 6891 §4–§6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6891): one valid OPT record, negotiated payload, extended RCODE/DO fields, bounded options, and deterministic malformed-input behavior; [RFC 7830 §3–§4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7830#section-3) and [§6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7830#section-6): Padding option and plaintext-transport privacy boundary; [RFC 7871 §6–§7](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7871#section-6): ECS wire format, recursive/authoritative handling, and the option an intermediate nameserver returns to a client that sent one. | `partial`: exactly one root-owned OPT; 512–1232 payload negotiation and UDP TC; DO/extended RCODE fields; BADVERS for nonzero versions; unknown-option ignore; bounded option parsing; and a query whose OPT record is malformed — more than one OPT, non-root owner, or malformed option framing — is answered FORMERR rather than dropped (RFC 6891 §6.1.1); and backwards-only response-name compression with uncompressed fallback. The scoped RFC 7830/RFC 7871 profile accepts at most one arbitrary-byte Padding option and emits one on the encrypted transports when the query asked for it, as RFC 7830 section 4 makes mandatory, while the cleartext UDP and TCP paths never emit it, as section 6 requires of an unencrypted transport; the padding octets are 0x00, the answer is rounded up to a multiple of 128 octets, and the padded answer never exceeds the payload size the requestor advertised. One deviation from RFC 7830 section 4 is stated rather than hidden: a requestor whose advertised payload size leaves no room for the option framing gets an answer with no Padding option, because the section 4 MUST cannot be met inside the size the requestor asked for; validates ECS only for address families 1/2, bounded source/scope prefixes, canonical truncated address bytes, and query scope zero; valid ECS is ignored without echo for authoritative answers, the client's ECS option is forwarded upstream with the SOURCE PREFIX-LENGTH the client asked for — shortened to the operator's `client-subnet-max-prefix` when the client asked for a longer prefix, and the address truncated to that many bits — and the query scope of 0 (RFC 7871 §7.1.1), the other outbound EDNS option payloads are stripped before forwarding, an answer is cached under the network the upstream said it covers, and a later client is served the entry whose scope covers its network with the longest prefix (RFC 7871 §7.3.1 and §7.3.2); a scope-0 answer serves every client, and a client that disclosed fewer bits than an entry’s scope is asked upstream; an answer to a client that carried the option carries it back in the OPT record, with the SCOPE PREFIX-LENGTH the upstream answered with — read out of the upstream's answer before the options are stripped, and 0 when the upstream sent no option (RFC 7871 §6 and §7.3) — and the ADDRESS the resolver sent upstream, so the client learns the scope of the answer it received (RFC 7871 §7.2.2); the address sent upstream is truncated to at most 24 bits of IPv4 and 56 bits of IPv6, the concealment lengths §11.1 recommends, while a client that disclosed fewer bits keeps its own prefix; a client that sent no option is never given one; an upstream answer that echoes the option must match the query's family, prefix, and address bits (RFC 7871 §7.3); and upstream responses containing unsolicited ECS are rejected before caching. | `partial` for that exact profile; no full RFC 6891/7830/7871 claim. Encrypted-transport Padding and complete option interoperability/fallback behavior remain excluded. The §7.1.3 retry without ADDRESS on a REFUSED is implemented: an upstream that refuses a query that carried a non-zero ADDRESS is asked once more with the same query and no ADDRESS, so it can answer the question without the scope, and a second REFUSED ends the exchange the way it ends without a client subnet. The scope an answer reports is the scope the upstream answered with (RFC 7871 §6 and §7.3): an upstream answer with no client subnet option has the scope of 0, and a scope the resolver cannot use — one wider than the prefix the query asked for — is reported as 0, so a client is never told the answer covers a scope the resolver did not learn from the upstream, and the entry stays confined to the network the query asked from instead of widening to every client. The cached copy keeps the scope the upstream answered with, so a later client learns the same scope. The client's source prefix is trimmed to the operator's `client-subnet-max-prefix` before the query goes upstream, and the address is truncated to the trimmed prefix; the answer is cached under the scope the upstream reported, cut from the trimmed network, so a covered client is served it and an uncovered one is asked upstream (RFC 7871 §7.1.1, §7.3.1 and §7.3.2). RFC 9018 Cookies and RFC 8914 EDE remain isolated from ECS/cache-key behavior. | [`src/dns/message.zig`](../src/dns/message.zig), [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig), [`src/dns/writer.zig`](../src/dns/writer.zig), [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig), [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig), and [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) | ECS query-scope and FORMERR tests in [`src/dns/message.zig`](../src/dns/message.zig) and [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig); Padding/ECS duplicate, family, prefix, address-length, and canonical-bit tests in [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig); authoritative no-echo coverage in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig); client-subnet forwarding, scope-separated cache key, upstream-ECS matching, outbound-option sanitization, and unsolicited-upstream-ECS-before-cache tests in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig); the trimming by "RFC 7871 section 7.1.1: the client subnet prefix is trimmed to the maximum cacheable prefix length" and "RFC 7871 section 7.3.1: a trimmed scope keys the cache, so another scope is not served from it" in the same file, and the retry by "RFC 7871 section 7.1.3: a refused query with a client subnet is asked again without the address" and "RFC 7871 section 7.1.3: a second refusal ends the exchange" there, and the directive by "config parser takes one maximum cacheable prefix length and refuses malformed ones" in [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) and "server context carries the denied source ports and the cacheable prefix into the forwarding policy" in [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig); the option the answer carries is covered there by "RFC 7871 section 7.2.2: the answer reports the scope the upstream answered with" and "RFC 7871 section 7.2.2: a client that sent no client subnet option gets none in the answer", which drive the real upstream path and assert the scope the upstream saw, by "RFC 7871 section 7.3: an upstream answer with no client subnet option has the scope of 0", and by "a cached answer reports the scope the upstream answered with"; the cache scope rules by "RFC 7871 section 7.3.1: a scope-0 answer is served to every client", "RFC 7871 section 7.3.2: the longest covering scope answers", "RFC 7871 section 7.3.2: a client that disclosed fewer bits than the scope is asked upstream", "RFC 7871 section 7.3: an unusable upstream scope is reported as 0 and confined to the query scope", and "RFC 7871 section 11.1: the upstream scope is masked to the recommended lengths", all in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig); and exact EDNS response/BADVERS/TC assertions in [`test/golden/golden_test.zig`](../test/golden/golden_test.zig) using [`test/golden/edns-response.gdns-fixture`](../test/golden/edns-response.gdns-fixture). |
| DNSSEC OK (DO) bit origin and gating | [RFC 3225 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3225#section-3): the DO bit as the most significant bit of the Z field in the EDNS(0) OPT header, the requirement that security RRs not be returned when DO is clear unless explicitly queried, that the query's DO bit be copied into the response, and that a recursive DNSSEC-aware server set DO on its own recursive requests; plus its Security Considerations rule that absent DNSSEC data in a DO=1 answer must not be read as "no security information". RFC 3225 is a Proposed Standard; the RFC Editor index lists no RFC that obsoletes it, and lists RFC 4033, RFC 4034, and RFC 4035 as updating it. | `supported`: the bit is parsed from the OPT TTL most significant bit into `EdnsState.dnssec_ok`, copied from the query into every authoritative and forwarded response OPT, and gates proof RRs on the authoritative path — covering RRSIGs and NSEC/NSEC3 denial proofs ride along only when the query sets DO=1 and are stripped otherwise, with AD set only on validated-secure forwards (never on authoritative answers). Both RFC 3225 section 3 forwarder obligations are implemented: the forwarder sets DO on every upstream request — an EDNS client query is re-composed with DO=1, and a client query without EDNS keeps its own octets and gains one appended OPT record with DO=1 — and an answer to a DO=0 client has RRSIG/NSEC/NSEC3 security RRs stripped on the way out, while DNSKEY/DS answers and explicit proof-type queries are kept and cached data is left intact. The reserved-bit policy is not deferred: the header-bit-semantics row below records the reserved Z bit as parsed and never encoded, and counted by `zimdns_reserved_bit_queries_total` and `zimdns_ad_bit_queries_total`, so nothing in the Z field is left open here. | `supported`: the authoritative copy-and-gate subset plus both forwarder obligations — DO on every upstream request and DO=0 stripping on forwarded answers — with the reserved-bit policy in place, as the header-bit-semantics row below records. | [`src/dns/message.zig`](../src/dns/message.zig), [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig), [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) | DO parse and round-trip tests in [`src/dns/message.zig`](../src/dns/message.zig) ("message preserves EDNS extended RCODE, DO bit, and option bytes"); DO copy-and-gate tests in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) ("DO-bit query receives covering RRSIGs, plain query does not", "explicit DNSKEY query is answered with or without DO", "NODATA with DO carries an NSEC denial proof, without DO only SOA"); exact DO-bit wire golden assertions in [`test/golden/golden_test.zig`](../test/golden/golden_test.zig) using [`dnssec-response.gdns-fixture`](../test/golden/dnssec-response.gdns-fixture); and DO relay and cache-isolation tests in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig). The upstream DO bit and the DO=0 stripping are proved on the wire in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig): "forwarder asks upstream with DO=1 for a client query without EDNS" captures the upstream request and reads its DO bit, and "a forwarded answer reaches a DO=0 client without the security RRs" shows the RRSIG in the upstream answer reaching a DO=1 client and not a DO=0 one. |
| TSIG request authentication and response signing | [RFC 8945 §§3–8](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8945#section-3), with the [RFC Editor errata check](https://www.rfc-editor.org/errata_search.php?rfc=8945) recorded on 2026-08-21: TSIG wire format, HMAC authentication, time checks, error responses, and UDP truncation, including the §4.2 key name and MAC Size field, the §5.2.1 key check, the §5.2.2.1 MAC truncation rules, and the §7 truncation policy, with [RFC 4868 §2.6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4868#section-2.6) for the output and truncation lengths of the three truncated algorithm names, and [RFC 2104 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2104#section-3) (HMAC keys are chosen at random and periodically refreshed) for the key replacement the reload provides. | `partial`: raw final-TSIG parsing, HMAC-SHA1/SHA224/SHA256/SHA384/SHA512 verification, strict key-file loading (the five RFC 8945 §6 HMAC-SHA names and the three truncated names of RFC 4868 §2.6, each with a secret of at least the keyed hash output of the hash it uses), ordinary authoritative-query authentication, and response signing are implemented, the algorithm name is matched case-insensitively as RFC 8945 §9 requires and the reply carries the spelling the peer sent, the three truncated names of the RFC 8945 section 6 table, hmac-sha256-128, hmac-sha384-192, and hmac-sha512-256, are matched on the wire: a key file may name one, a request signed with it is verified, and the reply is signed with the same algorithm and key, their MAC is the RFC 4868 truncation of the keyed hash to 16, 24, and 32 octets, or a longer one when the request carried a longer MAC (§7), the MAC length is checked against the keyed hash output of the hash function in use, the output length the truncated names keep (RFC 8945 section 5.2.2.1 case 1, RFC 4868 section 2.6), and the secret floor of a truncated name is the keyed hash output of the algorithm it truncates (RFC 4868 section 2.6), and the §4.2 MAC Size field is now honored: a request MAC truncated to the local policy floor or above authenticates against the truncated prefix of the computed digest, a request MAC longer than the keyed hash output of the hash function in use is rejected FORMERR (§5.2.2.1 case 1), a request MAC shorter than the larger of 10 octets and half the hash length is rejected FORMERR (§5.2.2.1 case 3), and the section 5.2 check order is honored: the key, the MAC, the time values, and the truncation policy are checked in that order, so a request that is both out of the fudge window and shorter than the local MAC floor is answered BADTIME and not BADTRUNC, while a request in time and short of that floor is still answered BADTRUNC; and a request MAC between the floor and the keyed hash output authenticates against the truncated prefix of the computed digest (§5.2.2.1 case 4), also under a truncated algorithm name, a request MAC below the policy floor is answered with a signed BADTRUNC (§5.2.4, §5.3.2), a zero-octet MAC never authenticates, and the reply MAC is at least as long as the request MAC (§7). The verification returns the MAC as it appears in the message, the digest cut to the MAC Size the peer sent, because RFC 8945 section 4.3.1 digests the request MAC "exactly as transmitted" and section 5.3.1 makes the prior MAC of a chained message the MAC of the previous message as it appears there; the signing side returns the same value, so a server that chains on the verified MAC computes the chain the peer computes, and a truncated MAC no longer produces a different prior MAC than the peer's. The written local policy floor is 12 octets for hmac-sha1 (§6 SHOULD implement SHA-1 truncated to 96 bits), 16 octets for hmac-sha256 (the §5.2.2.1 case 3 floor, SHA-256-128), 14 for hmac-sha224, 24 for hmac-sha384, and 32 for hmac-sha512 (the case 3 floor of half the keyed hash output, which is also the SHA-384-192 and SHA-512-256 truncation of RFC 4868); the three truncated names take the floor of the algorithm they truncate, which is their own MAC length of 16, 24, and 32 octets, and §7 is honored by accepting every longer MAC up to the full keyed hash output. One key name may now carry several algorithms: the key file accepts the same name with another algorithm and still refuses the same name with the same algorithm, a request is matched against every key that carries its name, and the algorithm the request names selects the one that authenticates it (§5.2.1), and the reply is signed with that same key and algorithm (§5.3). The bundled form is an operator choice that departs from the [§10](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8945#section-10) SHOULD that one algorithm be associated with a given key name; a name is never authenticated with an algorithm its key does not carry. The TSIG key file is re-read on request: a SIGHUP re-reads it as well as the zone file, so a key that was added is usable, a key whose secret changed is used with its new secret, and a key that was removed no longer authenticates, all without a restart. The new keyring is built completely before it replaces the served one, and the swap happens under the same lock the request path holds, so a request is verified against the old keyring or the new one, never a half-built one. A key file that is missing, unreadable, or malformed at reload time is reported on stderr like any other reload failure and leaves the served keys in place, so the server keeps answering with the keys it has; the startup parse is unchanged and a malformed key file is still fatal at startup. The secrets of the replaced keyring are wiped when it is dropped, as they are at startup. Still open: automatic key rollover (RFC 2104 §3 asks for keys to be refreshed periodically; the operator refreshes one by replacing the key file and reloading, and the server neither retires nor schedules a key on its own), the §6 entries the project does not implement (HMAC-MD5.SIG-ALG.REG.INT, whose use is a MUST NOT, and gss-tsig, whose cryptography is delegated to GSS), and a TSIG-signed transfer over the unreachable strict-TLS channel. The Do53 UPDATE, AXFR, and IXFR paths are not open: an authenticated update signs its reply and a signed transfer signs its first frame and chains every later frame (rows 102 and 87). A TSIG-signed NOTIFY is answered with a TSIG-signed response of the same shape (RFC 8945 §5.3), and an unsigned NOTIFY is answered with no TSIG record at all. | `partial`: the supported profile authenticates and signs ordinary authoritative UDP/TCP queries on Linux and macOS, preserves RFC 8945 Original ID and BADTIME fields, emits a question plus valid final TSIG on signed UDP overflow, and accepts a request whose MAC is truncated to the written local floor (12 octets for hmac-sha1, 16 for hmac-sha256) while answering a shorter one with a signed BADTRUNC. Every reply carries a MAC at least as long as the request's (§7), and the full keyed hash output is used whenever the request is untruncated. A key name may be bundled with several algorithms; the algorithm the request names selects the key that authenticates it, and the reply carries that key and algorithm. The [Zig-standard-library G4 decision](../src/dns/README.md) owns crypto maintenance and secret clearing. A NOTIFY that carries a TSIG is answered with a TSIG-signed response and an unsigned NOTIFY with no TSIG record (RFC 8945 §5.3); Dynamic UPDATE, a TSIG-signed AXFR/IXFR over the still-unreachable strict-TLS channel, the refresh-driven secondary role, automatic key rollover, the §6 algorithms beyond the HMAC-SHA family (HMAC-MD5.SIG-ALG.REG.INT and gss-tsig), and two-server interop remain explicitly deferred. The RFC 8945 section 5.2.3 MUST is implemented — a request whose Time Signed value lies outside the fudge window is answered NOTAUTH with BADTIME — while the section 5.2.3 SHOULD beside it, caching the most recent Time Signed value per key and answering BADTIME when a later message carries an earlier one, is deliberately not implemented: parallel signed queries can be reordered and a client that reads BADTIME as an unresolved name does not retry, so refusing an earlier Time Signed value would fail honest traffic, and what the omission costs is a replay inside the fudge window, which the fudge window already bounds. | [`src/dns/tsig.zig`](../src/dns/tsig.zig), [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig), [`src/auth/keyring.zig`](../src/auth/keyring.zig), [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig), [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig), [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig), [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig), [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig), and the TSIG crypto decision (`src/dns/README.md`) | Independent HMAC vectors and chaining checks in the TSIG vectors (`src/dns/README.md`) and [`src/dns/tsig.zig`](../src/dns/tsig.zig); wire/key/runtime unit tests in [`src/dns/tsig.zig`](../src/dns/tsig.zig), [`src/auth/keyring.zig`](../src/auth/keyring.zig), and [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig); truncation coverage in [`src/dns/tsig.zig`](../src/dns/tsig.zig) ("tsig SHA-1 truncation follows the local policy floor", which rebuilds the known-answer request with 12-, 11-, 21-, and 0-octet MAC fields) and in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) ("runtime accepts a truncated MAC and answers with a MAC at least as long", "runtime answers a MAC below the policy floor with a signed BADTRUNC", "runtime rejects a MAC longer than the keyed hash output", and "runtime rejects a zero-length MAC"); byte-exact signed response coverage in [`test/golden/golden_test.zig`](../test/golden/golden_test.zig); signed UDP loopback success, error, and classic/EDNS overflow coverage in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig) and [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig); and Linux-container plus macOS verification. The RFC 8945 §6 algorithm names, their keyed hash output lengths, and a per-algorithm known-answer MAC are asserted in [`src/dns/tsig.zig`](../src/dns/tsig.zig), together with the case-insensitive name match and the peer's spelling in the reply ("tsig matches the algorithm name case-insensitively and answers with the peer's spelling"), the truncated variants and their MAC lengths ("tsig verifies a request signed with a truncated algorithm name"), the MAC length rule of a truncated name ("tsig verifies a truncated name whose MAC is longer than the algorithm's own length" and "tsig refuses a MAC longer than the keyed hash output of a truncated name"), and the separation of the truncated names from the full family ("tsig keeps the truncated algorithm names apart from the full family"); the key-file algorithm names, the per-algorithm secret floor, and a request signed with a truncated name verified under the keyring key are asserted in [`src/auth/keyring.zig`](../src/auth/keyring.zig) ("a request signed with a truncated algorithm name is verified with the keyring key"), and the runtime path that verifies the request and signs the reply with the same key and algorithm has no per-algorithm branch; the bundled key name is asserted in [`src/auth/keyring.zig`](../src/auth/keyring.zig) ("keyring accepts one key name bundled with several algorithms" and "keyring refuses the same key name with the same algorithm twice") and in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) ("runtime authenticates a key name bundled with several algorithms", which signs the request with the second algorithm of the name and checks that the reply carries that algorithm and verifies only under that key, and "runtime refuses a request whose algorithm no key of the name carries"); and an hmac-sha512 request authenticates end to end and gets an hmac-sha512-signed answer in [`test/unit/tsig_sha512_runtime_test.zig`](../test/unit/tsig_sha512_runtime_test.zig). The key reload is proven in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig): a SIGHUP re-reads the key file and a request signed with the added key authenticates afterwards, a key the file no longer names is refused with BADKEY, a malformed or missing key file leaves the served keys answering, and the replaced keyring's freed secret slot is wiped. A TSIG-signed NOTIFY is answered with a TSIG that verifies under the same key and algorithm, an unsigned NOTIFY is answered with no TSIG record, and a NOTIFY whose MAC does not verify is still answered NOTAUTH with the BADSIG code, in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) ("runtime signs the answer to a signed NOTIFY", "an unsigned NOTIFY is answered with no TSIG record", and "a NOTIFY whose MAC does not verify is answered NOTAUTH with BADSIG"). A TSIG-signed AXFR answered with a sequence of signed frames, whose first frame verifies with the request MAC as a standard answer and whose later frames chain on the MAC of the frame before them, is asserted in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) ("a signed AXFR is answered with signed frames a client can verify"). |
| DNS over UDP/TCP transport | [RFC 7766 §6–§8](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7766), [RFC 9210 §3–§4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9210): two-octet TCP framing, bounded messages/connections, finite timeouts, and parallel response preparation with out-of-order sends; [RFC 9210 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9210#section-3): "All DNS resolvers and servers MUST support and service both UDP and TCP queries" and "A name server MAY limit the resources it devots to queries, but it MUST NOT refuse to service a query just because it would have succeeded with another transport protocol"; [§4.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9210#section-4.2): the connection management a server SHOULD or MAY provide; [§4.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9210#section-4.5): the recommended defaults. | `partial` for the named bounded subset: classic UDP limits, malformed/idle-safe continuous loops, and persistent TCP framing with at most 16 complete frames per connection. Each frame gets a fresh five-second complete-frame read/write deadline. Two of the RFC 9210 section 4.2 connection limits are the operator's: `tcp-max-connections-per-source <count>` caps the connections one source address holds at once, and a connection over the cap is closed at once, the way a connection with no worker slot is closed; `tcp-max-connection-duration <seconds>` caps the lifetime of one connection, and the connection is closed when the duration is over. Without either directive the server keeps its own policy: no per-source cap and no limit on the total duration of a connection. The other bounds stay a fixed ZIMDNS policy: 16 complete frames per connection, a 65535-octet frame, a five-second per-frame read/write deadline, and 8 concurrent TCP workers. Exactly 8 active TCP worker tasks are allowed under a global fixed ZIMDNS resource policy (not an RFC-defined cap); accepted connections with no worker slot close. Answers run concurrently: [`Runtime`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) guards the served zone and key state with a read-write lock, so any number of authoritative answers share it and a reload takes it exclusively, while one resolver mutex serializes the forwarder's own answers — the ceiling that remains for concurrent forwarding. Inbound TCP answers a query with the dispatch UDP uses: an authoritative question is answered from the zone and a forwarding question is answered by the forwarder, with RFC 7766 section 7's parallel preparation and out-of-order sends: a connection holds at most four frames in flight, each written whole under a per-connection mutex, so a pipelined answer is not held behind a slow forwarded one. Initial support is Linux/macOS only; TCP serving returns `UnsupportedPlatform` elsewhere. | `partial` for the exact named first-release subset: two-octet TCP framing, at most 16 complete frames per connection, a fresh five-second deadline on every frame read and write, and serial query handling. RFC 9210 section 3's rule is met on the inbound path: a forwarding query that arrives over TCP is answered by the forwarder, exactly as the same query over UDP is answered, and the answer goes back over the same connection as RFC 7766 section 5 requires ("all recursive and authoritative servers MUST send responses using the same transport as the query arrived on"). The bounds that stay a project policy and do not regress: 16 complete frames per connection, a 65535-octet frame, a five-second per-frame read/write deadline, and 8 concurrent TCP workers. The two connection limits RFC 9210 section 4.2 makes configurable are now the operator's, with no limit as the default: `tcp-max-connections-per-source <count>` for the connections one source address holds at once, and `tcp-max-connection-duration <seconds>` for the total duration of one connection. Still open: TCP serving on platforms other than Linux and macOS, which returns `UnsupportedPlatform`; the limits RFC 9210 section 4.2 and section 4.5 still leave fixed, which are a project policy and not operator-set: the transactions per connection are the fixed 16-frame cap and the total number of connections is the fixed worker cap — the idle timeout is no longer among them, because `tcp-idle-timeout <seconds>` sets it now, as RFC 9210 section 4.2 asks ("DNS server software SHOULD provide a configurable timeout for idle TCP connections"), with a five-second default, a 1 to 3600 second bound, and the edns-tcp-keepalive option carrying the connection's own timeout as the EDNS(0) TCP keepalive row records; and TCP Fast Open. | [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig), [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig), [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig), [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig), [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig) | Listener byte-frame, malformed, EOF/idle-recovery, and 16-frame-cap tests are in [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig); the inbound-TCP forwarding tests — a forwarding query answered with the upstream's answer over a real TCP socket, the same query answered the same way over UDP, an authoritative query over TCP unchanged, a client the recursion ACL refuses, and a truncated upstream answer retried over TCP upstream — are in [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig); configured two-zone UDP/TCP socket tests plus FIFO multi-frame and 8/9 active-connection-cap tests are in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig); the operator's connection limits — a source over the per-source cap refused while a connection of another source is served, a connection closed when its configured duration is over, and two connections of one source served when no cap is configured — are in [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig); the directive tests (one value per directive, a duplicate and a malformed value refused) are in [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig), and the wiring from the configuration to the listener is in [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig); the configurable idle timeout is proved by "config parser takes one TCP idle timeout" in [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) and by "server context carries the TCP idle timeout to the listener and the answer policy" in [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig); the out-of-order proof is "RFC 7766 section 7: a pipelined answer is not held behind a slow forwarded one" in [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig), where a real loopback upstream holds the first pipelined query for 400 ms and the authoritative second answer reaches the wire first; the integration test "concurrent TCP connections each answer every pipelined frame while workers are occupied" in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig) now matches each of its 96 pipelined frames to its query by message ID. |
| Safe forwarding and recursion policy | [RFC 1035 §4.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1035), [RFC 7766 §6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7766): explicit recursion policy, bounded UDP exchange, response identity validation, and TCP retry after TC. | `supported` for this exact subset: forwarding is opt-in only for `QUERY`/`RD`/`IN` queries from configured CIDRs; non-`QUERY` requests receive local `NOTIMP` before cache or policy evaluation, authoritative zones are selected first, denied clients receive `REFUSED` before any upstream I/O, and the TCP ingress runs that same policy, with the peer's address the client the recursion ACL, the cookie, and the ECS scope see. Upstream UDP uses two fresh source sockets/IDs and exact source/ID/question checks. A client query that carried no EDNS(0) leaves with one appended OPT that advertises this server's own 1232-octet payload (the DNS Flag Day 2020 recommendation), not the classic 512-octet size, so an ordinary referral-sized response is not truncated. TC retries over TCP only on platforms with enforced upstream TCP deadlines; otherwise it fails closed as `SERVFAIL` with EDNS Network Error. | `supported` for this exact subset over both ingress transports: the recursion ACL and the `REFUSED` a denied client gets before any upstream I/O, the local `NOTIMP` a non-`QUERY` request gets before cache or policy evaluation, the authoritative zone selected first, the two fresh upstream source sockets and IDs with the exact source, ID, and question checks, the TC retry over TCP, the cache keys that keep CD, DO, and the RFC 7871 scope apart, and the EDNS Network Error a failed exchange carries. The inbound TCP path is served by the same dispatch as UDP, so a forwarding query that arrives over TCP is answered with the same recursion ACL, the same cache, the same EDE behavior, and the same upstream checks as the same query over UDP, and an upstream answer that carries TC is still retried over TCP upstream. Out of scope for this profile: the upstream exchange stays UDP-first with that TCP retry, which RFC 7766 section 5 permits ("Stub resolvers and recursive resolvers MAY elect to send either TCP or UDP queries depending on local operational reasons"), so a TCP-first upstream policy, which would need a directive this profile has no use for, is absent; a limit on the TCP connections one source address may hold, which RFC 9210 section 4.2 makes a MAY, is absent; and the pipelined TCP queries of RFC 7766 section 6.2.1.1 are answered serially, not out of order. With `recursion allow` and no `forwarder`, the same ACL enables iterative resolution from the built-in root hints ([`src/iterate.zig`](../src/iterate.zig)): a referral is followed only down a child of the current zone, each delegation is asked the next label toward the question with QTYPE=A and only the last hop carries the client's name and type (RFC 9156 §2.3): a referral descends, an authoritative NOERROR continues with the next label against the same servers, NXDOMAIN ends the resolution (RFC 8020 §2), and a response that is neither authoritative nor a denial falls back to the full question, a glue address outside that child's bailiwick is used only as the place to send the next query, an alias chain is followed and merged into one answer, and the eight-alias and thirty-two-referral bounds end a hostile chain; a name server with no glue is resolved through its own delegation; a delegation the walk learns is kept for later names inside that zone until the zone's NS TTL runs out (capped at one day, thirty-two delegations of eight servers each), and an unreachable cached delegation falls back to the root hints. The iterative path validates when `trust-anchor-file` names an anchor: the hops carry DO=1, the resolver fetches the DS each parent publishes and the DNSKEY each zone publishes up to the anchor (bounded at four signers and eight ancestor zones, best effort), caching each fetched RRset by zone and type until its TTL runs out (forty-eight entries, TTL capped at one day), and the answer is secure only when that chain authenticates it; an unsigned answer is relayed as insecure once the parent's signed DS denial proves an insecure delegation, and a zone that publishes a DS makes the unsigned answer bogus (SERVFAIL with the RFC 8914 code). | [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig), [`src/iterate.zig`](../src/iterate.zig), [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig), [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig), [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) | ACL, non-`QUERY` local rejection, real loopback forwarding, wrong source/ID/question rejection, and TC-to-TCP tests in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig); the platform deadline gate is unit-tested in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig). The inbound-TCP path's forwarding, UDP parity, authoritative answer, refused client, and TC-retry tests are in [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig). Strict policy parser tests are in [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig). The referral, alias-chain, out-of-bailiwick glue, and bound tests in [`src/iterate.zig`](../src/iterate.zig), the loopback iterative answer test in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig), the minimized-hop walk and its fallback tests in [`src/iterate.zig`](../src/iterate.zig), and the parent-DS chain and KSK-to-ZSK adoption tests in [`src/dns/validate.zig`](../src/dns/validate.zig) cover the iterative path, and the live root-anchored run is recorded in the handoff. |
| Forged-answer resilience on the forwarding path | [RFC 5452 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5452#section-3) (the four conditions under which DNS data must be accepted), [§6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5452#section-6) (accept only in-domain records), [§9.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5452#section-9.1) (query-matching MUSTs), [§9.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5452#section-9.2) (extending the Q-ID space with unpredictable ports and IDs), and [§9.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5452#section-9.3) (may abandon UDP for TCP on suspected spoofing). RFC 5452 is a Proposed Standard that updates [RFC 2181](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2181); its §5 is "Birthday Attacks" and the in-domain rule is §6, not §5. The records §6's rule must leave alone are named by [RFC 2308 §5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2308#section-5) (the zone SOA of a negative answer) and [RFC 1034 §4.3.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1034#section-4.3.2) (the NS records of a referral). | `partial`: on every upstream UDP reply ZIMDNS checks the exact source address and port against the configured forwarder, the per-attempt cryptographically random 16-bit query ID drawn over the full 0–65535 range, QR, the opcode, exactly one question, and byte-for-byte question name, type, and class equality, and it rejects an unsolicited upstream ECS option; each of the two UDP attempts binds its own socket and draws a fresh randomized ID, so the two attempts carry distinct query IDs from distinct OS-assigned ephemeral source ports, and a TC reply is retried over TCP. §6's in-domain rule is implemented on the forwarding path: before an upstream answer is cached and relayed, its additional section and its authority section are filtered, and a record whose owner name is not in the domain of the query is dropped — a name is in the domain of the query when it is the query name itself or one of its ancestors or descendants, that is when the name is at or below the query name or the query name is at or below it, compared without regard to case (RFC 1035 section 2.3.3). The answer, the authority, and the additional sections are filtered, and the cached copy holds the filtered sections, so a later client is answered with the same records. The authority section keeps the records no client can use the answer without: the zone SOA of a negative answer (RFC 2308 section 5) with the DNSSEC denial proofs that ride with it (RFC 4034 section 4, RFC 5155 section 7.2), and the NS records of a referral (RFC 1034 section 4.3.2), so a negative answer never loses its SOA and a referral never loses the delegation it names. A referral is not an answer, but its additional section is filtered too: a record survives only when the delegation the query is about names its owner as the target of an NS record of the authority section, that NS record is in the domain of the query, and that target sits inside the zone the delegation names, so a referral keeps the glue of its own delegation and loses every other record the upstream put there, a referral whose glue is entirely out of that zone keeps none of it, a referral for a zone the query is not about is dropped whole (its NS records and every glue record), and the SOA or NS an exempt record keeps must name the query name itself or one of its ancestors, so an SOA for another domain cannot ride in a negative answer.
