| NXDOMAIN scope (RFC 8020) | [RFC 8020 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8020#section-2): "When an iterative caching DNS resolver receives an NXDOMAIN response, it SHOULD store it in its cache and then all names and resource record sets (RRsets) at or below that node SHOULD be considered unreachable. Subsequent queries for such names SHOULD elicit an NXDOMAIN response."; the same section's cut rule: "For a descendant name of the original NXDOMAIN name, the same set of NSEC or NSEC3 records proves the nonexistence of the descendant name. The iterative, caching resolver MUST return these NSEC or NSEC3 records in the response to the triggering query if the query had the DNSSEC OK (DO) bit set."; the same section's Warning: "if there is a chain of CNAME (or DNAME), the name that does not exist is the last of the chain ([RFC6604]) and not the QNAME. The NXDOMAIN stored in the cache is for the denied name, not always for the QNAME."; [§3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8020#section-3.1): "The correct response to ENTs is NODATA (i.e., a response code of NOERROR and an empty answer section)"; [§5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8020#section-5) repeating that an authoritative server answering an ENT with NXDOMAIN "are definitely wrong"; and [§3.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8020#section-3.2), which revises RFC 2308 §5 to cover a descendant QNAME: "or where the QNAME is a descendant of the original QNAME and the QCLASS is the same." RFC 8020 updates RFC 1034 and RFC 2308. | `partial`: the authoritative denial shape and the resolver-side §2 cut. The authoritative side answers a CNAME or DNAME chain whose last name lies inside a loaded zone and has no record at or below it with NXDOMAIN, the whole alias chain in the answer section, and the zone SOA in the authority section, so the denial names the last name of the chain; an alias target outside that zone keeps the plain alias answer so the client chases it at its own server; and an empty non-terminal is NODATA with the negative SOA. The forwarding path now implements the §2 cut with the §3.2 descendant rule: once an NXDOMAIN is cached, a later query for a name at or below it is answered NXDOMAIN from the cache, with the cached authority section and the negative TTL and no upstream query, while a sibling or unrelated name is not cut, a name with its own cached answer keeps that answer, and the cut expires with the cached entry. The cut is anchored on the name that does not exist, as §2's Warning words it — the last name of a CNAME or DNAME chain when the answer carried one, and the queried name when it did not — so the denial of an alias chain is stored under the denied name with the chain's own records stripped, and the queried name keeps no cache entry and is asked upstream again; the cut is applied to validated and unvalidated denials alike, since §2 makes a validated-only cut a MAY; the closest enclosing denial wins; and the lookup is a linear scan over the same bounded 64-entry table, so no subtree index and no per-name growth is added. A denial this server synthesizes from a cached NSEC proof (RFC 8198) is now stored in the same negative cache, so the §2 cut covers that denial too: the synthesized NXDOMAIN answers the names at or below the name it was synthesized for, and a name outside that subtree is not answered from it. The cut answer now carries the proof that justifies it: the NSEC or NSEC3 records the denial rests on, each with the RRSIG that covers it, in the authority section, and only for a client that set the DO bit, the rule the authoritative path already uses. The proof is the one the upstream answer carried, kept byte for byte, and it is cached with the denial, so the next client in the scope gets the same proof without another upstream query. A denial with no proof available is still served as it was: the upstream answer carried no NSEC or NSEC3 with the RRSIG that covers it, and a missing proof is no reason to withhold the denial. The forwarding path also synthesizes the NODATA response of RFC 8198 section 5.3 for a name the cached wildcard covers and a type it does not hold: NOERROR with an empty answer section, the zone SOA with the RRSIG that covers it, and the proof -- the closest encloser and next-closer records the positive answer rests on, plus the record that shows the wildcard's own types -- in the authority section, each with the RRSIG that covers it (RFC 4035 section 3.1.3, RFC 5155 section 8.7); the project's own validator accepts the NSEC form of that proof, where the NSEC sits at the wildcard's own name and leaves the queried type and the CNAME bit clear (RFC 4035 section 3.1.3.4), and an NSEC that does not cover the query proves nothing.| `partial`: the authoritative denial shape and the resolver-side §2 cut with the §3.2 descendant rule, over the bounded in-memory profile. A cut answer carries the cached denial's authority section with the negative TTL it has left (RFC 2308 §3/§5/§6) and the client's own question. Denials are held in memory only: nothing is cached across a restart. A denial this server synthesizes from a cached DNSSEC proof (RFC 8198) is stored as a reusable negative entry: a synthesized NXDOMAIN covers the names at or below the name it was synthesized for (the §2 cut), a synthesized NODATA covers that name and type only (RFC 2308 §5), and the entry lives as long as the NSEC proof it rests on, capped at the three hours RFC 8198 §5.4 sets. The forwarding path now denies from an NSEC3 proof as well: a validated-secure denial's NSEC or NSEC3 proof is harvested, and a later query the proof covers is answered with the denial that proof establishes, with no new upstream query and with the same denial, the same SOA, and the same TTL rules the NSEC path produces, so a client cannot tell which proof produced it. The NSEC3 denial follows RFC 5155 §8.3-§8.6: a NODATA needs an NSEC3 that matches the QNAME with the QTYPE and the CNAME bit clear, and an NXDOMAIN needs a closest encloser proof — the encloser matched by an NSEC3, the next closer name covered by one — plus an NSEC3 that covers the wildcard at that encloser. A covering NSEC3 with the Opt-Out flag set proves no non-existence (RFC 8198 §5.2), and neither does an encloser whose NSEC3 carries the DNAME bit (RFC 5155 §8.3). The forwarding path refuses a malformed proof: a proof whose RDATA kept a compression pointer is malformed and is refused as such RFC 4034 §4.1.1 says a sender MUST NOT compress the NSEC next domain name, and RFC 3597 §4 forbids compression in the RDATA of a type that is not well known, so such a proof is not cached, denies nothing, and is not carried in a cut answer. The NSEC3 RDATA holds no name at all (RFC 5155 §3.2: the Next Hashed Owner Name is the binary hash, not a domain name), so nothing in it can be compressed. The forwarding path also synthesizes the positive wildcard answer of RFC 8198 §5.3, which it is free to do: an upstream answer whose RRSIG Labels field is below the label count of the queried name is a wildcard expansion (RFC 4035 §5.3.4), the wildcard it came from is the one the RRSIG names (RFC 4035 §5.3.2), and that wildcard's records are cached with the signature that covers them and with the proof that arrived with them. The RRset is cached whole or not at all: the signature covers the whole RRset (RFC 4035 §5.3.2), so a wildcard RRset the cache cannot hold whole is not cached and the name it covers goes upstream (RFC 8198 §5.3). A later query the wildcard covers is answered with those records under the queried name, not under the wildcard's name, and with the signature that covers the wildcard RRset under the queried name as well (RFC 4035 §5.3.2): a client that set the DO bit gets an answer it can validate, and no answer is served at all without that signature, because an unsigned answer for a signed name is bogus (RFC 4035 §5.5). The answer's TTL is the TTL of the source RRset, held down to the life of the proof and never above the RRSIG's Original TTL (RFC 4035 §5.3.2): RFC 1034 §4.3.3 keeps the contents of the wildcard RRs unmodified when they synthesize RRs, and RFC 8198 §5.4's bound by the life of the proof is what holds the TTL down. The signature rides with the TTL of the records it covers (RFC 4034 §3.1), so the two never differ on the wire. The positive answer carries no zone SOA: the SOA of RFC 2308 §3 and its MINIMUM rule of §5 govern a negative answer, and an SOA carried in a positive answer without the signature that covers it would leave a validating client with an unsigned RRset in a signed zone (RFC 4035 §5.5). The proof must establish the answer: the closest encloser, which is the wildcard's own parent, must exist and the queried name must not, proved by an NSEC that matches the closest encloser and one that covers the queried name (RFC 4035 §5.4), or by an NSEC3 that matches the closest encloser and one that covers the hash of the queried name with no Opt-Out flag (RFC 5155 §8.8, RFC 8198 §5.2); the wildcard and the proof must be of the same zone, the wildcard's zone being the signer name of the signature that covers it (RFC 4035 §5.3.1), the proof is looked for in that zone, so a cache that holds another zone's proofs answers the wildcard as well, and the wildcard covers the name only when the name is one label below the wildcard's own parent, so nothing can exist between the wildcard and the queried name (RFC 1034 §4.3.3). A query the wildcard proof does not cover goes upstream as before. What stays open on that path: the NODATA response for a name the wildcard covers is synthesized only when the cache holds the record that shows the wildcard's own types (an NSEC that matches the wildcard, or an NSEC3 that matches the wildcard's hash, with the queried type and the CNAME bit clear), each with the RRSIG that covers it; a query the cache cannot prove that way goes upstream as before, and the NODATA is served only to a client that set the DO bit, as the wildcard answer is; the wildcard is harvested only from an answer that carries the RRSIG, so a client that did not set the DO bit gets no wildcard answer; and the NSEC proof records ride in the authority section only when each of them came with the RRSIG that covers it (RFC 4035 §3.1.3), an answer whose proof could not be carried is served all the same, the way a denial without a proof is. The negative TTL is reduced to an SOA MINIMUM: the aggressive cache keeps the zone SOA with the proof it arrived with, and the denial's TTL is the smaller of that SOA's TTL and its MINIMUM field (RFC 2308 §5), bounded by the TTL of the proof it rests on and by the three hours RFC 8198 §5.4 sets. The SOA rides with the RRSIG that covers it, so the denial a client with the DO bit receives is one the project's own validator judges secure and not bogus (RFC 4035 §5.5); a zone that does not sign its SOA has none to carry, and the denial it serves stays as it was. The forwarding path also synthesizes the NODATA response of RFC 8198 section 5.3 for a name the cached wildcard covers and a type it does not hold: NOERROR with an empty answer section, the zone SOA with the RRSIG that covers it, and the proof -- the closest encloser and next-closer records the positive answer rests on, plus the record that shows the wildcard's own types -- in the authority section, each with the RRSIG that covers it (RFC 4035 section 3.1.3, RFC 5155 section 8.7); the project's own validator accepts the NSEC form of that proof, where the NSEC sits at the wildcard's own name and leaves the queried type and the CNAME bit clear (RFC 4035 section 3.1.3.4), and an NSEC that does not cover the query proves nothing.| [`src/zone/index.zig`](../src/zone/index.zig) (`lookupExact` returns the denial when the chain ends at a missing in-zone name; `Zone.contains` and `Zone.nodeExists` supply the two bounds), [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) (the alias records and the synthesized CNAME ride in the answer beside the SOA), and [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) (`Cache.cutLookup` answers a descendant from the cached denial, `isDescendant` compares the two names on their label structure, `applyNegativeTtl` sets the served negative TTL, `coveringRrsig` pairs a cached NSEC with the RRSIG that covers it, and `aggressiveAnswer` puts the proof in the authority section for a client that set DO, `harvestWildcard` caches the wildcard an expanded answer carried with the zone its signature names, and `wildcardAnswer` answers the name the wildcard covers), and [`src/dns/dnssec.zig`](../src/dns/dnssec.zig) (`keepProof` keeps the NSEC or NSEC3 and its RRSIG as the upstream published them, `proofMalformed` finds the proof whose RDATA kept a compression pointer and `proofNamesUncompressed` refuses to carry it, `deny` hands the denial the proof it rests on, `denyNsec3` denies from a cached NSEC3 chain by the rules of RFC 5155 §8.3-§8.6, and `insertWildcard` and `wildcardAnswer` keep a wildcard RRset with the signature that covers it and hand out the answer its proof establishes) | [`src/zone/index.zig`](../src/zone/index.zig) ("zone index synthesizes bounded DNAME redirects" now expects the NXDOMAIN denial with the SOA, the DNAME record, and the synthesized `www.target.example.` target). Wire-level coverage in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) ("RFC 8020: an alias chain ending at a missing in-zone name denies the last name of the chain" checks RCODE NXDOMAIN, AA, one CNAME in the answer and one SOA in the authority section, and that an out-of-zone target stays NOERROR with the CNAME) and ("RFC 8020: an empty non-terminal is NODATA, never NXDOMAIN"). Resolver-side coverage in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig): "RFC 8020 section 2: a cached NXDOMAIN cuts the names below it" (a direct child and a deeper descendant are cut, a sibling and the name above are not), "RFC 8020 section 2: a cached answer below a cut name still wins", "RFC 8020 section 2: the cut expires with the cached entry", and "RFC 8020 section 2: a descendant of a cached NXDOMAIN is answered without an upstream query" (the forwarder points at a closed port, so an upstream attempt would fail instead of answering). The reuse of a synthesized denial is covered in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) by "RFC 8198 section 4: a synthesized denial is reused from the negative cache" (the client below the cut is answered from the cache, and a name outside the cut is asked upstream), "RFC 8198 section 5.4: a synthesized denial lives as long as its proof", and "RFC 2308 section 5: a synthesized NODATA covers its own name only". The proof in a cut answer is covered in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) by "RFC 8020 section 2: a cut answer carries the NSEC proof and its RRSIG" (the client that set DO gets the two NSEC records the denial rests on and the RRSIG that covers each, beside the zone SOA with the RRSIG that covers it), "RFC 8020 section 2: a cut answer for a client without DO carries no proof", "RFC 8020 section 2: the cut answer for a name below the denial carries the cached proof" (the second client in the scope is answered from the cache with the proof and no upstream query), and "RFC 8020 section 2: a cut answer with no proof is served all the same". The NSEC3 denial is covered in [`src/dns/dnssec.zig`](../src/dns/dnssec.zig) by "aggressive NSEC3 cache denies from a closest encloser proof (RFC 8198 5.2)" (the name the next closer name covers is denied NXDOMAIN, a name below it shares the denial, the encloser itself is a NODATA for a type it does not hold, and a name the proof does not cover and a name outside the zone are not denied), "an NSEC3 proof that does not establish the denial is not used (RFC 8198 5.2)" (an Opt-Out cover, a missing wildcard cover, a missing next-closer cover, and a DNAME bit on the encloser each deny nothing), "an NSEC3 proof with a malformed bitmap denies nothing", and "aggressive NSEC3 cache proofs expire like the NSEC ones (RFC 8198 4)". The refusal of a malformed proof is covered in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) by "a proof whose RDATA kept a compression pointer is refused as malformed" (the proof is not cached, so it denies nothing and no cut answer carries it). The wildcard answer is covered in [`src/dns/dnssec.zig`](../src/dns/dnssec.zig) by "aggressive cache answers the name a cached wildcard proof covers (RFC 8198 5.3)" (the name the wildcard covers is answered with the wildcard's records and the signature that covers them, and a name below it, a name with no proof that it does not exist, and a type the wildcard does not hold are not), "a wildcard answers only with a proof of its own zone (RFC 4035 5.3.1)" (a wildcard of one zone is not answered by the proof of another), "a wildcard answers only where the closest encloser is proven (RFC 5155 8.8)" (a name the covering NSEC proves absent is not answered when the closest encloser is unproven), "a reused wildcard slot does not serve the wildcard it held" (a wildcard whose record borrows the message it came from is not cached, and the slot it would take keeps the wildcard and the records it held), "a wildcard RRset the cache cannot hold whole is not cached (RFC 4035 5.3.2)" (a wildcard of five records is not cached, so the name it covers is not answered from the cache), and "a wildcard is answered from a cache that holds another zone's proofs too" (the proof of the zone the wildcard belongs to is found among another zone's proofs), and "a record type the RFCs do not forbid is not malformed" (an A record is not called malformed because its RDATA holds no name, while an NSEC whose next domain name was compressed still is). In [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) by "a cached wildcard proof answers the client without an upstream query" (the forwarder points at a closed port, so an upstream attempt would fail instead of answering; the answer carries the wildcard's record and the RRSIG that covers it under the queried name, and no SOA), "a DO=1 client gets the wildcard's RRSIG and the answer validates" (the same answer, signed with a test key, and the project's own validator accepts it as secure), "a wildcard RRset the cache cannot hold whole is not answered from the cache" (a wildcard of five records is not cached, so the question goes upstream), "the RRSIG of a synthesized wildcard answer carries the TTL of the records it covers" (the signature rides with the TTL the proof bounds), and "a name the cached wildcard proof does not cover goes upstream". The forwarding path over an upstream answer that carries an NSEC3 proof is covered in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) by "RFC 8198 section 5.2: an NSEC3 proof in an upstream answer denies without a second query" (the query the proof covers is answered NXDOMAIN with the SOA and the three NSEC3 records and their RRSIGs in the authority section, and a name the proof does not cover is asked upstream). The §2 anchoring of the denial is proved by the two tests "RFC 8020 section 2: the denial of an alias chain is stored under the name that does not exist" and "RFC 8020 section 2: a denial without a chain is stored under the name that was asked for" in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig). |
| Referral glue and TC (RFC 9471) | [RFC 9471 §3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9471#section-3.1), which updates RFC 1034: "when a name server generates a referral response, it MUST include all available glue records for in-domain name servers in the additional section or MUST set TC=1 if constrained by message size", and [§3.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9471#section-3.3) the corresponding RFC 1034 §4.3.2 step 3b text "If all glue RRs for in-domain name servers do not fit, set TC=1 in the header"; [§3.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9471#section-3.2) sibling-domain glue, a SHOULD whose omission is permitted. | `supported`: every A and AAAA record the zone holds for a delegation name server whose name is inside the zone's own origin is attached to the referral, which covers the in-domain name servers of RFC 9471 section 3.1 (the MUST) and the sibling-domain name servers of section 3.2 (the SHOULD), because a zone delegated from the same parent lies inside the delegating zone's origin; a name server outside the origin contributes nothing (RFC 5452 section 6) and no glue is fetched beyond the zone's own records; a datagram response that cannot hold the referral sets TC=1 and drops the answer, authority, and additional sections so the client retries over TCP, which is the section 3.1 MUST; a referral that fits is sent whole with TC clear.| `supported` for that exact subset: section 3.1 in-domain glue and section 3.2 sibling glue the zone holds, with TC-on-truncation and authoritative invariants, all with unit and loopback UDP/TCP evidence. No glue is fetched beyond zone records, and externally out-of-zone glue remains unsupported. | [`src/zone/index.zig`](../src/zone/index.zig) (`appendDelegation` attaches every in-origin A and AAAA for each delegation NS target; out-of-origin targets contribute nothing), [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) (`.referral` populates authority and additional; `truncateClassicUdpResponse` and `truncateSignedClassicUdpResponse` set TC), [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig) (both UDP send paths truncate before sending)| Three tests separate the halves: "a referral carries only the glue of the delegation it names" in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) asserts that the in-domain name server and the name server inside the zone's origin ride in the additional section while the name server outside the origin does not, "a referral carries the glue of a name server of a sibling zone" asserts the same for the name server of a zone delegated from the same parent (RFC 9471 section 2.2), and "RFC 9471: a referral too large for the datagram limit sets TC=1" builds a zone whose referral cannot fit the 512-octet classic limit and asserts TC is set on the truncated answer. The in-bailiwick filter is also unit-tested in [`src/zone/index.zig`](../src/zone/index.zig) ("zone index returns both in-domain and sibling-domain glue on a referral" and "zone index drops out-of-bailiwick glue on a referral"), and the classic-limit TC behaviour is asserted end to end in [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig) ("classic UDP response over 512 bytes sets TC instead of clipping"). |
| Host name syntax and length (RFC 1123 §2.1) | [RFC 1123 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1123#section-2.1) "Host Names and Numbers": "the restriction on the first character is relaxed to allow either a letter or a digit. Host software MUST support this more liberal syntax." and "Host software MUST handle host names of up to 63 characters and SHOULD handle host names of up to 255 characters." RFC 1123 is a Standards Track host requirements document; its §4.1.2 subsections belong to the FTP requirements it corrects (RFC 959), and §4.1.2.2 is "Telnet Format Control", so the DNS-relevant host requirement is §2.1, not §4.1.2.2. | `supported` for this declared name-server profile: the name text the server is given is bounded at 63 octets a label and 255 octets a name (`max_label_len` and `max_wire_len` in [`src/dns/name.zig`](../src/dns/name.zig)), and a label may begin with a digit. The RFC 952 hyphen rule — a label may neither begin nor end with a hyphen — is enforced by the shared `Name.hasHyphenEdgeLabel` rule: the zone parser's `validateName` refuses such a name, so a zone-file owner label, the `$ORIGIN`, and the domain fields of the RDATA it parses are refused with the parser's `BadName` error, and the `query` command's name argument is refused with the CLI's `BadName` error before a packet is built. | `supported` for this declared name-server profile: the name text a host enters — the `query` command's name argument and every zone-file owner label — accepts a leading digit and enforces the 63-octet label and 255-octet name bounds, so §2.1's first-character relaxation and its length rules hold for the whole name-entry surface. The RFC 952 hyphen rule is enforced on every name the server is given: the zone parser's `validateName` refuses a name whose any label begins or ends with a hyphen, through the shared `Name.hasHyphenEdgeLabel` rule, so a zone-file owner label, the `$ORIGIN`, and the domain fields of the RDATA it parses (`NS`, `CNAME`, `DNAME`, `PTR`, `MX`, `RT`, `AFSDB`, `RP`, `SVCB`, `HTTPS`, `SRV`, and the two `SOA` names) may not carry one, and the refusal is the parser's `BadName` error; the `query` command's name argument obeys the same rule, so a query for a name whose label begins or ends with a hyphen is refused with the CLI's `BadName` error instead of being sent to a zone that can never hold that name. Excluded from this profile rather than missing from it: the rest of the host-name character syntax — a label may still hold any octet, so the underscore labels of `_443._tcp` and `_sip._tcp`, the `*` wildcard owner, and every other non-letter, non-digit octet a zone file uses are still accepted, and the `query` command's name argument still takes any presentation text within the length bounds apart from that hyphen rule. The rest of §2.1 and of RFC 1123 bind applications rather than a name server: entering a host identity as either a domain name or a dotted-decimal address ("The host SHOULD check the string syntactically for a dotted-decimal number before looking it up in the Domain Name System"), the §2.2 application-layer name-to-address translation, and the RFC 1122/1123 transport requirements are not a profile capability. | `supported` for the same profile: the §2.1 first-character relaxation and the 63-octet label and 255-octet name bounds; the RFC 952 hyphen rule — a label may neither begin nor end with a hyphen — for the names the server serves and for the `query` command's name argument. Out of scope for a name server: the rest of the RFC 952 label character syntax, the dotted-decimal host-identity input, and the application-layer requirements. | [`src/dns/name.zig`](../src/dns/name.zig) (`Name.fromText` for presentation text, `Name.hasHyphenEdgeLabel` for the RFC 952 hyphen rule, `Name.parse` and `max_label_len`/`max_wire_len` for received names), [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig) and [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig) (the CLI's name argument, which `buildQueryPacketForName` refuses when a label begins or ends with a hyphen), [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig) (`validateName` for every zone-file name: the owner labels, the `$ORIGIN`, and the domain fields of the RDATA, each refused by the shared `Name.hasHyphenEdgeLabel` rule) | Nine tests in [`test/unit/host_name_syntax_test.zig`](../test/unit/host_name_syntax_test.zig) drive the two entry surfaces this row names: "RFC 1123 2.1: the query command's name argument accepts a leading digit" builds a query packet for `4example.com.` and reads that name back off the wire; "RFC 1123 2.1: the query command's name argument refuses an over-long label and name" refuses a 64-octet label and a 256-wire-octet name with `BadName`; "RFC 1123 2.1: the query command's name argument refuses a leading or trailing hyphen" refuses the name `-bad.example.` and the name `bad-.example.` with `BadName`; "RFC 1123 2.1: the query command's name argument accepts an underscore label and an interior hyphen" builds a query for `_443._tcp.example.` and reads that name back off the wire; "RFC 1123 2.1: a zone-file owner label accepts a leading digit" loads `4www` as `4www.example.`; "RFC 1123 2.1: a zone-file owner label refuses a leading or trailing hyphen" refuses the owner `-bad` and the owner `bad-` with `BadName`; "RFC 1123 2.1: a zone-file owner label accepts an underscore label and an interior hyphen" loads `_443._tcp` and `sri-nic`; "RFC 1123 2.1: a zone-file owner label reaches the 255-octet name bound" loads a 255-wire-octet owner; and "RFC 1123 2.1: a zone-file owner label refuses an over-long label and name" refuses a 64-octet label and a 256-wire-octet owner with `BadName`. The shared rule is `Name.hasHyphenEdgeLabel` in [`src/dns/name.zig`](../src/dns/name.zig), covered there by "RFC 1123 2.1: a label may neither begin nor end with a hyphen"; the zone side calls it from `validateName` in [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig) and the CLI calls it from `buildQueryPacketForName` in [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig), and the two tests named above — "RFC 1123 2.1: a zone-file owner label refuses a leading or trailing hyphen" and "RFC 1123 2.1: the query command's name argument refuses a leading or trailing hyphen" — prove that both surfaces refuse exactly the names the shared rule refuses. 
| Name-server clarifications (RFC 2181) | [RFC 2181 §5.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2181#section-5.1) whole-RRSet answers and the TC requirement; [§5.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2181#section-5.2) "In no case may a server send an RRSet with TTLs not all equal"; [§6.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2181#section-6.1) "A server for a zone should not return authoritative answers for queries related to names in another zone, which includes the NS, and perhaps A, records at a zone cut, unless it also happens to be a server for the other zone."; [§6.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2181#section-6.2) "in none of these cases should a server for the parent zone, not also being a server for the subzone, set the AA bit in any response for a label at a zone cut"; [§8](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2181#section-8) unsigned TTL semantics and "Implementations should treat TTL values received with the most significant bit set as if the entire value received was zero."; [§9](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2181#section-9) when TC is and is not set, including that extra records that do not fit must not make a reply set TC; [RFC 1034 §4.3.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1034#section-4.3.2) referral format, and [RFC 9471 §3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9471#section-3.1) and [§3.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9471#section-3.2) in-domain and sibling glue in a referral; [§10.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2181#section-10.1) CNAME exclusivity, "An alias name (label of a CNAME record) may, if DNSSEC is in use, have SIG, NXT, and KEY RRs, but may have no other data." RFC 2181 is a Standards Track clarification memo that updates RFC 1034, RFC 1035, and RFC 1123 and is itself updated by RFC 2535, RFC 4033, RFC 4034, RFC 4035, RFC 4343, RFC 5452, and RFC 8767; it uses no BCP 14 requirement keywords, so each item above is quoted rather than paraphrased as a MUST. | `supported` for this declared name-server profile: §5.1 is implemented — a query returns the whole RRSet, and the shared datagram truncation path sets TC when that RRSet does not fit. §6.1 and §6.2 are implemented through AA clear on every referral plus REFUSED for a name no loaded zone is authoritative for. §10.1 is implemented through the zone parser's refusal of a CNAME that shares an owner with other data. §5.2 is implemented — records sharing one owner/type are normalized to the lowest TTL before serving, so unequal RRsets no longer reach the wire — while §8 remains implemented on both sides of the wire: a received TTL with the sign bit set counts as the whole value zero (`normalizeTtl` in [`src/dns/record.zig`](../src/dns/record.zig)), and a zone TTL above 2^31-1 is refused at parse (`max_ttl` in [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig)), so no TTL this server writes to the wire carries a sign bit. §9 is implemented on the shared truncation path. Its additional-section rule holds on the authoritative path: a referral's additional section carries only the address records of the delegation's own name servers, in names the zone holds glue for, so no data of a name it does not serve rides along, and the same response builder serves the referral on UDP, on TCP, and on the TSIG-signed path. Its TC rule now follows the section: a reply whose answer and authority sections fit the datagram limit is sent with the additional section omitted and the TC bit clear, because §9 says the extra data that did not fit is omitted rather than truncated away with a TC; a reply whose answer section does not fit keeps the TC bit and drops the sections, so the client retries over TCP; a referral keeps TC, because RFC 9471 §3.1 requires TC=1 for a referral that cannot hold the glue of its in-domain name servers. The TSIG a signed message ends with stays in the reply, and so does the EDNS OPT record, so a reply that omits the additional section is still an EDNS reply (RFC 6891 §6.1.1). §4 source/port selection, §5.4 cache merging, and §7 SOA placement are out of scope for this server's profile rather than missing from it. | `supported` for the same profile: the named subset — §5.1 whole-RRSet answers, §5.2 uniform per-RRset TTLs, §6.1/§6.2 zone authority, §8 TTL semantics, and §10.1 CNAME exclusivity — plus §9's rule that TC marks an RRSet that could not be included. §9's additional-section rule is implemented: a referral's additional section carries only the address records of the delegation's own name servers, and only for names the zone holds glue for. §9's TC rule is implemented: TC marks a reply whose answer section did not fit, and the extra data of a reply that does fit is omitted with the TC bit clear. The EDNS OPT record stays in a reply that omits the additional section, as RFC 6891 §6.1.1 asks of a reply to a query that carried an OPT. §4 source/port selection, §5.4 cache merging, and §7 SOA placement are not this server's profile. | [`src/zone/index.zig`](../src/zone/index.zig) (zone-cut referral and `Zone.contains`), [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig) (CNAME co-ownership refusal, per-record TTL retention, the `max_ttl` bound), [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) (whole-RRset answers, AA computation, TC truncation; `replyWithoutAdditionalSection` omits the additional section of a reply whose answer and authority sections fit and leaves the TC bit clear, and `coreLength` measures the part of the reply a reply is required to carry), [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) (the REFUSED answer for a name no loaded zone is authoritative for), [`src/dns/record.zig`](../src/dns/record.zig) (TTL field decode) | §5.1 and §5.2 in [`test/unit/rfc2181_name_server_test.zig`](../test/unit/rfc2181_name_server_test.zig) ("RFC 2181 5.1 and 5.2: an RRSet is answered whole at the lowest TTL" answers a two-record A RRSet with two answers, both carrying the lowest TTL of the set) and in [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig) ("zone parser serves one RRSet at its lowest TTL (RFC 2181 section 5.2)"); the TC half of §5.1 in [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig) ("classic UDP response over 512 bytes sets TC instead of clipping", where 40 A records at one owner cannot fit, so TC is set and the answer section is empty) and in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) ("RFC 9471: a referral too large for the datagram limit sets TC=1"). §9's own rule has five tests in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig): "RFC 2181 section 9: an answer whose additional section does not fit is sent without it and without TC" builds a reply whose answer and authority sections fit but whose additional section does not and asserts that the reply carries no TC bit, keeps the answer and the authority record, and has an empty additional section; "RFC 2181 section 9: an answer whose answer section does not fit still sets TC" asserts that a reply whose answer section does not fit sets TC and drops the answer; "RFC 2181 section 9: a referral whose glue does not fit keeps TC" asserts that a referral keeps the TC bit; "RFC 2181 section 9: a reply that fits is sent byte for byte" asserts that a reply within the limit goes out unchanged; and "RFC 2181 section 9: the signed path omits the additional section the same way" asserts that the signed path omits the additional section, keeps the TSIG the message ends with, and that the MAC recomputed over the reply authenticates it. "RFC 6891 section 6.1.1: a reply without the additional section keeps the OPT record" asserts that a reply that drops the additional section keeps the OPT record, so the extended RCODE and the DO bit of the answer survive. §6.1 and §6.2: AA is clear on a referral in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) ("RFC 9471: a referral carries in-domain and sibling-domain glue", "a referral carries only the glue of the delegation it names", "a referral carries the glue of a name server of a sibling zone", "an authoritative answer keeps its additional section byte for byte", and "authoritative response emits referral glue and uncompressed DNAME CNAME"), and a name no loaded zone is authoritative for is REFUSED with AA clear in [`test/unit/rfc2181_name_server_test.zig`](../test/unit/rfc2181_name_server_test.zig) ("RFC 2181 6.1: a name no loaded zone is authoritative for is REFUSED"). §8 in [`src/dns/record.zig`](../src/dns/record.zig) ("received TTL with the sign bit set counts as zero" and "OPT record keeps its EDNS TTL field") and in [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig) ("zone parser refuses TTLs above 2^31-1, including SOA MINIMUM (RFC 2181 §8)"). §10.1 in [`src/zone/parser.zig`](../src/zone/parser.zig) ("zone parser rejects CNAME co-ownership") and in [`test/unit/rfc2181_name_server_test.zig`](../test/unit/rfc2181_name_server_test.zig) ("RFC 2181 10.1: a CNAME sharing an owner with other data is refused", which refuses the CNAME that follows another record at the same owner). |

## Resolution, cache, and diagnostics

| Area | RFC requirement | Current baseline | First-release target | Implementation path | Test path / evidence |
|---|---|---:|---:|---|---|
| Negative answers and cache | [RFC 2308 §3–§6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2308): NXDOMAIN/NODATA SOA semantics, negative TTL extraction, cache aging, and the two rules that bind a cached negative answer to the SOA it carries — §5: "Negative responses without SOA records SHOULD NOT be cached as there is no way to prevent the negative responses looping forever even with a short TTL", and §6: "it MUST add the cached SOA record to the authority section of the response with the TTL decremented by the amount of time it was stored in the cache" — with §5's second paragraph revised by [RFC 8020 §3.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8020#section-3.2). [RFC 8198 §5.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8198#section-5.4) caps the effective TTL of a negative response at the three hours it takes from RFC 2308 §5, and asks a resolver that uses NSEC or NSEC3 aggressively to "reduce the TTL of NSEC and NSEC3 records to match the SOA.MINIMUM field in the authority section of a negative response, if SOA.MINIMUM is smaller". [RFC 9520 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9520#section-2) (Standards Track, December 2023) is about resolution failures, and it states that NXDOMAIN and NODATA "are not conditions for resolution failure" and "can be cached as described in [RFC2308]"; its [§3.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9520#section-3.2) rule (cache a resolution failure for at least 1 second and no longer than 5 minutes) therefore binds resolution failures, not the negative TTL of an NXDOMAIN or NODATA answer, and that rule is now implemented: a resolution failure is held in a bounded failure cache for at least the operator's `minimum-failure-cache-time` seconds and never longer than the RFC's five minutes, and the next client for the same question is answered from it without a new upstream query. Section 3.2's backoff SHOULD is implemented as well: "Resolvers SHOULD employ an exponential or linear backoff algorithm to increase the cache duration for persistent resolution failures", and its minimum cache duration stays configurable by the operator. [RFC 4035 §4.7](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4035#section-4.7) is the restriction RFC 9520 §3.4 keeps with its "with some restrictions" wording: "In order to prevent caching of a transient validation failure (which might be the result of an attack), resolvers SHOULD track queries that result in validation failures and SHOULD only answer from the BAD cache after the number of times that responses to queries for that particular <QNAME, QTYPE, QCLASS> have failed to validate exceeds a threshold value" — and it leaves that threshold to the implementation. | `supported` for the bounded in-memory profile: authoritative NXDOMAIN/NODATA SOA construction, positive and SOA-derived negative entries, TTL aging, no caching of failures or TC responses in the answer cache, and cache-key isolation for CD and EDNS DO variants. The negative TTL is the smaller of the SOA record's TTL and the SOA MINIMUM field (RFC 2308 §3/§5), and the cached SOA is served with that TTL already decremented (RFC 2308 §6). The negative cache is subtree-aware: a cached NXDOMAIN answers the names below it (RFC 8020 §2, see the RFC 8020 row). A resolution failure is cached as well: an upstream SERVFAIL, an upstream REFUSED, an upstream FORMERR, and an unreachable upstream are held in a bounded 64-entry failure cache beside the answer cache, and the next client for the same question is answered from it with the same RCODE (SERVFAIL for the unreachable upstream, REFUSED for a refused upstream) and without a new upstream query. A cached failure expires on its own — for the operator's `minimum-failure-cache-time` seconds (default 5, inside RFC 9520 §3.2's one-second floor and five-minute ceiling) for the first failure of a question, and for twice that time for a failure that keeps repeating, up to the RFC's five-minute ceiling — is never answered as an NXDOMAIN or a NODATA, never sets AD, and never enters the answer cache. A question that resolves again forgets its failure count, so its next failure is held for the operator's minimum again. A DNSSEC validation failure is such a failure, and RFC 4035 §4.7 holds it back: a bogus upstream answer is answered SERVFAIL with the RFC 8914 code the validator produced, and that failure is held in the same cache, but the cache of it is used only after the question has failed to validate more times than the threshold of one, so the first failure is never answered from the cache — the next client for the same question goes upstream again and is answered with the same SERVFAIL and the same code from the fresh validation. The other resolution failures are not validation failures, so the threshold does not touch them: an upstream SERVFAIL, an upstream REFUSED, an upstream FORMERR, and an unreachable upstream are answered from the cache on the first failure, as before. A denial this server synthesizes from a cached DNSSEC proof (RFC 8198) joins the same negative cache: it is stored with the scope it covers (an NXDOMAIN covers the names at or below the name it was synthesized for, a NODATA that name and type only), it is stored with the SOA of the zone the proof came from — the SOA the upstream answer carried — so the client can cache the denial itself (RFC 2308 §3/§6), its TTL is the negative TTL of RFC 2308 §5 (the smaller of that SOA's TTL and its MINIMUM field) bounded by the TTL of the proof it rests on and by the three hours RFC 8198 §5.4 sets, and the next client for a name in that scope is answered from it without an upstream query, with that SOA in the authority section and its TTL already decremented. A synthesized denial with no SOA is not cached at all, because the upstream answer that produced it carried none (RFC 2308 §5). | `supported` for the bounded profile: the negative TTL rule of RFC 2308 §3/§5, the decremented SOA of §6, the RFC 8020 §2 cut, and the RFC 9520 §3.2 failure cache over the same bounded in-memory profile. RFC 9520 §3.4's MUST to cache DNSSEC validation failures is implemented: a validation failure is held in the same bounded failure cache with the SERVFAIL RCODE and the RFC 8914 DNSSEC-bogus code the validator produced, and RFC 4035 §4.7's restriction is implemented with it, so that cache is used only after the question has failed to validate more times than the threshold of one — the number the implementation picked, because the RFC leaves it open and one is the smallest threshold that keeps a single transient failure out of the cache: the first failure is answered with the fresh SERVFAIL and its code, the next client for the same question goes upstream again, and every client from the third on is answered from the cache with the same RCODE and the same code and without a new upstream query; the entry expires on its own — for the operator's `minimum-failure-cache-time` seconds for the first failure of the question and longer for a failure that keeps repeating, never past §3.2's five-minute ceiling — is never answered as an NXDOMAIN or a NODATA, never sets AD, and never enters the answer cache. §3.2's backoff SHOULD is implemented: the cached time of a failure doubles with every consecutive failure of the same question, from the operator's `minimum-failure-cache-time` seconds up to the RFC's five-minute ceiling, and a question that resolves again starts again at the operator's minimum. Out of scope: durable on-disk caching. The denial this server synthesizes from a cached RFC 8198 proof is cached now, with the scope it covers, with the SOA of the zone the proof came from, and with the negative TTL of RFC 2308 §5 (the smaller of the SOA's TTL and its MINIMUM field); the cached and the served denial carry that SOA in the authority section with the TTL it has left, so the client can cache the denial itself, and a synthesized denial with no SOA is not cached. A cut answer carries the proof that justifies it — the NSEC records the denial rests on, each with the RRSIG that covers it, in the authority section, and only for a client that set the DO bit — so the NSEC or NSEC3 records RFC 8020 §2 asks a cut answer to carry are not missing from that path. The NSEC3 case is on that path as well: `deny` falls through to the NSEC3 chain (`denyNsec3`, RFC 8198 §5.2), and the resolver test "RFC 8198 section 5.2: an NSEC3 proof in an upstream answer denies without a second query" answers the next query from the cache with the NSEC3 records in the authority section, so an NSEC3 denial does not need a second upstream query. | [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig), [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) (`cacheTtl` derives the negative TTL from the SOA, `applyNegativeTtl` serves it decremented, `Cache.cutLookup` cuts the subtree, `Cache.failures` holds the resolution failures (`FailureCache.ttlFor` doubles the cached time of a failure that keeps repeating, up to the RFC's five-minute ceiling, `FailureCache.forget` forgets the count when the question resolves again, and `FailureEntry.servable` keeps a validation failure out of the cache until the count of the validation failures of that question passes the threshold of RFC 4035 §4.7), `sectionSoa` picks the zone SOA out of an upstream answer, and `aggressiveAnswer` and `denialTtl` give a synthesized denial that SOA and its negative TTL), [`src/dns/dnssec.zig`](../src/dns/dnssec.zig) (the aggressive-NSEC cache keeps the zone SOA with the proof it arrived with, so `deny` hands the denial the SOA to carry, and it keeps the RRSIG the validator accepted over each proof and over the SOA (RFC 4035 section 5.4) rather than the first signature that covers the RRset), [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) (the `minimum-failure-cache-time` directive), and [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig) (the cache time reaches the resolver's policy) | Authoritative negative tests in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig), cache positive/negative aging, exclusion, and CD/DO isolation tests in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig), the negative TTL rule in "RFC 2308 section 5: the negative TTL is the smaller of the SOA TTL and the SOA MINIMUM" in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig), the subtree cut in the four "RFC 8020 section 2" tests in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig), the reuse of a synthesized denial in the three "RFC 8198" and "RFC 2308 section 5" tests in the same file, the SOA of a cached denial in "RFC 2308 section 6: a cached denial carries the zone SOA", its negative TTL in "RFC 2308 section 5: the negative TTL of a synthesized denial is the SOA's", and the refusal to cache a denial without one in "RFC 2308 section 5: a denial without an SOA is not cached" in the same file, the failure cache in the seven "RFC 9520 section 3.2" tests in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) (a SERVFAIL answered from the cache without a second upstream query, a cached failure answered as the failure it is, a cached failure expiring at the maximum cache time, a failure that keeps repeating being held longer, the backoff never passing the five-minute ceiling, a question that resolves again forgetting its failure count, and the failure cache leaving the answer cache's own bounds alone), and the cached validation failure in the two "RFC 9520 section 3.4" tests in the same file (a cached validation failure answered as the failure it is, and a cached validation failure expiring like every other failure), and the threshold of RFC 4035 §4.7 in the three "RFC 4035 section 4.7" tests in the same file (a validation failure not answered from the cache before the threshold, a validation failure past the threshold answered from the cache, and a failure that is not a validation failure answered from the cache at once), the directive accept and reject tests in [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) ("config parser takes one RFC 9520 failure cache time and rejects every other shape"), the config-to-policy carrying test in [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig) ("server context carries the RFC 9520 failure cache time into the forwarding policy"), and live forwarding tests in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig). |
| Serve stale | [RFC 8767 §4–§6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8767): bounded stale window and explicit stale-response policy. | `supported` for a 24-hour stale window with a 30-second stale TTL, only after an upstream failure and only from the bounded 64-entry in-memory cache. | `supported` for that exact bounded policy; prefetch, persistence, and stale-if-error policy controls are out of scope. | [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) | Fresh/stale positive and negative aging, the stale-window boundary, and the expired-entry retention the failure path depends on are tested in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) ("cache ages positive and negative responses and emits stale EDE", "RFC 9520 section 4: a lookup past the TTL keeps the entry for the stale path"), together with the RFC 2308 negative-TTL ceiling ("RFC 2308 section 5: an upstream negative TTL is capped at three hours") and the CNAME-with-NODATA case ("RFC 2308 section 2.2: a CNAME with NODATA is cached by the SOA, not the chain"). |
| Extended DNS Errors | [RFC 8914 §2–§4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8914): EDE option encoding and deliberate mapping of stale/upstream conditions. [RFC 8914 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8914#section-2) states that the option "can be included in any response (SERVFAIL, NXDOMAIN, REFUSED, even NOERROR, etc.) to a query that includes an OPT pseudo-RR", gives the option its fields — OPTION-CODE 15, the 16-bit INFO-CODE, and the EXTRA-TEXT, whose OPTION-LENGTH is "2 plus the length of the EXTRA-TEXT field" — and [§3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8914#section-3) asks that "the source of the error SHOULD be attributed in the EXTRA-TEXT field". [§4.16](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8914#section-4.16) assigns INFO-CODE 15 to "Blocked": a domain on a blocklist imposed by the operator's own policy, and [§4.19](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8914#section-4.19) assigns INFO-CODE 18 to "Prohibited": "An authoritative server or recursive resolver that receives a query from an 'unauthorized' client can annotate its REFUSED message with this code. Examples of 'unauthorized' clients are recursive queries from IP addresses outside the network, blocklisted IP addresses, local policy, etc." | `partial`: the EDE codes this server emits are Stale Answer (3), Stale NXDOMAIN (19), Network Error (23), Blocked (15), Prohibited (18), Not Authoritative (20), plus the RFC 8914 DNSSEC-bogus codes 6–10 (DNSKEY Missing (9) now emitted as SERVFAIL when a configured DS anchor names the signer zone but no DNSKEY RRset arrives) and the four codes that explain an answer the server returns without a signed verification — Unsupported DNSKEY Algorithm (1), Unsupported DS Digest Type (2), DNSSEC Indeterminate (5), and No Zone Key Bit Set (11) — which ride on the answer the client still gets, because RFC 4035 section 5.2 leaves such a zone unsigned rather than bogus, and the resolver that emits the forwarding codes exists only when a forwarder, recursion ACL, or cookie policy is configured. The cached copy of such an answer carries the same code, so a client served from the cache is told what the client that fetched it was told. A name the operator's blocklist refuses is answered REFUSED with the Blocked code (15) and the EXTRA-TEXT "blocked by policy", on the authoritative path and before any forwarding, and only when the query carried an OPT pseudo-RR: a query without one is refused exactly as before, with no OPT record and no EDE option. A client the operator's recursion ACL refuses is answered REFUSED with the Prohibited code (18) and the EXTRA-TEXT "prohibited by policy", on the forwarding path and before any upstream query, and only when the query carried an OPT pseudo-RR: a query without one is refused exactly as before, with no OPT record and no EDE option.  A client served a resolution failure from the failure cache (RFC 9520 section 3.2) is told the error is a cached one: the response carries INFO-CODE 13 with the EXTRA-TEXT "cached error" next to the code the fresh failure carried, so the client still learns what the error was, and only when the query carried an OPT pseudo-RR: a query without one is answered with the plain cached error exactly as before, with no OPT record and no EDE option.The other refusals (a malformed query, a refused transfer) carry no EDE option. Every EDE option this server writes carries an EXTRA-TEXT that says why: the authoritative path writes "blocked by policy" for the Blocked code, the forwarding path writes "prohibited by policy" for the Prohibited code, the refusal that is not authoritative writes "not authoritative for this name", and the forwarding path writes a purpose string for each of its codes ("stale answer", "stale nxdomain", "network error", "dnssec bogus", "signature expired", "signature not yet valid", "dnskey missing", "rrsigs missing", "unsupported dnskey algorithm", "unsupported ds digest type", "dnssec indeterminate", "no zone key bit set"). When a response must be shortened to fit the datagram, the EDE option is the first thing dropped: the OPT record is re-encoded without it and the truncation bit is set, as [RFC 8914 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8914#section-3) asks ("servers SHOULD truncate messages by dropping EDE options before dropping other data from packets", "Implementations SHOULD set the truncation bit when dropping EDE options"). The OPT record itself stays, because its extended RCODE, its DO bit, and its payload size are header fields of the record and not the option, and the answer and authority sections stay as far as they fit. A reply that is shortened past that keeps the OPT record as well, on the signed path as on the unsigned one, with the TC bit set. A response that fits is sent byte for byte, the EDE option and all. | `partial` for that exact subset; no full EDE taxonomy claim. The codes this server does not use stay open: 0 Other, 4 Forged Answer, 12 NSEC Missing, 14 Not Ready, 16 Censored, 17 Filtered, 21 Not Supported, 22 No Reachable Authority, and 24 Invalid Data. The Prohibited code (18) is in use: a client the recursion ACL refuses is told it is prohibited, with the EXTRA-TEXT "prohibited by policy", and only when the query carried an OPT pseudo-RR. The Not Authoritative code (20) is in use: both refusals of [§4.21](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8914#section-4.21) — the REFUSED answer to a query with the RD bit clear, and the REFUSED answer to a domain the server is not authoritative for and is not configured to recurse for — carry INFO-CODE 20 with the EXTRA-TEXT "not authoritative for this name", and only when the query carried an OPT pseudo-RR: a query without one is refused exactly as before, with no OPT record and no EDE option. The four codes that explain an insecure answer are in use — 1 Unsupported DNSKEY Algorithm, 2 Unsupported DS Digest Type, 5 DNSSEC Indeterminate, and 11 No Zone Key Bit Set — and they ride on the answer the client still gets instead of failing it (RFC 4035 section 5.2). RFC 8914 §3's truncation preference is met: a message that must be shortened drops its EDE option before any other data, and the truncation bit says so. The signed path's full truncation keeps the OPT record as the unsigned path does, so the DO bit, the payload size, and the extended RCODE survive it, while the answer and authority sections are dropped, the TC bit says the message is not the whole one, and the TSIG stays the last record of it. What stays open is the error code of a shortened reply: RFC 8914 §3 has the EDE option dropped first, so a client that gets a truncated reply does not learn the code and has to ask again to see it. | [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig), [`src/dns/dnssec.zig`](../src/dns/dnssec.zig), [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) (the blocked and prohibited refusals and the EDE options they carry, and the truncation that drops the EDE option), and [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) (the blocklist and recursion-ACL refusal paths) | Stale-code, network-error, DNSSEC-bogus, and purpose-text assertions in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) ("cache ages positive and negative responses and emits stale EDE", "network failures add EDE only when the query has EDNS", "dnssec bogus responses carry SERVFAIL with the EDE code and text", "aggressive NSEC answers SERVFAIL with bogus EDE on malformed bitmap", "ede purpose text covers emitted codes", "RFC 8914 section 4.2: an answer the server cannot verify carries the code that says why", "the cached copy of an unverifiable answer carries the same code"); the blocked refusal, the query without EDNS, and the refusal that is not a blocklist refusal in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) ("a blocklisted name is answered REFUSED with the RFC 8914 Blocked EDE code", "a blocklisted name without EDNS is refused without an EDE option", "a refusal that is not a blocklist refusal carries no EDE option", "a client outside the recursion ACL is refused with the RFC 8914 Prohibited EDE code", "a client outside the recursion ACL without EDNS is refused without an EDE option", "a refused transfer carries no EDE option"); the byte-for-byte comparison of the blocked refusal with the plain refusal, the byte-for-byte comparison of the prohibited refusal with the plain refusal, the EDE option's Extra Text and OPTION-LENGTH, and the plain refusal that carries no EDE option in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) ("RFC 8914: a blocked refusal differs from a plain refusal only by the EDE option", "RFC 8914: a prohibited refusal differs from a plain refusal only by the EDE option", "RFC 8914: the blocked refusal's EDE option carries its Extra Text", "RFC 8914: a plain refusal carries no EDE option"); upstream source/identity failures and TC fallback are exercised in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig). The DS-anchor-without-DNSKEY EDE trigger (RFC 8914 4.10) is exercised in [`src/dns/validate.zig`](../src/dns/validate.zig) ("a DS anchor whose child publishes no DNSKEY is bogus with the DNSKEY Missing EDE (RFC 8914 4.10)"). The codes that explain an insecure answer — 1, 2, 5, and 11 — are exercised in [`src/dns/validate.zig`](../src/dns/validate.zig) ("a DNSKEY RRset with only an unsupported algorithm leaves the answer insecure with the Unsupported DNSKEY Algorithm EDE (RFC 8914 4.2)", "a DS whose digest type this build cannot compute leaves the answer insecure with the Unsupported DS Digest Type EDE (RFC 8914 4.3)", "a DNSKEY without the Zone Key flag leaves the answer insecure with the No Zone Key Bit Set EDE (RFC 8914 4.12)", "a DS that matches no DNSKEY leaves the status indeterminate with the DNSSEC Indeterminate EDE (RFC 8914 4.6)", and "a chain that validates cleanly carries no EDE"), and their INFO-CODEs and EXTRA-TEXT purpose strings in [`src/dns/dnssec.zig`](../src/dns/dnssec.zig). The Zone Key flag the No Zone Key Bit Set code reads is RFC 4034 section 2.1.1's bit 7 (0x0100), the low bit of the DNSKEY flags' first octet, and the validator reads that bit. The truncation rule of §3 is proved in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig) ("RFC 8914 section 3: a truncated answer drops its EDE option and sets TC", "RFC 8914 section 3: the answer and authority section survive the EDE drop", "RFC 8914 section 3: an answer that fits keeps its EDE option", "RFC 8914 section 3: the signed path drops the EDE option the same way", "RFC 8914 section 3: the RRSIGs over the records that remain still verify", "RFC 8914 section 3: the signed truncation drops the EDE option and keeps the OPT record", "RFC 6891 section 6.1.1: a signed answer too large for the datagram keeps its OPT record", "RFC 8945 section 4.2: the TSIG is the last record of a truncated signed reply", and "the unsigned truncation sends the bytes it has always sent"), and the same over a real socket in [`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig) ("EDNS UDP signed response keeps OPT and retains TSIG after truncation"). |
| DNS Cookies | [RFC 7873 §4–§7](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7873), [RFC 9018 §4–§6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9018): configurable responder validation, missing/invalid-cookie compatibility behavior, and secret rotation. | `supported` for an opt-in HMAC-SHA256 responder policy: 8-byte client cookies, 16–40-byte cookie validation, current/previous secrets, generated server cookies, and BADCOOKIE responses. Missing cookies retain normal processing; opt-in strict replay protection uses a 32-entry CookieReplayDb FIFO ring keyed by client address (only full cookies enter the window, off by default). The resolver side is always on: every upstream query carries the Cookie option with one unpredictable 8-octet client cookie and, once a server hands one back, that server's cookie (kept for one hour per server, bounded ring); a response whose cookie does not echo this client's cookie is dropped as a forged answer, and an RFC 7873 section 5.3 BADCOOKIE answer is retried once with the returned server cookie. | `supported` for that exact responder subset including the 32-entry CookieReplayDb FIFO opt-in; no timestamp database or generic cookie-policy claim. | [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig), [`src/dns/header.zig`](../src/dns/header.zig), [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) | Cookie lengths, current/previous rotation, invalid/missing behavior, BADCOOKIE extended RCODE, bounded replay-window, replay-enforcement, and replay-off tests in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig); strict syntax tests in [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig). |
| DNS64 address synthesis (RFC 6147) | [RFC 6147 §5.1.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6147#section-5.1.1): "By default, DNS64 implementations MUST NOT synthesize AAAA RRs when real AAAA RRs exist"; [§5.1.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6147#section-5.1.2): an RCODE other than 0 is treated as though the answer were empty, with RCODE=3 (Name Error) the one RCODE that is "handled according to normal DNS operation"; [§5.1.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6147#section-5.1.4): the special exclusion set for AAAA records, where an AAAA inside an excluded range "MUST" be treated as though the answer were empty and the offending records "MUST NOT" be returned; [§5.1.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6147#section-5.1.5): "the CNAME or DNAME chain is followed until the first terminating A or AAAA record is reached", and the chain "is included as part of the answer along with the synthetic AAAA"; [RFC 6672 §2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6672#section-2.2): a DNAME substitution replaces the suffix labels of the name with the labels in the DNAME's RDATA, and only whole labels are replaced; [§5.1.6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6147#section-5.1.6): an empty answer section is the trigger and the A records of the name are retrieved with another query; [§5.1.7](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6147#section-5.1.7): the synthetic record takes the A record's NAME and CLASS, type 28, a 16-octet RDATA, and "the minimum of the TTL of the original A RR and the SOA RR for the queried domain"; [§5.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6147#section-5.2): "If no prefix is available, the algorithm MUST use the Well-Known Prefix 64:ff9b::/96"; [§5.3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6147#section-5.3.1): a DNS64 "MUST" answer a PTR query for a record in IP6.ARPA whose address matches a configured Pref64::/n with one of two alternatives, option 2 being a synthesized CNAME into IN-ADDR.ARPA whose target "SHOULD" have PTR RDATA and no existing CNAME; [§5.3.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6147#section-5.3.2): the additional section is passed unchanged and never synthesized; [§5.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6147#section-5.4): the answer section is populated per §5.1.7 and the authority and additional sections come from the response the final query produced; [§5.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6147#section-5.5): the DNSSEC rules for a synthesized record, including that a validating resolver MUST validate the negative AAAA answer before querying for A; [RFC 6052 §2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6052#section-2.2): the IPv4-embedded IPv6 address format, the only allowed prefix lengths (32, 40, 48, 56, 64, 96), the reserved u octet whose 8 bits "MUST be set to zero", and where the IPv4 address sits in each form; [§2.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6052#section-2.4) Table 1: the worked examples this row's tests use; [§3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6052#section-3.1): "The Well-Known Prefix MUST NOT be used to represent non-global IPv4 addresses" | `partial`: the NAT64 prefixes are operator-supplied with `nat64-prefix <prefix> [<ipv4-range>]`, repeatable, and a prefix is rejected at config-parse time unless it is an IPv6 prefix of one of RFC 6052 §2.2's six lengths with a zero u octet; the optional range is the IPv4 address range the prefix serves and is rejected unless it is an IPv4 CIDR. On the forwarding path, an IN AAAA query whose upstream answer section is empty is answered with one AAAA per A record of the same name, the prefix that serves the A record's IPv4 address (the most specific configured IPv4 range wins, and a prefix configured without a range serves every IPv4 address), the IPv4 address embedded in that prefix exactly where RFC 6052 §2.2 puts it; the Well-Known Prefix is no exception: RFC 6052 §3.1 says it must not represent a non-global IPv4 address, so it never serves one. The addresses it must not represent are the blocks RFC 5735 §3 lists, the private ranges of RFC 1918 among them: 0.0.0.0/8, 10.0.0.0/8, 127.0.0.0/8, 169.254.0.0/16, 172.16.0.0/12, 192.0.0.0/24, 192.0.2.0/24, 192.88.99.0/24, 192.168.0.0/16, 198.18.0.0/15, 198.51.100.0/24, 203.0.113.0/24, 224.0.0.0/4 and 240.0.0.0/4. A network-specific prefix may still serve such an address; a name with a real AAAA in the upstream answer is never synthesized over (§5.1.1). An answer that holds a CNAME or DNAME chain instead of an address is followed to the owner that holds the A records (§5.1.5): the CNAME at the queried name, or the DNAME at an ancestor with the name below it substituted (RFC 6672 §3.1), names that owner, and the A records of that owner come from one more upstream query. The chain stays in the answer section next to the synthetic AAAA, and the synthetic record carries the A record's owner name and class, so the answer names the host the alias points at. The chase is bounded: a name that repeats in the chain ends it, a chain longer than 16 hops ends it, and a chain that leaves the zone the answer came from, which is the zone the answer's SOA names, ends it; that zone comes from the answer's SOA, so an answer that carries no SOA is chased under the hop limit and the repeat check alone; the upstream is asked at most once per hop, so the queries the synthesis makes stay bounded. An AAAA record whose address is inside the §5.1.4 exclusion set is not real AAAA data: the offending records are dropped from the answer section, and an upstream answer that held only such records counts as empty, so the synthesis proceeds for that name. The exclusion set is the IPv4-mapped network ::ffff:0:0/96, the range the RFC says to include by default, the Well-Known Prefix 64:ff9b::/96 of RFC 6052 §2.1, the site's own Pref64::/n, the prefixes the server synthesizes into, and the IPv6 prefix ranges the operator named with `dns64-exclude <ipv6-prefix>` — the mechanism §5.1.4 asks for; without that directive the default set applies, and a range that is not a valid IPv6 prefix is refused at parse time. An upstream RCODE other than 0 counts as an empty answer (§5.1.2), so the A query follows an error answer as well and the client is answered with the synthesized record under NOERROR rather than with the error; NXDOMAIN is the one RCODE that is handled as the name error it is, so it is relayed unchanged and no A query follows it. The synthesized record carries the A record's TTL capped by the SOA's (§5.1.7), the authority section keeps the zone's SOA with the RFC 2308 §5 negative TTL so the missing AAAA can be cached, and the answer is neither authoritative nor DNSSEC-secure: no RRSIG is attached and AD is clear, because the record comes from no zone. The A records come from one more upstream query, for the owner the chain ended at or for the same name when there is no chain, through the same forwarder, with the same source, ID, and question checks as any other query. That query carries the client subnet scope the AAAA answer was fetched for, so both answers are fetched for one scope: [RFC 7871 §7.1.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7871#section-7.1.1) sets the outgoing SOURCE PREFIX-LENGTH to the shorter of the client's SOURCE PREFIX-LENGTH and the server's maximum cacheable prefix length, and [§7.3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7871#section-7.3.1) ties the records of an answer to the network the answer was fetched for, so an address fetched for one scope is never put beside an answer fetched for another. A client that sent no client subnet option gets an A query with no option either. The answer the client receives reports the scope of the answer the synthesis rests on, which is the scope of the A answer and not the scope the AAAA answer carried. The answers the synthesis rests on are validated whenever this server validates at all, which is when it has a configured trust anchor and the client did not set CD: the negative AAAA answer is validated before the A query follows it, and the A answer is validated before the synthesis (§5.5). The DO bit of the client's query decides only whether the proofs are handed back, not whether the answer is checked, because the upstream request always carries DO=1 (RFC 3225 §3), so skipping the validation would let an attacker use DNS64 to circumvent DNSSEC. An answer that does not validate ends the exchange with SERVFAIL and the RFC 8914 INFO-CODE that says why (§5.5 item 2). The AD bit follows the RFC's own rule (§5.5 item 1): with CD clear and DO clear it stays clear, and a synthesized answer never carries it, because the record comes from no zone. Without a configured prefix the forwarding path is exactly what it was: no synthesis and no second upstream query. That last part is a plain departure from RFC 6147 §5.2, whose "If no prefix is available, the algorithm MUST use the Well-Known Prefix 64:ff9b::/96" this server does not follow. The profile's DNS64 is opt-in through the operator's `nat64-prefix` directive, and the RFC's default of the Well-Known Prefix is not used, because the profile does not assume the operator wants DNS64 turned on. The A query that fetches the records is asked again without the address when the upstream refuses it ([RFC 7871 §7.1.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7871#section-7.1.3)), so an upstream that refuses a query carrying an address no longer ends the synthesis with the empty answer, and the answer reports the scope the A answer was actually fetched for, which is the /0 scope after such a retry (RFC 7871 §7.3.1). Named gaps, which is why the row is `partial`: the §5.5 DNSSEC rules for a synthesized record are implemented — the negative AAAA answer and the A answer are validated before the synthesis whenever this server has a trust anchor and the client did not set CD, and a synthesis whose negative AAAA answer and A answer both validated carries the AD bit when the client asked with the DO bit, which is §5.5 item 2's "SHOULD set the AD bit", while a client that asked without DO gets no AD bit, as §5.5 item 1 requires; the §5.3.1 IP6.ARPA reverse mapping is implemented in its option-2 form only: a PTR query in class IN whose 32-nibble ip6.arpa name is exactly an RFC 6052 embedding of one configured prefix, with the address re-embedded byte for byte so the reserved and suffix bits are zero, is answered with a synthesized CNAME into the matching IN-ADDR.ARPA name followed by the PTR records that name owns, and only when the target has PTR data and no CNAME of its own; the answer is NOERROR with AA and AD clear; the synthesized CNAME is unsigned; option 1 (authoritative answers for the prefix) is not provided; and a client that set CD gets no synthesis; an A record whose IPv4 address no configured range serves is not synthesized at all, so the configured prefixes are the only ones used and the Well-Known Prefix is never reached for; and §5.4's rule that the authority and additional sections are copied from the final (A) query's response is narrowed to the zone's SOA carried by the negative AAAA answer, and the RFC 8198 aggressive-NSEC path answers an empty AAAA from a cached denial proof without the synthesis, and a denial this server synthesized from such a proof is now kept in the negative cache, so the client that asked for it without DO is answered from the cache with the same denial instead of a synthesis; the authoritative path still synthesizes no records, because the referral path attaches only the A and AAAA records the zone already holds for a delegation's name servers (`appendDelegation` in [`src/zone/index.zig`](../src/zone/index.zig)) and the typed RDATA rows record that no A, AAAA, or TXT additional records are synthesized | `partial`: the bounded profile above — operator-supplied NAT64 prefixes, each with the IPv4 range it serves, the RFC 6052 §2.2 placement, the RFC 6052 §3.1 rule that the Well-Known Prefix never represents a non-global IPv4 address, the §5.1.4 exclusion set with the operator's excluded ranges on top of the default set, synthesis on the forwarding path only, the A record's TTL capped by the SOA's, the zone's SOA in the authority section, the chase bounded by the zone the answer's SOA names (an answer without an SOA is chased under the hop limit and the repeat check alone), no authority or DNSSEC claim on the synthesized answer, and the A query fetched for the scope the AAAA answer was fetched for and retried without the address when the upstream refuses it (RFC 7871 §7.1.3), so the answer the client gets reports the scope the A answer was actually fetched for. The §5.5 validation of the negative AAAA answer and of the A answer is in, for a resolver with a trust anchor and a client that did not set CD, and so is the AD bit of a synthesized answer under §5.5 item 2, set when the client asked with the DO bit and both answers validated and clear otherwise; the §5.3.1 reverse mapping is the option-2 CNAME form, triggered only when the IN-ADDR.ARPA target has PTR data and no CNAME, with the synthesized CNAME unsigned and AD clear and option 1 left out, and the RFC 6147 §5.2 default of the Well-Known Prefix stays out of the first release because the profile's DNS64 is opt-in through the operator's `nat64-prefix` directive | [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) (`Nat64Prefix.parse`, `Nat64Prefix.isWellKnown`, `Nat64Prefix.represents`, the `nat64-prefix` directive with its optional IPv4 range, and the `dns64-exclude` directive), [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig) (the prefixes and the excluded ranges reach the runtime's resolver policy), [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) (`Policy.nat64_prefixes`, `Policy.dns64_excluded`, `Dns64List`, `synthesizeDns64Answer`, [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig) ("RFC 6052 section 2.2: the IPv4 address sits in the prefix as the RFC's table shows"); the directive accept and reject tests in
