| Zone transfers (AXFR/IXFR/NOTIFY) | [RFC 5936 §4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5936#section-4): AXFR sequencing and framing; [RFC 1995](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1995): IXFR query and fallback; [RFC 1996 §3.7](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1996#section-3.7), [§4.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1996#section-4.5), and [§4.7](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1996#section-4.7): the NOTIFY request, the SOA it hints at, and its response shape; [RFC 1982 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1982#section-3): the serial arithmetic the NOTIFY comparison uses; [RFC 9103](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9103): strict-TLS zone transfer, whose §7.1 requires the server to select the `dot` ALPN token and whose §7.2 requires TLS 1.3 or later. | `partial`: SOA-framed AXFR sequences with the apex SOA first and last, bounded standalone frames, and IXFR answered from the authority SOA serial, incrementally when the zone's delta log connects that serial to the current one — the incremental answer is the RFC 1995 section 4 difference sequence, the records the old zone held and the new one does not followed by the records the new one holds and the old one did not — and with a full-transfer fallback otherwise, all built, unit-tested, and served over plain DNS/TCP: the ordinary authoritative TCP loop recognises an AXFR/IXFR question inside the general Do53 frame loop ([`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig):984) and hands it to [`Runtime.encodeTransferFrames`](../src/auth/runtime.zig), so `--serve-tcp` answers an unsigned AXFR or IXFR on the wire; a NOTIFY for a served zone is answered by the serial comparison instead of being mirrored: the server reads the SOA the master hints at in the NOTIFY's answer section, compares that serial with the zone's own serial with the RFC 1982 arithmetic, and answers NOERROR when the hinted serial is newer and NOTAUTH when it is not. RFC 1996 §4.7 gives the response shape (NOERROR, the QR bit, the echoed question, and no answer records), so the zone's own SOA in the answer section and the NOTAUTH refusal for a serial that is not newer are this profile's own rules and not RFC 1996's, because RFC 1996 §3.11 leaves the comparison to the receiving server's own refresh decision; a NOTIFY for a zone this server does not serve is REFUSED as any other out-of-zone request is, a NOTIFY that carries a TSIG is answered with a TSIG-signed response of the same shape (RFC 8945 §5.3) and an unsigned NOTIFY with no TSIG record, and a NOTIFY with no SOA hint is legal and is answered NOERROR, because RFC 1996 §3.7 lets a master notify without a hint and the slave then asks its masters for the new data, while a NOTIFY whose question is not the zone's SOA is FORMERR as any other malformed question is; no refresh timer follows and no transfer is triggered, because this profile pulls a zone only at startup (the catalog consumer row above); `--serve-xfr` serves the RFC 9103 strict-TLS transfer channel: the listener terminates a TLS 1.3 handshake with the `dot` ALPN token, as section 7.1 requires, and then speaks the RFC 7858 section 3.3 framing over that channel, so a transfer query is answered with the same SOA-framed AXFR and IXFR sequences the Do53 TCP path serves; the transfer port never answers a cleartext AXFR, and an out-of-zone or TSIG-signed transfer query is still refused with one REFUSED message each. A TSIG-signed AXFR or IXFR over the reachable Do53 TCP path is answered with a sequence of signed frames instead: the first frame is a standard answer whose MAC covers the request MAC and the whole TSIG record (RFC 8945 §5.3), and every later frame chains on the MAC of the frame before it (§5.3.1), so a client that verifies the chain accepts the whole transfer, and a signed request for a name this server does not serve is refused with a signed frame.
The master side of NOTIFY is implemented: `notify <apex> <address:port>` (repeatable) sends one RFC 1996 NOTIFY, with
the zone's SOA in the answer section, after an RFC 2136 update or a SIGHUP reload that moved the serial; the targets are
the configured addresses rather than the zone's NS RRset, and the datagram is sent once with no retry, so a lost
announcement falls back to the secondary's refresh timer. | `partial`: AXFR/IXFR message shaping and sequencing stay served over DNS/TCP as the baseline does, and they are served again over the RFC 9103 strict-TLS channel `--serve-xfr` opens, so the same SOA-framed sequences reach a secondary over a TLS 1.3 session with the `dot` token. A NOTIFY for a served zone is answered with the serial comparison, the RCODE that comparison dictates, and the zone's own SOA in the answer section; the SOA in the answer section and the NOTAUTH refusal for a serial that is not newer are this profile's own rules, since RFC 1996 §4.7 gives the response shape without answer records. A NOTIFY that carries a TSIG is answered with a TSIG-signed response and an unsigned NOTIFY with no TSIG record (RFC 8945 §5.3). The refresh trigger and the secondary role that would follow a NOTIFY stay excluded, as do outbound what a secondary still lacks is the refresh side and the inbound transfer client, and the catalog consumer re-fetches only at startup. | [`src/dns/types.zig`](../src/dns/types.zig) (types 251/252), [`src/zone/transfer.zig`](../src/zone/transfer.zig), [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig), [`src/transfer/axfr.zig`](../src/transfer/axfr.zig), [`src/transfer/xfr_tls.zig`](../src/transfer/xfr_tls.zig) | AXFR/IXFR classification, framing, ordering, chunking, fallback, refusal, TSIG-chaining-primitive, and secondary-timer unit tests in [`src/transfer/axfr.zig`](../src/transfer/axfr.zig), [`src/transfer/xfr_tls.zig`](../src/transfer/xfr_tls.zig), and [`src/zone/transfer.zig`](../src/zone/transfer.zig), plus the encrypted-listener refusal test in [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig); the NOTIFY serial comparison — the NOERROR answer with the zone's own SOA, the NOTAUTH answer for an equal or older serial, and the RFC 1982 wrap case — in [`src/zone/transfer.zig`](../src/zone/transfer.zig) ("transfer answers a NOTIFY with a newer serial NOERROR and the zone SOA", "transfer answers a NOTIFY with an equal or older serial NOTAUTH", "transfer answers a NOTIFY whose serial wrapped past 2^32 NOERROR"), and the wire-level NOTIFY answers — NOERROR with the zone's SOA, NOTAUTH for an equal or older serial, NOERROR without an SOA hint, FORMERR for a question that is not the zone's SOA, REFUSED for a zone this server does not serve — in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) ("runtime answers NOTIFY by comparing the serial and refuses transfers singly"), and the signed NOTIFY answers — a TSIG that verifies under the same key and algorithm, no TSIG record at all for an unsigned NOTIFY, and NOTAUTH with BADSIG for a NOTIFY whose MAC does not verify — in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) ("runtime signs the answer to a signed NOTIFY", "an unsigned NOTIFY is answered with no TSIG record", and "a NOTIFY whose MAC does not verify is answered NOTAUTH with BADSIG"); a TSIG-signed AXFR answered with a sequence of signed frames, whose first frame verifies with the request MAC as a standard answer and whose later frames chain on the MAC of the frame before them, is asserted in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) ("a signed AXFR is answered with signed frames a client can verify"), and the signed refusal of an out-of-zone transfer request in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) ("a signed out-of-zone AXFR is refused with a signed frame"); the pre-change run is the dated snapshot [`docs/reports/transfer-1995-1996-5936-9103.log`](../docs/reports/transfer-1995-1996-5936-9103.log); BIND/Knot/NSD loopback interop is deferred to [`test/interop/README.md`](../test/interop/README.md). Socket integration carries transfer bytes over the reachable Do53 TCP path in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig): an AXFR query and an IXFR query carrying a stale client serial in its authority SOA go over a loopback TCP connection, the reply is read as two-octet-prefixed frames to end of stream, and the concatenated answers are asserted record by record against the loaded zone — apex SOA first, apex SOA last, AA set, echoed question, no authority or additional data — so the RFC 1995 full-transfer fallback is observed on the wire rather than inferred. This row stays `partial` because the RFC 1995 and RFC 1996 rows it names are still partial: the incremental answer's difference sequence and the NOTIFY shape are this profile's own rules. |
| DNSSEC authoritative serving minimum | [RFC 4034](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4034): DNSKEY/RRSIG/NSEC/DS wire data; [RFC 4035 §3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4035#section-3.1): DO-bit processing without AD; [RFC 4035 §3.1.3.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4035#section-3.1.3.3): the wildcard-expansion proof (the signed NSEC covering the next closer name) rides with a signed positive wildcard answer; [RFC 5155 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-3) and [§5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-5): the NSEC3 RR and its iterated salted hashing; [RFC 5155 §7.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-7.2) with [§7.2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-7.2.1)–[§7.2.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-7.2.5): the NSEC3 denial proofs an authoritative server serves (the closest encloser proof, the name error answer, the no-data answer, the wildcard no-data answer), and [§7.2.7](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-7.2.7): the Opt-Out closest provable encloser proof a referral to an unsigned subzone carries, and [§8.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-8.4): the validator's MUST to find an NSEC3 that covers the wildcard at the closest encloser, and [§8.6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-8.6): the validator's MUST that the NSEC3 covering the next closer carries the Opt-Out flag; [RFC 5155 §6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-6): the Opt-Out flag and the closest provable encloser it makes necessary; [RFC 5155 §7.2.9](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-7.2.9): server failure when a run-time hash collision keeps the chain from proving the name; [RFC 5155 §7.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-7.3): the apex NSEC3PARAM that states the parameters of the chain; [RFC 9276](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9276) (BCP 236, which updates RFC 5155): the iteration and salt guidance for a published chain; [RFC 8914](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8914): DNSSEC-bogus EDE INFO-CODEs 6–10; [RFC 8198 §4–§5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8198): aggressive NSEC use. [RFC 7129](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7129) ("Authenticated Denial of Existence in the DNS") is an Informational RFC: it does not update RFC 5155 and is cited here as commentary on the same denial-of-existence mechanisms only. [RFC 4033](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4033) ("DNS Security Introduction and Requirements", a Proposed Standard that obsoletes RFC 2535 and updates RFC 1034) is cited here as the requirements-and-introduction statement only: it defines no wire format, encoding, or message-processing rule, so it contributes no implementable requirement to this row and deliberately gets no row of its own. Its normative obligations on DNSSEC-aware resolvers, validators, and signing authorities are covered by the DNSSEC signing/validation row in the deferred table below, which implements signing and validation over both answer paths, so this matrix records RFC 4033 as `supported` for that profile. | `partial`: zone DNSSEC RRs are parsed from zone text (including RFC 3597 `\#` generic form) and served opaque as bounded Unknown RDATA; covering RRSIGs and NSEC denial proofs ride along only when the query sets DO=1 and are stripped otherwise; AD is set on the forwarding path only when this server validated every RRset (upstream AD is always cleared first); RRSIG validity windows map to DNSSEC-bogus EDE codes; NSEC3 hashing is pinned to the python hashlib oracle; and an aggressive NSEC cache synthesizes NODATA/NXDOMAIN., which is then stored in the negative cache with the scope it covers, so the next client for a name in that scope is answered without an upstream query (RFC 8020 §2) NSEC3 denial serving on the authoritative path: a zone whose text carries an NSEC3 chain (type 50 records plus the apex NSEC3PARAM) answers a denial with NSEC3 records and not with NSEC ones. A NODATA answer carries the NSEC3 whose owner hash matches the queried name, whose bitmap leaves the QTYPE and CNAME bits clear. An NXDOMAIN answer carries the closest encloser proof (the NSEC3 that matches the closest encloser and the NSEC3 that covers the next closer) plus the NSEC3 that covers the wildcard at the closest encloser, or the NSEC3 that matches that wildcard when the chain holds it (RFC 5155 section 7.2.2), which is a deviation from that section: it asks for the NSEC3 that covers the (nonexistent) wildcard, so the proof sent here shows the wildcard exists rather than that it does not. A validator that follows RFC 5155 section 8.4, which makes an NSEC3 that covers the wildcard a MUST to verify, finds no such record in that proof and answers SERVFAIL for the name error. A query for an NSEC3 owner name is answered as a name error: the owner of an NSEC3 record is a hash and names nothing in the zone (RFC 5155 section 7.2.8), so the zone index reads it as absent and the answer carries the name error proof of section 7.2.2. A wildcard NODATA carries the closest encloser proof plus the NSEC3 that matches the wildcard. A DS NODATA whose name the chain does not hold is answered with the closest provable encloser proof of RFC 5155 section 7.2.4: the NSEC3 that matches the closest provable encloser and the NSEC3 that covers the next closer name, which must carry the Opt-Out flag. A DS query for a name that does not exist is a name error and not a no-data answer, so it is answered with the name error proof of RFC 5155 section 7.2.2: the closest encloser proof and the NSEC3 that covers the wildcard at the closest encloser. A referral to a delegation the chain holds carries the NSEC3 that matches the delegation name, with the RRSIG that covers it and the apex NSEC3PARAM with the RRSIG that covers it (RFC 5155 section 7.2.7, first sentence; RFC 4035 section 5.4: every proof RRset must itself be authenticated); the record served is the one that proves the delegation unsigned, its bitmap has the NS bit set and the DS bit clear (RFC 5155 section 8.9), and the project's own validator with an anchor that covers the zone accepts the answer as insecure, not bogus; a record whose NS bit is clear proves nothing about the delegation and is not served, so the referral falls through to the Opt-Out closest provable encloser proof of section 7.2.7 — the project's own choice over the section's unconditional inclusion rule, because a validator judges a record with the NS bit clear to be bogus; a delegation whose NSEC3 carries the DS bit is a secure delegation, which section 7.2.7 forbids in that record, so such a referral keeps the answer the zone always gave. A referral to an unsigned subzone under a chain whose NSEC3 that covers the next closer name carries the Opt-Out flag carries the closest provable encloser proof of RFC 5155 section 7.2.7: the NSEC3 that matches the closest provable encloser of the delegation name and the NSEC3 that covers its next closer name. A chain that carries no Opt-Out flag leaves such a referral the way the zone always answered it. The proof is the zone's own NSEC3 records with the RRSIGs that cover them, never a reconstruction. Each proof record rides with the RRSIG that covers it, and every proof rides only when the query sets DO=1. The proof carries the apex NSEC3PARAM, which states the parameters the chain was built with, and the RRSIG that covers it; a zone that does not sign its NSEC3PARAM has none served. A zone with no NSEC3 chain keeps its NSEC proofs and the bytes it has always answered with, and a chain that cannot prove the denial, and an NSEC denial that attaches no proof at all, are answered with server failure instead of with a wrong or unsigned answer, and the last NSEC of a zone covers the names after it (RFC 4035 §5.4 wrap), but only when the query sets DO=1, because the same query without DO is still answered NXDOMAIN or NODATA. The run-time hash collision is the case RFC 5155 section 7.2.9 names; the wider refusals this server makes (a chain that cannot cover the name, and a DS NODATA whose next closer name is covered by an NSEC3 without the Opt-Out flag) are the project's own choice. | `partial` for serving only; signing, validation, and trust-anchor management are covered in their own rows below. The chain this row proves with is the one the zone signing row builds from an apex NSEC3PARAM, so the denial served here comes from that chain. The name error answer for a query for an NSEC3 owner name (RFC 5155 section 7.2.8) and the wildcard proof a name error carries when the chain holds that wildcard (RFC 5155 section 7.2.2) are served. The Opt-Out closest provable encloser proof for a DS NODATA (RFC 5155 section 7.2.4) is served from the zone's own NSEC3 records, so a zone whose text carries such a chain answers the DS query of an unsigned delegation with that proof instead of with server failure. The name error proof of RFC 5155 section 7.2.2 is served for a DS query for a name that does not exist, and the Opt-Out closest provable encloser proof of RFC 5155 section 7.2.7 is served for a referral to an unsigned subzone under an Opt-Out chain, so neither is refused with server failure any more; the project's own validator accepts both answers, and answers them insecure rather than secure when the chain they are proven over carries an iteration count above 0 (RFC 9276 section 3.2, the NSEC3 parameter guidance row below). Open in the NSEC3 denial serving named here: the Opt-Out flag in the signer, which refuses a NSEC3PARAM that asks for it (the signing row above), so a chain with the flag reaches the serving path only as a zone's own records; and the RFC 9276 parameter recommendation (iterations 0, empty salt), which binds the publisher of a chain — this server serves whatever parameters the zone publishes and does not enforce them. | [`src/dns/dnssec.zig`](../src/dns/dnssec.zig), [`src/dns/validate.zig`](../src/dns/validate.zig) (the shared NSEC3 RDATA decoder and the hash span test), output in [`docs/reports/dnssec-served-subset.log`](../docs/reports/dnssec-served-subset.log). |
| Authoritative zone signing (RRSIG, DNSKEY, DS, NSEC and NSEC3 generation) | [RFC 4034 §3.1.8.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4034#section-3.1.8.1) and [§5.1.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4034#section-5.1.4): RRSIG RDATA and DNSKEY RDATA; [RFC 4034 §3.1.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4034#section-3.1.5): the Signature Expiration and Inception fields, the validity period of a signature; [RFC 4034 Appendix B](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4034#appendix-B) key tag; [RFC 4035 §2.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4035#section-2.3): the DNSKEY and RRSIG RRsets a signed zone must carry; [RFC 4034 §6.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4034#section-6.1)–[§6.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4034#section-6.3) canonical RR form and order; [RFC 4034 §3.1.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4034#section-3.1.3) wildcard Labels; [RFC 5155 §7.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-7.1): the NSEC3 chain a signed zone publishes (one NSEC3 per owner name, the chain in hash order, and the type bitmaps); [RFC 5155 §6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-6) and [§3.1.2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-3.1.2.1): the Opt-Out flag of an NSEC3 record; [RFC 5155 §7.2.7](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-7.2.7): the closest provable encloser proof of a referral to an unsigned subzone; [RFC 5155 §4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-4) and [§4.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-4.3): the NSEC3PARAM record and its presentation form (the salt as hexadecimal digits, "-" when it is empty); [RFC 5155 §7.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-7.3): the parameters of the chain are indicated by an NSEC3PARAM RR present at the zone apex; [RFC 5155 §10.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5155#section-10.5): the RFC's own procedure for a zone that goes back to NSEC removes the NSEC3PARAM RRset; [RFC 6781 §4.1.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6781#section-4.1.2): the Double-Signature key-signing key rollover, whose Figure 4 stages are the four configuration states the operator drives — an Informational document whose procedure is prescribed practice and not a Standards Track requirement; and [RFC 9276 §3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9276#section-3.1): the parameter recommendation for a published chain (iterations 0, empty salt) — deferred by this row and named as open below. The choice between the two chains is the project's own operator policy, not an RFC rule: RFC 4034 and RFC 5155 say how a chain is built once a zone publishes one, and neither says how an operator turns the NSEC3 chain off again. | `partial`: `dnssec-key-file <path>` signs every configured zone once at startup — apex DNSKEY and DS, one NSEC per authoritative owner in canonical order, one RRSIG per RRset — and the records are served from memory with no zone-file rewrite and no SOA serial change. A zone may name a second key with `dnssec-ksk-file <path>`: RFC 6781 section 3.1 then separates the roles. The zone publishes two DNSKEYs -- the key-signing key with the Zone Key flag and the Secure Entry Point flag (257), and the zone-signing key with the Zone Key flag alone (256) -- the one DS the zone publishes states the key-signing key's key tag and digest, the DNSKEY RRset is signed by the key-signing key, and every other RRset, the SOA and the denial records included, is signed by the zone-signing key. A zone that names no key-signing key is the single-type signing scheme: one DNSKEY with flags 257, one DS that states it, and that key signing every RRset, byte for byte as before for a zone whose RDATA names are already in the canonical lower case (RFC 4034 section 6.2 with RFC 6840 section 5.1). One key per start otherwise, a 30-day validity window that a reload refreshes, a zone that already carries DNSSEC records is refused rather than signed twice, and a delegation keeps NS and DS while dropping the child subtree. A zone that is reloaded is signed again: the reload rebuilds the whole chain -- the apex DNSKEY and DS, the NSEC or NSEC3 records, and the RRSIGs -- over the new content, so the signatures and the denial records the server serves are the ones of the new zone and not the ones of the old. The key does not move: RFC 4034 section 5.1.4 makes the DS a digest of the DNSKEY it names and section 5.1.1 its key tag, so a validator that holds the old DS still validates the reloaded zone. The chain the reloaded zone publishes is the chain the served zone published, with the NSEC3 parameters its own apex NSEC3PARAM states (RFC 5155 section 4.1), so a reload never swaps one chain for the other. A reload that cannot sign -- a zone file that does not parse, one that carries no RFC 1982 newer serial, one that already carries DNSSEC records, or one whose chain contradicts the served one -- leaves the served zone and its signatures in place, and a zone that was not signed is not signed by a reload. The signatures a reload makes carry a window that starts at the reload: the validity period the server was configured with, counted from the reload (RFC 4034 section 3.1.5), so a zone that has been running for weeks is signed with a window that has weeks left. A zone that is not reloaded keeps the window it was signed with, so its signatures still end when that window ends. The chain a signed zone publishes is the operator's choice: `dnssec-chain nsec` publishes the RFC 4034 chain, `dnssec-chain nsec3 <iterations> <salt>` publishes the RFC 5155 chain, and without the directive the zone's own apex NSEC3PARAM is the opt-in, as it always was. The NSEC chain is one NSEC per authoritative owner in canonical order, each naming the next owner with the last wrapping to the first. The NSEC3 chain is one NSEC3 per owner name -- every authoritative owner and every empty non-terminal (RFC 5155 section 7.1) -- in hash order, each naming the next hash with the last wrapping to the first, and every one of them is signed with the zone key. Its parameters are the zone's own apex NSEC3PARAM when the zone states one (that record states the parameters of the chain, RFC 5155 section 4.1) and the parameters of the directive otherwise, in which case the signer publishes the apex NSEC3PARAM that states them (RFC 5155 section 7.3); a zone that states an NSEC3PARAM and a configuration that names other parameters are refused rather than served as two chains. The type bitmap of an NSEC3 names the types present at the original owner name, the RRSIG bit, and, at the apex, the NSEC3PARAM bit; per RFC 5155 section 7.1 it never names the NSEC3 type itself. Only SHA-1 is implemented: a NSEC3PARAM that asks for another hash algorithm is refused rather than published with parameters this build cannot honour. The Opt-Out flag of the zone's own apex NSEC3PARAM is the opt-in for an Opt-Out chain (RFC 5155 section 3.1.2.1): the owner name of an unsigned delegation is left out of the chain (RFC 5155 section 7.1), every record of that chain carries the Opt-Out bit, and so the record that covers the next closer name of the delegation carries it too, which is what a referral to that delegation needs (RFC 5155 section 7.2.7). A chain that is not Opt-Out is unchanged: every owner name keeps its NSEC3 and no record of it carries the flag. A zone that already carries a hand-written NSEC3 chain is still refused, because it already carries DNSSEC records. The zone's own apex NSEC3PARAM record stays published in the NSEC3 mode, because it states that chain's parameters; in the NSEC mode it is dropped from the served zone before the zone is signed, because RFC 5155 section 7.3 makes that record the statement of the chain the zone publishes: a zone that kept it while publishing the NSEC chain would tell a validator to look for NSEC3 records it does not carry, and RFC 5155 section 10.5 removes the NSEC3PARAM RRset when a zone goes back to NSEC. The dropped record is not signed either. A zone that carries an apex ZONEMD record keeps a digest that covers the record the served zone no longer carries, so the startup digest check refuses that zone until the operator removes the NSEC3PARAM from the zone file or names the NSEC3 chain. The owner name of an NSEC3 is the hash in unpadded base32hex written in the canonical lowercase form of RFC 4034 section 6.2, so the signature the zone makes over the record verifies. Not implemented in this signing path (validation and trust anchors live in the validation rows): the parent side and the timing of the RFC 6781 section 4.1.2 key-signing key rollover and Ed448. A wildcard-expanded answer now also carries its covering RRSIG under the expanded answer owner, with the wildcard's label count (RFC 4034 section 3.1.3; RFC 4035 section 5.3.2), so the response validates. | `partial`: the key-signing key separation of RFC 6781 section 3.1 is implemented and operator-reachable (`dnssec-ksk-file`), and the child side of the RFC 6781 section 4.1.2 Double-Signature key-signing key rollover is implemented and operator-reachable beside it (`dnssec-new-ksk-file`): the zone publishes the zone-signing key with the Zone Key flag alone (256) and both key-signing keys with the Zone Key flag and the Secure Entry Point flag (257), signs the apex DNSKEY RRset once with each key-signing key, and publishes exactly one DS, for the key `dnssec-ksk-file` names, so the parent swaps that DS and the server changes nothing else. RFC 6781 is Informational, so this is prescribed practice and not a Standards Track requirement. What stays open in it is the parent side of the rollover — the DS edit in the parent zone and the wait of at least TTL_DS — which is the operator's duty, and the RFC 5011-style revoke phase of section 4.1.2.1 is implemented on the child side: the key to be removed is published with the RFC 5011 section 3 REVOKE bit set (0x0080 of the DNSKEY flags, so the published DNSKEY carries 385 rather than 257) and still signs the DNSKEY RRset that carries it, which is the self-signature the section asks for, while the one DS the zone publishes keeps stating the key `dnssec-ksk-file` names — a DNSKEY with the REVOKE bit has a different fingerprint and so no longer matches a DS that names it (RFC 5011 section 2.1). Also open: RFC 5011's automated trust-anchor rollover, which is a decision rather than a gap because the operator's anchor file is the whole trust configuration, and the two RFC 9904 deviations: RSA/SHA-1 (5 and 7) validation, an "Implement for DNSSEC Validation" MUST this build does not meet, and Ed448 (16) validation, RECOMMENDED and absent. RSA/SHA-1 is not offered for signing, which the registry's "Implement for DNSSEC Signing" column marks NOT RECOMMENDED, and RSA/SHA-2 is implemented (the RFC 5702 row above). Both halves of the RFC 9276 section 3.1 parameter recommendation are enforced: the iterations count of 0 that the section makes a MUST is enforced, and a chain whose parameters ask for more than 0 is refused rather than published, and the recommendation that an operator should not use a salt is enforced as well, because the signer refuses a salted chain — the `dnssec-chain` directive with a salt is refused at parse time and a zone whose own apex NSEC3PARAM states a salt is refused at signing. The Opt-Out flag is read from the zone's own apex NSEC3PARAM, and RFC 5155 section 4.1.2 leaves the flags of that record at zero and RFC 9276 section 2.2 calls them reserved, so the opt-in is a convention of this project rather than an RFC field; the flag on the NSEC3 records themselves is the one RFC 5155 section 3.1.2.1 defines. The chain is rebuilt on every reload, so a zone that changes afterwards is signed again. What stays open on the reload path: the key file is read at startup only, so a key the operator replaces takes effect at the next start and not at a reload; and a reload that would change the parameters of the NSEC3 chain is refused rather than served, because a chain change is a procedure of its own that this build does not have. | [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig) (`signZone`, `resignZone`, which signs a zone again with the chain the served zone publishes, `Options.fromNow`, which counts the window from the moment of the signing, `selectChain`, `apexNsec3Param`, `nsec3paramRdata`, and `appendNsec3Chain`, which builds the chain the apex NSEC3PARAM describes), [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) (the reload that signs the zone again), [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) (`dnssec-key-file`, `dnssec-ksk-file`, `dnssec-new-ksk-file`, and `dnssec-chain`), [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig), [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig) (DO bit and section output), [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig) (`--dnssec`), [`examples/dnssec/config.gdns`](../examples/dnssec/config.gdns) | Directive parsing and its rejection of a duplicate, missing, or trailing-token value in [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig), which also takes the `dnssec-chain` directive and refuses every other value, parameter, and repeat; startup signing, apex DNSKEY/DS, one NSEC per authoritative owner, covering RRSIGs for SOA, DNSKEY, A, and the wildcard owner, the chain the configuration names, and the refusal of a configuration whose NSEC3 parameters the zone's apex NSEC3PARAM contradicts, in [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig), which runs on `std.testing.allocator` so a generated record that outlives its zone fails the test; the key-signing key separation tests (two DNSKEYs, the flags each carries, the DS that states the key-signing key, the signature each key makes, and the DNSKEY RRset and the zone's RRsets the project's own validator accepts), the Double-Signature key-signing key rollover test — the three published DNSKEYs, the two signatures over the DNSKEY RRset, the one DS, and the project's own validator accepting the zone — in [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig) ("RFC 6781 section 4.1.2: a double-signature KSK rollover publishes both key-signing keys and signs the DNSKEY RRset with both"), the startup test that a zone with a second key-signing key publishes both DNSKEYs at the apex and the one test per refusal — no zone-signing key, no key-signing key for the DS to state, the same key twice, and a missing or unusable key file — in [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig), and the directive's shape in [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) ("config parser accepts one DNSSEC new key-signing key-file path and no other shape", and "config parser marks the second key-signing key as revoked with one word"), with the procedure itself in [`src/zone/README.md`](../src/zone/README.md); the RFC 6781 section 4.1.2.1 revoke phase is proved by the test that the revoked key-signing key is published with the REVOKE bit and still signs the DNSKEY RRset that carries it, in [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig) ("RFC 6781 section 4.1.2.1: a revoked key-signing key is published with the REVOKE bit and still signs the DNSKEY RRset"), by the startup test that a revoked second key-signing key is published with that bit and the one that refuses a word the directive does not know, in [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig), and by the parser test named above; and the RFC 8080 §6.1, RFC 6605 §6.1, and RFC 6605 §6.2 vectors, the Appendix B key tag, the NSEC chain walk, delegation, already-signed refusal, and the wildcard-label test in [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig), which also carries the NSEC3 chain tests (the chain's shape, order, and next-hash link; the oracle hashes; the signatures the project's own validator accepts; the unchanged NSEC chain of a zone without an NSEC3PARAM; the Opt-Out chain of a zone with an unsigned delegation, the unchanged chain of the same zone without the flag, and the referral the project's own validator accepts, in [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig); and the two chain-choice tests, where `dnssec-chain nsec` publishes the NSEC chain in a zone that states an apex NSEC3PARAM and drops that record, and `dnssec-chain nsec3` publishes the NSEC3 chain, with the apex NSEC3PARAM that states its parameters, in a zone that states none; the chain a zone that is signed again keeps, in [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig) ("a zone that is signed again keeps the chain the served zone publishes"); the reload that signs a zone again, the key tag and the DS that do not move, the window a reload refreshes, the window a zone that is not reloaded keeps, the denial records of the new content, the failed reload that keeps the served signed zone, and the zone a reload does not sign, in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) ("a signed zone that is reloaded is signed again with the same key", "a reload signs the zone with a window that starts now", "a zone that is not reloaded keeps the window it was signed with", "a reload that fails leaves the served signed zone in place", and "a zone that was not signed is not signed by a reload"); the DO-bit and authority-section rendering tests in [`src/cli/query.zig`](../src/cli/query.zig); and an end-to-end config → sign → serve → query transcript over UDP in [`examples/dnssec/config.gdns`](../examples/dnssec/config.gdns). |


| Use of SHA-256 in DS records | [RFC 4509 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4509#section-2) and [§2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4509#section-2.1): digest type 2, the untruncated 32-byte `SHA_256(DNSKEY owner name \| DNSKEY RDATA)` digest, and the [§2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4509#section-2.2) wire format; [§3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4509#section-3) implementation requirement. | `supported`: a SHA-256 DS digest (digest type 2) is generated for the apex DNSKEY of every zone signed with Ed25519 or ECDSA P-256 and served; a zone signed with ECDSA P-384 gets the SHA-384 digest (type 4) that [RFC 6605 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6605#section-2) requires, so the type-2 digest is not generated for that key. The validator consumes DS anchors (`Anchors.parse`, DS-then-DNSKEY chain test). Excluded from the profile: the §2.3 example DNSKEY is an RSA key (IANA algorithm 5) that this build cannot hold — RSA is out of scope in this profile — so that published vector is not reproduced here, and the [§4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4509#section-4) dual-digest deployment advice and the [§6.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4509#section-6.1) downgrade defence are validator obligations that stay out of scope. | `supported`: the digest type 2 record is generated and served for every zone signed with Ed25519 or ECDSA P-256, the validator consumes a DS anchor, and the SHA-256 digests are checked against the published values in RFC 6605 §6.1 (P-256) and RFC 8080 §6.1 (Ed25519). The RFC's own §2.3 example key, which is an RSA key this build cannot hold, and the validator obligations of §4 and §6.1 stay out of the first release. | [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig) (`Key.dsRdata`, `DigestType`), [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig) | The SHA-256 DS digests are checked against the published values in RFC 6605 §6.1 (P-256) and RFC 8080 §6.1 (Ed25519), each independently confirmed with dnspython 2.8.0, and the served apex DS is exercised end to end in [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig) and by the [`examples/dnssec/config.gdns`](../examples/dnssec/config.gdns) transcript. |
| ECDSA for DNSSEC | [RFC 6605 §4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6605#section-4): the DNSKEY public key as the uncompressed curve point `x \| y` with no SEC 1 `0x04` octet, and the RRSIG signature as `r \| s` at 32 octets per integer for P-256 and 48 for P-384; [§2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6605#section-2): SHA-384 DS records with digest type 4; [§7](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6605#section-7): algorithm numbers 13 and 14. | `supported`: algorithm 13 (P-256 with SHA-256) and algorithm 14 (P-384 with SHA-384) are signed, including the DS digest the §2 addition requires for P-384. Signing and verification are implemented for both algorithms (`verifyEcdsa` in `src/dns/validate.zig`); no other RFC 6605 obligation (the §5 NSEC3 obligation is a validator duty) is claimed beyond what `src/dns/validate.zig` proves. The signatures printed in RFC 6605 §6.1 and §6.2 are not reproduced: each was checked against its own published DNSKEY over its own published RRset and neither verifies, so the published example is internally inconsistent — a defect in the printed vector rather than in this implementation — and this row is pinned to the RFC's DNSKEY encodings, key tags, and DS digests, which are reproduced byte for byte. Excluded from the profile: the §5 NSEC3 obligation, which is a validator duty. | `supported`: both algorithms are signed and verified, the §2 digest type 4 is produced for P-384, and the RFC 6605 §6.1 and §6.2 DNSKEY encodings, key tags, and DS digests are reproduced byte for byte. The RFC's own printed signatures are not reproduced, because they do not verify against the RFC's own published DNSKEY and RRset. | [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig) (`Algorithm`, `Key.dnskeyRdata`, `Key.dsRdata`, `DigestType`), [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig) | The RFC 6605 §6.1 and §6.2 DNSKEY encodings, key tags (55648, 10771), and SHA-256/SHA-384 DS digests are asserted byte for byte in [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig), each independently confirmed with dnspython 2.8.0, and a P-256 signature over a fixed RRset verifies against the key its DNSKEY publishes, with a tampered preimage rejected. |
| EdDSA for DNSSEC | [RFC 8080 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8080#section-3): the 32-octet Ed25519 public key as a simple bit string; [§4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8080#section-4): the 64-octet Ed25519 signature as a simple bit string; [§5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8080#section-5) and [§7](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8080#section-7): algorithm number 15 for Ed25519 and 16 for Ed448; [§8](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8080#section-8): "A private key used for a DNSSEC zone MUST NOT be used for any other purpose than for that zone." [RFC 4034 §2.1.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4034#section-2.1.1): the Zone Key flag (bit 7) that marks a key which signs a zone, and the Secure Entry Point flag (bit 15); [RFC 6781 §3.2.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6781#section-3.2.3): the Secure Entry Point flag on the keys of a single-type signing scheme. | `partial`: algorithm 15 (Ed25519) only. Ed448, algorithm 16, is **absent**, not partial — the standard library has no Ed448 primitive and adding one is out of scope, so a key file naming it is refused rather than served wrongly. Signing and verification for Ed25519 (`verifyEd25519` in `src/dns/validate.zig`); the §8 key separation is enforced: the DNSKEY the signer emits carries the Zone Key flag (RFC 4034 §2.1.1) and the Secure Entry Point flag with it, the pair RFC 8080 §6.1 publishes and the pair RFC 6781 §3.2.3 asks a single-type signing scheme to publish, and a key that has already signed a zone is refused for a second zone at startup (`Error.KeyUsedForAnotherZone`), so one key file cannot sign two zones. The signature printed in RFC 8080 §6.1 is not used — it was checked against the DNSKEY the same section publishes over the RRset the same section publishes and does not verify; the section also prints `RRSIG MX 3 3600`, which omits the Algorithm field RFC 4034 §3.2 requires, and Labels 3 for `example.com.`, which RFC 4034 §3.1.3 puts at 2. | `partial`: the Ed25519 subset — algorithm 15 signed and verified with the §3 and §4 wire formats, and the §6.1 DNSKEY, key tag, and DS digest reproduced. The §8 key-separation rule is enforced: one key signs one zone, and a second zone configured with the same key file is refused at startup. Ed448 (algorithm 16) stays out of the first release. | [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig) (`Algorithm.ed25519`, `Key.dnskeyRdata`, `Key.sign`, `Key.signed_zone`), [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) | The RFC 8080 §6.1 Ed25519 DNSKEY, key tag 35217, and SHA-256 DS digest are reproduced, and the whole signed zone — including the RRSIG bytes produced by dnspython 2.8.0 with `cryptography` — matches byte for byte in [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig). The wildcard RRSIG is pinned to bytes dnspython 2.8.0 accepted over `*.home.arpa. IN A 192.168.1.99`, rejecting a tampered RRset and a DNSKEY at the wrong owner. The key-file parser refuses any algorithm but the three implemented ones in [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig). The Zone Key flag on the emitted DNSKEY, the refusal of a second zone for a key that has signed one, and the unchanged signing of a single zone are asserted in [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig) ("RFC 4034 section 2.1.1: the DNSKEY the signer emits carries the Zone Key flag", "RFC 8080 section 8: a key that has signed one zone is refused for a second one", and "RFC 8080 section 8: one key signs one zone, and that zone is signed as before"). |
| Encrypted transports (DoT/DoH, DoQ deferred) | [RFC 7858](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7858): DNS over TLS port 853 and framing, with §3.1 naming no ALPN token and the `dot` token defined by the [IANA ALPN protocol ID registry](https://www.iana.org/assignments/tls-extensiontype-values#alpn-protocol-ids) entry referenced to that RFC; [RFC 8484](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8484): DNS over HTTPS GET/POST mapping, a document with no ALPN text, so the DoH token follows the implemented HTTP/1.1 profile; [RFC 9103](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9103): strict-TLS transfer channel (see transfer row), whose §7.1 requires the `dot` token and §7.2 requires TLS 1.3 or later, both scoped to transfers rather than general DoT; [RFC 9250](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9250): DNS over QUIC, deferred with a typed error. | `partial`: RFC 7858 DoT is user-facing. `--serve-dot` terminates a TLS 1.3 handshake with the `dot` ALPN token and then speaks the RFC 7858 section 3.3 two-octet length-prefixed framing over that channel, so the port never answers a cleartext DNS frame: the handshake comes first, a client that offers no common ALPN token is refused with the RFC 7301 no_application_protocol alert, and the operator's certificate and key are loaded before the port is bound, so a missing, unreadable, or non-PEM identity refuses the run and leaves no port open. The resolver side of RFC 7858 is implemented too: `forwarder <address>:853 tls <name>` sends every upstream query over DNS over TLS (`src/transport/tls_client.zig`), offering the `dot` ALPN token (RFC 8310 section 8.1), accepting a server that negotiates no token once its certificate verifies, refusing one that selects a different token, verifying the certificate against the platform trust store for the operator's configured name, keeping the DS and DNSKEY chain fetches that validation needs on the same encrypted channel, and never falling back to a cleartext exchange. The DoH client is implemented on the same terms: `forwarder <address>:443 doh <name> <path>` sends one RFC 8484 section 4.1 POST per query with `application/dns-message`, requires that media type on the answer, decodes a content-length or chunked body, keeps chain fetches on the same channel, and speaks HTTP/1.1 only, so a provider whose endpoint requires HTTP/2 (Quad9's does) fails closed with a non-200 status instead of a wrong answer. The DoT listener carries the same bounds as the cleartext TCP listener: at most 16 frames per connection, the operator's per-source connection cap (RFC 9210 section 4.2), the operator's connection-duration limit, a worker bound, a per-frame deadline, and the same message-size bound. It also honors the SIGHUP zone reload the cleartext paths honor, so a reload reaches the DoT port and the answer that follows it comes from the new zone. The TLS handshake runs under one absolute deadline, the connection's when the operator configured one and the module's own window otherwise, so a peer that dribbles handshake bytes is dropped inside that window instead of holding a worker slot. The port answers through the same runtime entry point the cleartext TCP port uses, so the peer address reaches the recursion ACL, the DNS cookie check, and the local-zone table of RFC 6303, RFC 6761, RFC 7793, and RFC 8375, and one query is answered the same way whichever port it arrives on. The frame deadline is enforced over the raw descriptor under a poll deadline rather than a socket timeout, because a timed-out read through the standard library's socket reader surfaces as EAGAIN, which that library treats as a fatal error. Requiring the `dot` token is stricter than RFC 7858 needs, so a client that offers no ALPN extension at all is refused; that is a deliberate policy, and it is named here because it is an interoperability limit. The DoT port this build binds by default is 1053, not the IANA-registered 853; an operator reaches 853 with a `listen dns dot <address>:853` line. RFC 8484 DoH is served over the same TLS server: `--serve-doh` terminates a TLS 1.3 handshake with the `http/1.1` ALPN token, then maps the RFC 8484 section 4.1 GET and POST requests to DNS wire bytes and answers with `application/dns-message`; the base64url `dns` parameter carries the query without padding, the response freshness lifetime is the smallest TTL in the answer section or, with no answer records, the SOA's MINIMUM field (RFC 8484 section 5.1), and the port never answers in cleartext. The DoH channel holds the same bounds as the DoT port: one absolute deadline per exchange on the raw descriptor under the TLS connection, the operator's connection-duration limit over the whole connection, and a bounded exchange count per connection, so a peer that stalls in the middle of an exchange loses that exchange and not the server. The port answers through the same runtime entry point the cleartext TCP port uses, so the peer address reaches the recursion ACL, the DNS cookie check, and the local-zone table, and one query is answered the same way whichever port it arrives on. The request mapping refuses what RFC 9112 section 6.1 says to refuse: a request carrying both a transfer coding and a content length is answered 400, because the two disagree about the body, and two different content lengths in one request are answered 400 for the same reason; a head over the 8192-octet budget is answered 431, a body over the 65535-octet budget is answered 413, and a POST whose media type is not `application/dns-message` is answered 415; the last exchange of a connection carries `connection: close` so the client does not reuse a connection the server is about to close. RFC 8484 section 5.2 makes HTTP/2 the minimum RECOMMENDED version of HTTP for DoH, so an HTTP/1.1-only server breaks no MUST of that document, but the negotiated token is `http/1.1` and not `h2`, so a client that requires HTTP/2 cannot use this port. The DoH port this build binds by default is 1053, not the HTTPS default 443; an operator reaches 443 with a `listen dns doh <address>:443` line. The RFC 9103 strict-TLS transfer channel is served by `--serve-xfr` over the same TLS server, with the `dot` token and TLS 1.3 as sections 7.1 and 7.2 require, and the port this build binds by default is 1053 rather than the 853 section 7.3 recommends. DoQ (RFC 9250) has no listener, no command-line flag, and no name in the configuration: it stays deferred because it needs a QUIC stack, not TCP plus TLS. | `supported` for DoT: the RFC 7858 framing, the `dot` ALPN token, the TLS 1.3 handshake, and the fail-closed startup are implemented and tested. `supported` for RFC 8484's requirements, with the HTTP/1.1-only deviation stated above; the head itself is held to its HTTP documents: an HTTP/1.1 request without a `Host` header is refused with 400 (RFC 9112 §3.2), a 405 carries `Allow: GET, POST` (RFC 9110 §15.5.6), and a `100-continue` expectation this channel does not honor is answered with a final 417 (RFC 9110 §10.1.1). DoQ stays deferred; HTTP/3 and ODoH stay out of scope. | [`src/transport/tls.zig`](../src/transport/tls.zig), [`src/transport/tls_server.zig`](../src/transport/tls_server.zig), [`src/transport/dot.zig`](../src/transport/dot.zig), [`src/transport/doh.zig`](../src/transport/doh.zig), [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig), [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig), the encrypted-transport decision (`src/transport/README.md`), [`docs/../src/transport/README.md`](../src/transport/README.md) | ALPN, opt-in gating, identity validation, per-transport startup gating, and the ALPN-token test in [`src/transport/tls.zig`](../src/transport/tls.zig); DoT framing, client-policy, padding, handshake-window, and metrics tests in [`src/transport/dot.zig`](../src/transport/dot.zig) ("a DoT query for a locally served name answers the same way with and without the counters", "a DoT query the recursion ACL refuses is refused over DoT too", "a DoT peer that dribbles handshake bytes is dropped inside the handshake window", "DoT channel rejects a half-written length prefix", and "RFC 7830: the DoT answer carries no padding when the query did not ask for it"); DoH GET, POST, rejection, and keep-alive tests plus the real-handshake, bound-port, ALPN-refusal, and cleartext-refusal tests in [`src/transport/doh.zig`](../src/transport/doh.zig) ("a DoH query for a locally served name is answered from the local zone table", "a DoH query the recursion ACL refuses is refused over DoH too", and "the DoH answer is the same with and without the counters") ("DoH answers a GET and a POST over a real TLS 1.3 handshake with the http/1.1 ALPN token", "the DoH listener answers a query over the bound port", "DoH refuses a client that offers another ALPN token", "the DoH port never answers a cleartext HTTP request"); the startup-refusal and unbound-port test in [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig). The DoT claim rests on behavior, not on shape: "DoT answers a query over a real TLS 1.3 handshake with the dot ALPN token" completes a TLS 1.3 handshake with the pinned `tls.zig` server, negotiates the `dot` token, writes a DNS query in the RFC 7858 section 3.3 framing, and asserts the answer's QR bit, query ID, and answer count; "the DoT listener answers a query over the bound port" repeats that over the listener the operator starts, with the accept loop, the per-source cap, and the worker bound in place; "DoT refuses a client that offers another ALPN token" asserts the RFC 7301 no_application_protocol alert; "DoT refuses to start without a loadable identity and binds no port" asserts the fail-closed startup. The pre-change run is the dated snapshot [`docs/reports/encrypted-transport-7858-8484-9103-9250.log`](../docs/reports/encrypted-transport-7858-8484-9103-9250.log); `kdig`/`curl --doh-url` loopback interop is deferred to [`test/interop/README.md`](../test/interop/README.md). |
| Locally served DNS zones (RFC 6303 / 6761 / 7793 / 8375) | [RFC 6303 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6303#section-3) (BCP 163): an implementation returns authoritatively (AA=1) name errors for queries inside the [§4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6303#section-4) zones, returns SOA, NS, and "no data" answers at the zone name itself, defaults MNAME to the zone name and RNAME to `nobody.invalid.`, sets the record TTL and the SOA MINIMUM to the same negative TTL, emits no address records for the nameserver, and MUST provide a mechanism to disable the behaviour and SHOULD allow it zone by zone; [§4.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6303#section-4.1)–[§4.6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6303#section-4.6) name the zones and [§2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6303#section-2) covers sites that populate those zones themselves. [RFC 6598 §4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6598#section-4): "Reverse DNS queries for Shared Address Space addresses MUST NOT be forwarded to the global DNS infrastructure." [RFC 7793 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7793#section-2.1) (BCP 163) adds the 64 reverse zones of 100.64.0.0/10 to the same registry. [RFC 6761 §6.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6761#section-6.1): authoritative servers (item 5 of each reservation item) generate immediate local answers for the reserved names — [§6.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6761#section-6.2) `test.` returns an immediate negative response, [§6.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6761#section-6.3) `localhost.` returns the IP loopback address for address queries and a negative response for every other type, [§6.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6761#section-6.4) `invalid.` returns NXDOMAIN, and [§6.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6761#section-6.5) `example.` names are resolved normally. [RFC 8375 §4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8375#section-4) item B (Standards Track) makes `home.arpa.` and its subdomains behave as RFC 6303 §3 describes, with one carve-out: a DS question carrying DO must be forwarded so the delegation's own denial can be proved. | `supported`: the whole registered zone set is answered locally and is **on by default with no flag**, so the RFC 6598 §4 and RFC 7793 forwarding MUSTs hold in the default configuration. The zones are all 17 [RFC 6303 §4.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6303#section-4.1) RFC 1918 reverse zones, all seven [§4.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6303#section-4.2) RFC 5735/5737 zones, both 32-nibble [§4.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6303#section-4.3) IPv6 unspecified/loopback zones, [§4.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6303#section-4.4) `d.f.ip6.arpa.`, the four [§4.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6303#section-4.5) link-local zones, [§4.6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6303#section-4.6) `8.b.d.0.1.0.0.2.ip6.arpa.`, all 64 [RFC 7793 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7793#section-2.1) zones, `home.arpa.`, and RFC 6761 `test.`, `invalid.`, and `localhost.`. Answers are the RFC 6303 §3 example zone verbatim — `SOA <zone> nobody.invalid. 1 3600 1200 604800 10800`, `NS <zone>`, no glue, TTL and SOA MINIMUM both 10800 — with AA=1 NXDOMAIN below the apex, SOA/NS/NODATA at the apex, NXDOMAIN everywhere for `test.` and `invalid.`, and the loopback address for `localhost.` and names below it. An operator's own loaded primary zone always wins, which is RFC 6303 §2's "configured otherwise"; the table is consulted after it and before the blocklist, the cache, and the forwarding policy, so a configured forwarder never sees these names. `local-zones on` and `local-zones off` are the RFC 6303 §3 disable mechanism and are proved end to end over loopback UDP: two `zimdns server run` instances share one configuration except that directive, and the same question is answered locally on the default instance and by the forwarder on the other. The RFC 6303 §3 zone-by-zone override is implemented as the repeatable `local-zones except <apex>` directive, which drops one locally served apex from the table on a case-insensitive match (canonicalized to lowercase with trailing dot in the loader), and is proved end to end over loopback UDP. Deliberately out of scope: the RFC 8375 item B DS+DO carve-out only produces an answer when a forwarder is configured, because there is nothing to forward to otherwise; class IN only, so a non-IN question keeps the existing out-of-zone behaviour; AXFR/IXFR against these zones is refused rather than answered; TSIG-signed questions keep the ordinary authoritative path; and the RFC 6303 §5 out-of-scope names (site-local `fec0::/10`, `IP6.INT`, root-level `.local` and `.workgroup`) are absent by design, as are the RFC 6761 §6.5 `example.` names, which must resolve normally. | `supported`: the same named subset, plus a per-apex override via the `local-zones except <apex>` directive (case-insensitive apex match in `src/auth/runtime.zig`, canonicalized in `src/config/loader.zig`); evidence: `src/config/parser.zig`, `test/config/config_test.zig`, and the loopback UDP socket test in `test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`. | [`src/local_zone.zig`](../src/local_zone.zig), [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig), [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig), [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig), [`examples/local-zones.gdns`](../examples/local-zones.gdns) | Zone enumeration, label-boundary matching, the RFC 6761 treatments, the RFC 8375 DS carve-out, and wire round-trips of every answer shape in [`src/local_zone.zig`](../src/local_zone.zig); `local-zones` and `local-zones except` parsing and their failure modes in [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig); default-on, `local-zones off` and the `local-zones except` apexes reaching `Runtime`, and the shipped example configuration loading through the real loader with its zone and forwarder in [`test/config/config_test.zig`](../test/config/config_test.zig); a live loopback UDP upstream proving the local zone beats both a configured forwarder and a warm cache while the upstream's own request counter proves nothing was forwarded, plus a UDP dispatch test proving the zone answers with no resolver at all and a loopback UDP socket test proving an excepted apex falls through to REFUSED while a non-excepted apex is still answered locally, in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig). |

| Dynamic update (RFC 2136) | [RFC 2136 §3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2136#section-3.1) (the zone section: exactly one RR, ZTYPE SOA, else FORMERR; a zone the server does not serve, NOTAUTH), [§3.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2136#section-3.2) (the prerequisite section: TTL 0, NOTZONE, the CLASS ANY/NONE/zclass tests, and the rcodes NXDOMAIN, NXRRSET, YXDOMAIN, YXRRSET), [§3.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2136#section-3.4) (the update section: the prescan, add-or-replace with the CNAME and SOA rules, and the delete operations with the apex SOA/NS protection), [§3.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2136#section-3.5) (the change is committed to nonvolatile storage before the response), and [§3.8](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2136#section-3.8) (the response echoes the zone section). RFC 2136 is a Standards Track document. | `partial`: the UPDATE opcode is operator-reachable behind `allow-update <apex> <tsig-key-name>`. A request for a zone with no policy is NOTIMP and its body is not inspected; with a policy, the zone section is checked first (§3.1.1), then the request must carry a valid TSIG made with the named key (unsigned or otherwise-signed requests are REFUSED, §3.3.1 leaves permission to the implementation and this profile requires the channel). The prerequisite section follows §3.2.5 exactly: TTL 0, NOTZONE for a name outside the zone, the CLASS ANY (name in use, RRset exists), CLASS NONE (name not in use, RRset does not exist) and zclass (value-dependent RRset set equality) cases, with NXDOMAIN, NXRRSET, YXDOMAIN, YXRRSET and FORMERR as the RFC names them. The update section follows §3.4.1.3's prescan (NOTZONE, FORMERR for a metatype, for RDATA on a CLASS ANY record, and for a non-zero TTL) and §3.4.2.7's in-order application: an add replaces an equal RDATA (CNAME and non-CNAME data never share a name; an SOA is taken only with an RFC 1982 greater serial), a CLASS ANY record deletes an RRset or every RRset at a name with the apex SOA and NS protected, and a CLASS NONE record deletes one matching RR with the SOA and the last apex NS protected. The zone is rebuilt from the resulting records, its SOA serial rises by one when the update carried no newer SOA, it is signed again when it was signed, and the serial transition is journaled through the same exclusive-lock install path a reload uses. §3.5 is met by rewriting the zone file through a temporary file, flushing it to stable storage, and renaming it before the response; a write failure answers SERVFAIL and leaves the served zone unchanged. A CLASS IN add matches a CNAME by type, so a name never holds two CNAMEs (RFC 2181 §10.1), and the query, transfer, and channel types (ANY, AXFR, IXFR, MAILB, MAILA, TSIG, OPT) are FORMERR in an update (RFC 2136 §3.4.1.2). The rebuilt zone text renders every owner octet that is presentation syntax escaped per RFC 1035 §5.1, so an update owner cannot break the zone line it is written into. The response (§3.8) echoes the zone section and is TSIG-signed when the request was. | `partial`: the primary-master half of RFC 2136, over the configured zone files: `allow-update` names one TSIG key per zone, the request is authenticated by that key, every prerequisite and update operation of §3.2 and §3.4 is applied atomically, the change is persisted before the response, and the reply carries the RFC rcode and the request's TSIG. What stays open: the secondary role of §3.1.1 (a slave forwarding the update toward its primary), outbound NOTIFY of the change, update forwarding, and any SOA serial policy beyond a single increment. | [`src/zone/update.zig`](../src/zone/update.zig), [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig), [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) | Unit tests in [`src/zone/update.zig`](../src/zone/update.zig): "an update adds an A record and raises the serial", "a duplicate add replaces the record's TTL", "delete one RR removes only the matching RDATA", "delete an RRset removes the type and keeps the rest", "delete every RRset at an apex name keeps the SOA and the NS", "prerequisites refuse with the RFC rcode" (NXDOMAIN, NXRRSET, YXDOMAIN, YXRRSET, FORMERR, NOTZONE), "a value-dependent prerequisite must match the RRset exactly", "an update outside the zone is NOTZONE", "an SOA update with a greater serial is kept", and "an SOA update with a stale serial is ignored and the serial still rises". Runtime tests in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig): "runtime applies a signed RFC 2136 update and persists the zone" (the added record answers, the serial is 2, the zone file holds the new record, the response is signed), "runtime refuses an unsigned or wrongly keyed RFC 2136 update", and "authoritative runtime answers RFC 2136 UPDATE with NOTIMP" for a zone with no policy. Config tests: "allow-update names a zone and its key", "allow-update rejects a duplicate apex and a missing key", and "allow-update for a zone or key the server does not hold fails the load" in [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig). |

| RSA/SHA-2 DNSSEC algorithms (RFC 5702) | [RFC 5702 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5702#section-2.1) and [§2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5702#section-2.2) (algorithm 8, RSA/SHA-256, keys of 512 to 4096 bits; algorithm 10, RSA/SHA-512, keys of 1024 to 4096 bits, with the DNSKEY carried in the RFC 3110 form) and [§3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5702#section-3) (the RRSIG over RSASSA-PKCS1-v1_5 with the hash the algorithm names). RFC 5702 is a Standards Track document; RFC 3110 carries the RSA/SHA-1 algorithms 5 and 7, which the IANA "DNS Security Algorithm Numbers" registry, as revised by RFC 9904, marks NOT RECOMMENDED under "Implement for DNSSEC Signing" and MUST under "Implement for DNSSEC Validation". | `partial`: RSA/SHA-256 (8) and RSA/SHA-512 (10) are implemented for signing and validation. The key file accepts a base64 PKCS#1 `RSAPrivateKey` DER on an `RSASHA256` or `RSASHA512` line; the DNSKEY RDATA carries the modulus and the public exponent in the RFC 3110 one-octet or three-octet length form, the key tag and DS digests follow the same paths as the other algorithms, and the signature is the single modular exponentiation `EM^d mod n` over the RFC 8017 §9.2 encoding, computed on the constant-time path of `std.crypto.ff`. The validator verifies the same encoding with the standard library's RSA PKCS#1 v1.5 verifier for the RFC 5702 key-size floors (512 bits for algorithm 8, 1024 bits for algorithm 10) up to 4096 bits. RSA/SHA-1 (5) and RSA/SHA-1-NSEC3-SHA1 (7) are refused for signing: the registry's "Implement for DNSSEC Signing" column marks them NOT RECOMMENDED, while its "Implement for DNSSEC Validation" column marks them MUST, the validation deviation this row records. | `partial`: algorithms 8 and 10, sign and validate, byte-identical to the RFC's own §6.1 and §6.2 vectors; algorithms 5 and 7 are not offered for signing, which complies with their NOT RECOMMENDED "Implement for DNSSEC Signing" value, while their MUST "Implement for DNSSEC Validation" value is not met and is recorded as the RSA/SHA-1 validation deviation; Ed448 (16) is the other recorded deviation, RECOMMENDED for validation, MAY for signing, and absent. | [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig), [`src/dns/validate.zig`](../src/dns/validate.zig) | [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig): "RFC 5702 section 6.1: the RSA/SHA-256 DNSKEY, key tag, and signature match the published vector" (the DNSKEY base64, key tag 9033, and RRSIG signature compare byte for byte with the RFC) and "RFC 5702 section 6.2: the RSA/SHA-512 DNSKEY, key tag, and signature match the published vector" (key tag 3740), plus "a zone signed with an RSA/SHA-256 key validates against its own DNSKEY", which runs the project's validator over a freshly signed zone. |

## Deliberately deferred or excluded work

| Area | Status | Boundary |
|---|---:|---|
| DNR provisioning artifacts | `not-applicable` | RFC 9463 provisions encrypted resolvers through DHCP/Router Advertisements, not DNS-server behavior; no DHCP, RA, parser, or output artifact is provided. |
| DNSSEC signing/validation, iterative recursion, root priming | `supported` for the signing and validation subset | Zone *signing* is implemented and operator-reachable (`dnssec-key-file`; ECDSA P-256/P-384, Ed25519, RSA/SHA-256, RSA/SHA-512; NSEC denial serving). Chain *validation* is implemented on both answer paths (`trust-anchor-file`; `src/dns/validate.zig`): secure answers set AD, bogus answers return SERVFAIL with the RFC 8914 code, insecure answers relay without AD; validation runs before the cache put so bogus is never stored. Iterative recursion resolves from the root hints, primes the root with the RFC 9609 `. NS` query and the addresses it names, resolves a glue-less delegation's name server over A and then AAAA (RFC 1034 section 4.3.2 names no address family, and an IPv6-only name server is otherwise unreachable), minimizes each hop to one label (RFC 9156 section 3 steps 6c and 6d, with a full-question fallback), and caches the delegations and the DS/DNSKEY chain of each answer zone; a DNSKEY anchor that names the signer zone ends the walk there, and a DS anchor that names it fetches only that zone's DNSKEY RRset, because the parent's word is already in the anchor. A forwarded answer that carries signatures fetches the same chain through the forwarder. A forwarded unsigned answer names no zone, so the trust domain decides: under a root anchor it relays insecure, because a root anchor does not prove any child zone signed; under an anchor naming an ancestor it is bogus as a stripped signature. A forward-only resolver cannot see an unsigned delegation below that anchor, so those answers fail closed. A client `DS` question starts at the parent, so a warm delegation never answers for its own DS. The gaps of the signing and validation subset itself are named in the authoritative zone signing row and the DNSSEC operations row below: the parent side of the RFC 6781 section 4.1.2 key-signing key rollover, RFC 5011 automated rollover of the trust anchors, which is deliberately not implemented by decision, and the two RFC 9904 algorithm deviations, RSA/SHA-1 (5 and 7) validation and Ed448 (16) validation.
| Encrypted-resolver discovery (RFC 9461/9462 DDR) | [RFC 9461 §4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9461#section-4) (`alpn` and `port` on a service-mode binding), [§5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9461#section-5) (the `dohpath` key and its `{?dns}` URI Template), [RFC 9462 §4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9462#section-4) (the designated-resolver SVCB RRset at `_dns.resolver.arpa.`) and [§5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9462#section-5) (the known resolver name `_dns.<target>`). | `partial`: the publication half is implemented. `ddr <target> <alpn> <port> [<dohpath>]` (repeatable) makes the resolver answer a `SVCB` (or `ANY`) query for `_dns.resolver.arpa.` with one service-mode SVCB record per configured endpoint, built with the RFC 9461 `alpn`, `port`, and `dohpath` keys, and a query for any other QTYPE at that name is NODATA rather than a forward. Section 5's known-name publication is implemented too: a query whose QNAME is exactly `_dns.<target>` for a configured target answers with the records of that target only (owner the query name, class IN, the DDR TTL) for `SVCB` and `ANY`, any other QTYPE at that name is NODATA, and a `_dns.<name>` this server was not configured for falls through instead of being claimed. An HTTP ALPN is refused without a `{?dns}` `dohpath` path, and a non-HTTP ALPN is refused with one, as §5 requires. The client half of RFC 9462 — discovering an upstream's designated resolver and switching this server's own forwarding to DoT or DoH — is not implemented, by decision: section 4.2 requires the designating resolver's certificate to be validated by checking its IP address against the certificate's iPAddress subjectAltName, and the pinned `inic/tls.zig` dependency (commit `bd22bcb73f423389441a1de80bf031ef1b623cce`, pinned in [`build.zig.zon`](../build.zig.zon)) parses the peer certificate into a `CertificateParser` that keeps only the leaf public key and signature and returns no peer certificate (`src/handshake_common.zig`), so that SAN cannot be read; section 4.1 forbids automatic use without that validation, and the opportunistic mode of section 4.3 (sharing a private or local-designated resolver) is not implemented either. Extending or replacing that dependency to expose the peer certificate is an owner decision. | [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig): "ddr names the SVCB record for an encrypted endpoint" (valid shapes plus the five refusals); [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig): "the resolver publishes RFC 9461 SVCB records at _dns.resolver.arpa" (two records answered with the configured target and non-empty SvcParams, and the NODATA answer for a QTYPE the name does not carry), "RFC 9462 section 5 known names answer with their own target's records" (two targets, each known name returns only that target's records with the query name as owner, and an `A` query at a known name is NODATA), and "RFC 9462 section 5 does not claim a known name this server was not configured for" (an unconfigured `_dns.<name>` gets the fall-through refusal). |
| DoT, DoH, DoQ, HTTP/3, ODoH server transports | `partial`: DoT is a user-facing transport. RFC 7858 DoT is served by `--serve-dot`, which terminates a TLS 1.3 handshake with the `dot` ALPN token and then speaks the RFC 7858 section 3.3 length-prefixed framing over that channel ([`src/transport/dot.zig`](../src/transport/dot.zig), [`src/transport/tls_server.zig`](../src/transport/tls_server.zig)); the port never answers in cleartext, because the handshake comes first and the identity is loaded before the bind. RFC 8484 DoH is served by `--serve-doh`, which terminates a TLS 1.3 handshake with the `http/1.1` ALPN token and then maps the RFC 8484 GET and POST requests to DNS wire bytes and answers with `application/dns-message` ([`src/transport/doh.zig`](../src/transport/doh.zig)); the port never answers in cleartext, and `--serve-xfr` serves the RFC 9103 strict-TLS transfer channel over the same TLS server; the encrypted ports answer through the same runtime entry point the cleartext TCP port uses, so the peer address reaches the recursion ACL, the DNS cookie check, and the local-zone table, and one query is answered the same way whichever port it arrives on; DoH advertises `http/1.1` only, never the unimplemented `h2`, so a client that requires HTTP/2 cannot use it; DoQ has no listener, no flag, and no configuration name in this build, because it needs a QUIC stack rather than TCP plus TLS. | Framing, mapping, ALPN, and fail-closed refusal evidence in the [`src/transport/tls.zig`](../src/transport/tls.zig), [`src/transport/dot.zig`](../src/transport/dot.zig), and [`src/transport/doh.zig`](../src/transport/doh.zig) unit tests, plus the startup-refusal and unbound-port test in [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig); the DoT path is proven on the wire by the TLS 1.3 handshake, the negotiated `dot` token, and the answered query in the DoT unit tests. Loopback TLS interop with outside clients (`kdig`, `curl --doh-url`) stays open, and HTTP/3 and ODoH stay out of scope. |
| AXFR/IXFR, NOTIFY, TSIG transfer chaining, catalog/secondary zones, DHCP, clustering, plugins, web/remote administration | `partial` for transfer message shaping: SOA-framed AXFR sequences with RFC 1995 IXFR-to-full fallback in [`src/transfer/axfr.zig`](../src/transfer/axfr.zig) are served over plain DNS/TCP by the ordinary authoritative TCP loop ([`src/platform/listener.zig`](../src/platform/listener.zig):984 hands a recognised AXFR/IXFR question to [`Runtime.encodeTransferFrames`](../src/auth/runtime.zig)) whenever `--serve-tcp` is running, and are also framed by the strict-TLS transfer channel in [`src/transfer/xfr_tls.zig`](../src/transfer/xfr_tls.zig), which answers a transfer query through the same `Runtime.encodeTransferFrames` the cleartext path uses, so the encrypted channel serves the same incremental RFC 1995 answer rather than always falling back to a full transfer, and which refuses out-of-zone and TSIG-signed transfer queries; `--serve-xfr` serves the RFC 9103 strict-TLS transfer channel, so the strict-TLS transfer path is reachable and not merely the Do53 TCP one, and that channel holds the same bounds as the DoT port: the operator's connection-duration limit over the handshake and every frame, one absolute deadline per frame on the raw descriptor, and the SIGHUP zone reload. | Transfer sequencing and refusal evidence in the [`src/transfer/axfr.zig`](../src/transfer/axfr.zig) and [`src/transfer/xfr_tls.zig`](../src/transfer/xfr_tls.zig) unit tests, plus the encrypted-listener refusal test in [`src/main.zig`](../src/main.zig) and the Do53 TCP socket tests in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig); BIND/Knot/NSD interop over a strict-TLS transfer channel is still open: the channel itself is served now, and the outside-client probe is the one an outside secondary needs. The secondary role's refresh and inbound transfer client, the catalog consumer's refresh path, DHCP, clustering, plugins, and web/remote administration stay out of scope. Ordinary RFC 8945 TSIG query authentication and response signing are tracked in the partial TSIG row above. The IXFR delta log the transfer answers come from is durable only when the operator names an `ixfr-journal-file`: the RFC 1982 row above carries that directive, the file it names, and the startup recovery that replays it. Without the directive the log lives in memory, bounded to eight transitions, and no file is written. The RFC 1995 section 5 purge rule is the RFC 1982 row's business: the incremental answer and the full transfer are measured in wire bytes, an incremental answer that is not shorter than the full transfer is refused in favour of the full transfer, and the difference that no longer produces a shorter answer is purged from the in-memory store; the zone index registers the size of the zone's full transfer when it loads the zone, so the test is applied to a live answer. The file is bounded rather than purged: the journal keeps the newest transitions, each difference bounded, so a change whose difference does not fit is not recorded and its client gets the full transfer. |
| DNS catalog zones (RFC 9432) | [RFC 9432 §4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9432#section-4) (a catalog zone is an ordinary zone: SOA and NS present, one NS `invalid.` recommended), [§4.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9432#section-4.1) (`<unique>.zones.<catalog>` PTR records naming the member zones) and [§4.2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9432#section-4.2.1) (the `version.<catalog>` TXT RRset, exactly one RR, value `"2"`). | `partial`: the producer role is implemented. `catalog-zone <apex>` synthesizes the catalog for the zones the configuration serves — the schema-2 `version` TXT record, one `<mN>.zones.<apex>` PTR per configured zone in configuration order, and the SOA and NS (`invalid.`) that make it a valid zone — and the result is loaded, served, signed by `dnssec-key-file`, and transferred like any other zone, so a secondary that consumes catalogs can provision from this one. The consumer role is implemented for startup provisioning: `catalog-consumer <catalog-apex> <primary:address:port>` fetches the catalog by AXFR over DNS/TCP, requires the schema-2 `version` TXT record, refuses the broken shapes (`§5.1`: a missing or unknown version, two member nodes naming the same zone, a member naming the catalog), and fetches every member the catalog lists, serving it from memory.
