A `NOTIFY` for the catalog apex (`§5.4`) makes the runtime read it again by AXFR, revalidate the schema-2 shapes, and apply the members as one swap: added zones are served, listed zones whose serial advanced are replaced, a member whose `<unique>` label changed is removed and added again with fresh state (`§5.4`), and zones the catalog stops naming are dropped; an unchanged catalog serial means an unchanged membership, so the member transfers are skipped, and a NOTIFY from an address other than the configured primary is ignored (`§5.4` with RFC 1996 §3.10). A member node whose PTR RRset holds more than one record is refused (`§4.1`), and every fetched zone whose apex ZONEMD digest does not match is refused without replacing the served member. There is no refresh timer, a `NOTIFY` for a member zone does not trigger a re-read, and the `coo`, `group`, and custom properties (`§4.3`, `§4.4`) are neither produced nor processed. | [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig): "server context publishes a catalog zone listing the served zones" (the zone carries one TXT, two PTRs, the SOA and NS, and the runtime answers `version` with `"2"` and `m1.zones` with the first member) and "server context refuses a catalog apex that names a served zone", "the catalog consumer provisions the members a catalog names" (two AXFR fetches over a loopback primary, the member answered by the runtime), and "the catalog consumer refuses a schema version this build does not implement", plus [`test/integration/catalog_refresh_test.zig`](../test/integration/catalog_refresh_test.zig): "a catalog consumer adds, replaces, and drops members on refresh" (a `NOTIFY` for the catalog requests the read, the catalog serial decides whether the members are fetched, and the member list is replaced); [`src/zone/catalog.zig`](../src/zone/catalog.zig): "a catalog's members are read and a broken one is refused" (schema-2 version, duplicate target, and self-listing member refused); [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig): "catalog-zone names one catalog apex" and "catalog-consumer names one catalog and its primary"; [`src/zone/xfr_client.zig`](../src/zone/xfr_client.zig): "an AXFR over DNS/TCP returns the transferred zone as text". |
| DNS Blacklists zone-serving convention (RFC 5782) | [RFC 5782 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5782#section-2) and [§2.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5782#section-2.4) (reversed-octet IPv4 entry names, reversed-nibble IPv6 entry names), [§3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5782#section-3) (listed domain names under the DNSxL domain), [§2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5782#section-2.1) (an A record per entry, no records for an unlisted address) and [§5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5782#section-5) (the mandatory and forbidden test entries). | `supported` for the server-side convention: `blacklist-zone <apex> <file>` publishes a zone whose entries are the exact addresses and names in the file, each with `A 127.0.0.2`, the §5 test entries are always present (127.0.0.2, ::ffff:127.0.0.2, test), an address or name §5 forbids fails the load (127.0.0.1, ::ffff:127.0.0.1, invalid), and an unlisted query is NXDOMAIN. Address ranges and the per-entry TXT reason record (a §3 SHOULD) are not published; both are named here rather than implied. | [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig): "server context publishes an RFC 5782 blacklist zone" (reversed-octet IPv4 owner, reversed-nibble IPv6 owner, listed-name owner, the §5 test entries, a listed answer with `127.0.0.2`, and NXDOMAIN for an unlisted entry) and "an RFC 5782 entry list the convention forbids fails the load" (127.0.0.1 and a CIDR range); [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig): "blacklist-zone names an apex and its entry list". |
| TSIG key lifecycle: algorithm names, key files, and rollover | `partial`: the algorithm names are the five HMAC-SHA names of RFC 8945 §6, `hmac-sha1`, `hmac-sha224`, `hmac-sha256`, `hmac-sha384`, and `hmac-sha512`, plus the three truncated variants of RFC 4868 §2.6, `hmac-sha256-128`, `hmac-sha384-192`, and `hmac-sha512-256`, which a key file may name and a request may carry; every other §6 entry is refused, because `HMAC-MD5.SIG-ALG.REG.INT` is a MUST NOT use there and `gss-tsig` delegates the cryptography to GSS that this server does not implement. The key file is BIND syntax `key "NAME" { algorithm X; secret "BASE64"; };` with the key name read from the file; an optional `expires YYYYMMDDHHMMSS;` statement may follow the secret and is read as UTC to the exact second, a key without it never expires, and at or after that second the key is inactive, so a request that names it is answered NOTAUTH with the unsigned BADKEY (17) record an unknown key gets (RFC 8945 §5.2.1 and §5.3.2). RFC 8945 §8 defines only shared-secret handling and no key lifetime, so the directive is this profile's operator extension, tied to RFC 2104 §3's periodic-refresh advice. The secret must be at least as long as the keyed hash output of the hash its algorithm uses (20, 28, 32, 48, or 64 octets) and at most 64 octets, so an `hmac-sha512` or `hmac-sha512-256` secret is exactly 64 octets; a truncated name keeps the key length of the hash it truncates (RFC 4868 §2.6), so its floor is 32, 48, or 64 octets, and that floor is the RFC 8945 §8 SHOULD, held for every algorithm rather than relaxed for the truncated ones, and the 64-octet ceiling is the project's own bound. The MAC length is checked against the algorithm the request names (RFC 8945 §5.2.2.1): a MAC longer than the keyed hash output is a format error, one shorter than the larger of 10 octets and half the length of the hash function in use is a format error, and any other length is a truncation that is compared truncated. For a truncated name the keyed hash output is its own truncated length (16, 24, or 32 octets) while the hash function in use is the full one (32, 48, or 64), so a truncated name accepts exactly one MAC length. One key name may carry several algorithms: the file may name the same key again with another algorithm, which is how a peer that rotates its algorithm stays verifiable, while the same name with the same algorithm is refused as a duplicate; the name with the algorithm is the key. Error responses use the RFC 8945 §3 codes BADSIG (16), BADKEY (17), BADTIME (18), and BADTRUNC (22). The key file's lifecycle is partly automated: the `tsig-key-file` path is parsed once at startup and re-read on request, so a key can be added, changed, or removed without a restart. A SIGHUP re-reads the key file as well as the zone file; the new keyring is built completely before it replaces the served one, and the swap happens under the lock the request path holds, so a request is verified against the old keyring or the new one, never a half-built one. A key file that is missing, unreadable, or malformed at reload time is reported on stderr like any other reload failure and the served keys stay in place, so the server keeps answering; a malformed key file is still fatal at startup. The secrets of the replaced keyring are wiped when it is dropped, as they are at startup. Still open: automatic rollover (RFC 2104 §3 asks for keys to be refreshed periodically; the operator refreshes one by replacing the key file and reloading, and the server neither retires nor schedules a key on its own), the RFC 8145 §5.2 `_ta-` key-tag query workflow, gss-tsig, and TSIG transfer chaining. | Key parse, canonicalization, duplicate name-and-algorithm, algorithm, and secret-length tests, including every supported algorithm name with its required secret length, the three truncated names with the hash-length secret they need, a request signed with a truncated name verified under the keyring key, the bundled key name, and the refusal of `HMAC-MD5.SIG-ALG.REG.INT` and `gss-tsig`, in [`src/auth/keyring.zig`](../src/auth/keyring.zig); the bundled request authentication and the same-key reply signing in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig); algorithm-name, digest-length, and per-algorithm MAC tests in [`src/dns/tsig.zig`](../src/dns/tsig.zig); an hmac-sha512 request and answer in [`test/unit/tsig_sha512_runtime_test.zig`](../test/unit/tsig_sha512_runtime_test.zig); BADSIG/BADKEY/BADTIME/BADTRUNC emission in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig); single-key-file startup loading in [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig); and byte-exact signed-response coverage in [`test/golden/tsig-response.gdns-fixture`](../test/golden/tsig-response.gdns-fixture). The key reload is proven in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig): a SIGHUP re-reads the key file, a key the file adds authenticates afterwards and one it removes is refused with BADKEY, a malformed or missing key file leaves the served keys answering, and the replaced keyring's freed secret slot is wiped. Key expiry is proven in [`src/auth/keyring.zig`](../src/auth/keyring.zig) ("keyring parses an optional UTC expiry", "keyring parses a leap-day expiry", "keyring activeAt treats the expiry second as inactive", and "keyring refuses malformed expiries, duplicates, and trailing statements"), and on the request path in [`src/auth/runtime.zig`](../src/auth/runtime.zig) ("runtime answers an expired TSIG key with unsigned BADKEY", which answers the same signed request NOERROR before the expiry second and NOTAUTH with one unsigned TSIG record carrying error 17 at it). |
| DNSSEC operations: key rollover, trust anchors, and algorithm policy | `partial`: the signer, the key-signing key separation, the child side of the RFC 6781 section 4.1.2 key-signing key rollover, and a static trust-anchor store are implemented and reachable; automated trust-anchor rollover is not, and the RFC 9904 audit records two algorithm deviations below | RFC 6781's key-signing key rollover is implemented on its child side, and RFC 5011's managed trust-anchor rollover is not implemented, by decision, while the DNSSEC signing and validation subset itself is implemented per the [DNSSEC deferral decision](../src/dns/README.md). [RFC 9904](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9904) ("DNSSEC Cryptographic Algorithm Recommendation Update Process") obsoletes [RFC 8624](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8624) and moves the canonical algorithm recommendations to the [IANA "DNS Security Algorithm Numbers"](https://www.iana.org/assignments/dns-sec-alg-numbers/) and ["Digest Algorithms"](https://www.iana.org/assignments/ds-rr-types/) registries; this build was audited against them on 2026-11-03. It validates algorithms 8, 10, 13, 14, and 15 and signs with 8, 10, 13, 14, and 15. For DS digests it computes 1 (SHA-1), 2 (SHA-256), and 4 (SHA-384), meeting the digest registry's "Implement for DNSSEC Validation" MUST for 1 and 2 and its RECOMMENDED for 4; the signer emits digest types 2 and 4 only, never 1, which the "Implement for DNSSEC Delegation" column marks MUST NOT. Two RFC 9904 algorithm deviations are recorded: RSA/SHA-1 (5 and 7) validation is an "Implement for DNSSEC Validation" MUST this build does not meet, and Ed448 (16) validation is RECOMMENDED and absent. RSA/SHA-1 is not offered for signing, which complies with its NOT RECOMMENDED "Implement for DNSSEC Signing" value. Under the "Use for" columns, 5 and 7 are MUST NOT and 10 is NOT RECOMMENDED for signing use; the signer offers 10 because the operator names the key, and never offers 5 or 7, while validation use is RECOMMENDED for 5, 7, 8, 10, 13, 14, 15, and 16 and this build validates all but 5, 7, and 16. There is a static trust-anchor store (`Anchors.parse` in `src/dns/validate.zig`, loaded via `trust-anchor-file`; DNSKEY and DS anchors); the RFC 5011 section 2.1 revocation is implemented: a DNSKEY that carries the REVOKE bit and signed the DNSKEY RRset itself is not usable as a trust anchor, so a zone whose only anchor is revoked has no chain of trust and validates as insecure rather than bogus, and the bit alone proves nothing without that self-signature; the client of such a zone is told DNSSEC Indeterminate (RFC 8914 INFO-CODE 5), because RFC 8914 has no code that names a revoked key. The revoked-key policy lives in one place in the validator: a key that comes from the zone's own DNSKEY RRset is revoked only where the resolver saw that key's own RRSIG over that RRset, which is the proof RFC 5011 section 3 names, and a configured DNSKEY anchor is revoked on the bit alone, because the anchor file holds no RRset and so no self-signature to see, so an operator who writes a revoked key into the anchor file has revoked it; there is no RFC 5011 automated rollover, and that is a decision rather than a gap — the operator's anchor file is the whole trust configuration, so the forwarder never learns a trust anchor on its own — and no signature-expiration policy beyond the fixed 30-day signing window; the child side of the RFC 6781 section 4.1.2 Double-Signature key-signing key rollover is implemented and operator-reachable: `dnssec-new-ksk-file <path>` names the second key-signing key, the zone publishes both key-signing keys and signs the apex DNSKEY RRset once with each of them, and the one DS the zone publishes still states the key `dnssec-ksk-file` names, so the parent swaps that DS and the operator waits; the parent-side DS edit and the TTL_DS wait stay the operator's duty, and the RFC 5011-style revoke phase of section 4.1.2.1 is implemented on the child side: the key to be removed is published with the RFC 5011 section 3 REVOKE bit set and still signs the DNSKEY RRset that carries it; signing is reachable through the `dnssec-key-file` configuration directive, with a 30-day validity window that a reload refreshes, and the RFC 6781 section 3.1 separation of the roles is implemented and operator-reachable: `dnssec-ksk-file <path>` names the key-signing key, the zone publishes the key-signing key with the Zone Key flag and the Secure Entry Point flag (257) beside the zone-signing key with the Zone Key flag alone (256), the one DS the zone publishes states the key-signing key, the DNSKEY RRset is signed by the key-signing key, and every other RRset by the zone-signing key (RFC 6781 section 3.2, RFC 4034 section 2.1.1). The trust-anchor path authenticates the DNSKEY RRset through the DS and, for the zone's own RRsets, trusts the key the DS names; the validator trusts every key of an authenticated DNSKEY RRset (RFC 4035 section 5.3.1), so a zone that separates its key-signing key from its zone-signing key validates secure: the DS names the key-signing key that signs the DNSKEY RRset, and the zone-signing key signs the zone's other RRsets (RFC 6781 section 3.1). What stays open: RFC 5011's automated trust-anchor rollover, which is deliberately not implemented by decision, the parent side and the timing of the RFC 6781 section 4.1 rollover, and the two RFC 9904 deviations named above; [`src/auth/keyring.zig`](../src/auth/keyring.zig) is a TSIG shared-secret keyring, not a DNSSEC keyring. Zone DNSSEC RRs are parsed from zone text and served opaque per the DNSSEC authoritative serving row, with no algorithm policy enforced on that opaque serving path. The evidence for the rollover half of this row is the authoritative zone signing row above: [`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig) (the double-signature KSK rollover test), [`src/config/loader.zig`](../src/config/loader.zig) (the startup test and the one test per refusal), [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) (the directive's shape), and the procedure in [`src/zone/README.md`](../src/zone/README.md). |
| Obsoleted TSIG, NAPTR, and TCP-requirements documents (RFC 2845 / 2915 / 5966) | `not-applicable` | RFC 2845, RFC 2915, and RFC 5966 are Standards Track documents that the RFC Editor records as obsoleted: RFC 2845 is obsoleted by RFC 8945, RFC 2915 by RFC 3403, and RFC 5966 by RFC 7766. Each successor already has a row here: the TSIG row (`partial`), the NAPTR row (`supported`), and the DNS over UDP/TCP transport row (`partial`). An obsoleted document defines no behavior of its own, so no row of its own is due; the claim for each subject lives in its successor's row. |
| Observed DNS resolution misbehavior (RFC 4697) | `not-applicable` | RFC 4697 (BCP 123) is advice to iterative resolvers: its §2.1.1 forbids an iterative resolver from querying a non-responsive zone's parent for the NS RRSet, and the remaining sections address lame-server caching, glue retransmission, and update-agent behavior. This server performs no iterative resolution — the forwarder sends the full QNAME to a configured recursive upstream ([`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig)) and never follows an NS or DS referral — so the RFC has no subject here. RFC 9520, which widens RFC 4697's requirement to all query types and all ancestor zones, is recorded in the comparison section below. |
| Internationalized domain names in applications (RFC 5891) | `not-applicable` | RFC 5891 is a Standards Track document: it is the protocol registries and applications use to convert U-labels to A-labels; its §3.2 states that IDNs appearing in DNS queries and responses MUST be A-labels, so a name server that stores the A-label form carries no IDNA obligation of its own. ZIMDNS applies no IDNA processing at all: `Name.fromText` in [`src/dns/name.zig`](../src/dns/name.zig) carries the octets it is given and folds only ASCII case, so an internationalized name must reach the server already in A-label form. |
| DNSSEC implementation notes (RFC 6840) | `supported` | RFC 6840 is a Standards Track document, and its clarifications are implemented. The §4.1 clarifications (an ancestor-delegation NSEC or NSEC3 must not prove names below the cut, and neither must an NSEC or NSEC3 with the DNAME bit), §4.3 (a NODATA proof must also check the CNAME bit), §4.4 (an insecure delegation proof must check the NS bit or an NSEC3 opt-out), and §5.2 (an unknown DS digest algorithm is a downgrade signal) are implemented in the validator ([`src/dns/validate.zig`](../src/dns/validate.zig); the CNAME-bit rule also in [`src/dns/dnssec.zig`](../src/dns/dnssec.zig)); section 2.1's NSEC3 support is implemented on the serving side (the serving row above) and in the validator's NSEC3 closest-encloser proofs, with the "RFC 6840 section 4.1: a parent delegation NSEC cannot prove a name below the cut" test as the anchor. The canonical form follows RFC 4034 section 6.2 with RFC 6840 section 5.1: the owner name is lowercased and the names inside the RDATA of the rule-3 types are lowercased, except the NSEC's next domain name, which is signed exactly as it is published and is therefore not folded; the RRSIG's Signer's Name is canonical in the signed data as RFC 4035 section 5.3.2 requires; and only the RRsets that comprise the non-existence proof are judged, so a record the response carries beside the proof does not affect the answer; an NSEC or NSEC3 that is not part of the proof is therefore not authenticated at all, because RFC 4035 section 5.4 requires the RRsets that comprise the proof and nothing more, with one exception: a wildcard-expanded answer also authenticates the closest encloser's own record when it carries it, because the aggressive cache uses that record (RFC 8198 section 5.3), although the record is not required for the verdict. A denial proof is used only when it is authenticated: the signature over the NSEC or NSEC3 RRset is verified with the signer's trusted key set (RFC 4035 section 5.4 and section 5.3). A proof whose signature does not verify makes the answer bogus (RFC 4035 section 5.5); a proof this build cannot authenticate, for an unsupported algorithm or a signer with no trusted key path, leaves the answer insecure (RFC 4035 section 5.2); a proof with no signature over it is dropped, and the denial it would have supported is then unproven, which makes the answer bogus when a configured anchor covers the zone and insecure otherwise. §3.1's BAD cache is implemented: the resolution-failure cache of RFC 9520 — a bounded 64-entry cache of an upstream SERVFAIL, REFUSED, or FORMERR and of an unreachable upstream, bound by the operator's `minimum-failure-cache-time`, and the cached DNSSEC validation failures — is recorded in the Negative answers and cache row above and in the comparison section below. §4.2 is implemented: an answer to QTYPE=* is judged on the RRsets it carries at the QNAME in the query class — every one of them is validated, and the answer is bogus when any of them fails — and an answer that carries none of them must prove the absence with the rules of RFC 4035 §5.4, so the denial path runs on the content of the answer and not on its length. An answer that carries a DNAME above the QNAME is an answer and not an absence: the DNAME redirects the query (RFC 6672 section 3.1), and the only NSEC that could cover the name is the DNAME owner's, which section 4.1 bars from proving anything below that owner. §2.2's RSA/SHA-2 algorithms are implemented (RFC 5702 row above): the validator verifies algorithms 8 and 10 before the other checks, under the same trust-anchor and algorithm policy as every other signature, and the RFC's own §6.1 and §6.2 signatures verify against the keys they publish. |
| Authenticated denial of existence commentary (RFC 7129) | `not-applicable` | RFC 7129 ("Authenticated Denial of Existence in the DNS") is an Informational memorandum: it states that it provides background commentary and context for the NSEC and NSEC3 mechanisms and defines no requirement a server must meet, so it is not a conformance target and this profile claims nothing against it. The denial-of-existence mechanisms it describes are carried by the DNSSEC authoritative serving minimum row above: NSEC denial serving, and the NSEC3 denial proofs built with the iteration count and salt the zone's own records carry (`nsec3Hash` in [`src/dns/dnssec.zig`](../src/dns/dnssec.zig), the closest-encloser proofs in [`src/dns/validate.zig`](../src/dns/validate.zig), and the proof selection in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig)). |
| EDNS EXPIRE option (RFC 7314) | `supported` | RFC 7314 is Experimental and defines EDNS option code 9 so that a secondary can honor the primary's SOA EXPIRE. The primary side is implemented and tested: a query carrying the zero-length EXPIRE option of section 2 is answered with the length-4 option of section 3.1, carrying the zone's SOA EXPIRE field in seconds and network byte order, a malformed option is answered FORMERR, and section 3.3 is honored because a server that is not authoritative for the zone never adds the option — a referral to a delegated child does not, and the forwarding path, which is authoritative for nothing, neither forwards nor answers with it. Out of scope for this profile: the secondary side's timed behavior, because this server has no refresh or expire timer — the section 3.2 answer, the section 4 rule that a secondary adds the option to its refresh and transfer queries, and the expire-timer update the returned value drives have no subject here. The option is interpreted in [`src/dns/rdata.zig`](../src/dns/rdata.zig) and answered in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig). The proof is the tests "RFC 7314 section 3.1: an EXPIRE request is answered with the zone's SOA EXPIRE value", "RFC 7314 section 2: the EXPIRE option is answered only when it was asked for", "RFC 7314 section 3.3: a non-authoritative answer carries no EXPIRE option", and "RFC 7314 section 2: a malformed EXPIRE query is answered FORMERR" in [`src/auth/response.zig`](../src/auth/response.zig), with the forwarding path covered by "buildUpstreamQuery strips the other client EDNS options but preserves payload size and DO" in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig). |
| DNSSEC key rollover timing (RFC 7583) | `out of scope` | RFC 7583 is Informational and describes the timing relationships of a DNSSEC key rollover. The server times nothing: it has no key-scheduling clock, no timer that retires a key, and no notion of when a parent has changed a DS. The rollover it does have is the child side of RFC 6781 section 4.1.2 (`dnssec-ksk-file` beside `dnssec-new-ksk-file`), and every wait that section asks for is the operator's: the wait of at least TTL_DS after the new DS RR is published at the parent, and the wait of one more maximum zone TTL between the revoked publication of the old key and its removal. The procedure is written down in [`src/zone/README.md`](../src/zone/README.md), so what this row excludes is the server-side timing, not the rollover itself. |
| QNAME minimisation (RFC 7816) | `not-applicable` | RFC 7816 is Experimental and is obsoleted by RFC 9156, so the obligation lives in the successor: the iterative walk asks one label per hop and falls back to the full question when a minimized hop cannot move it (RFC 9156 section 3 steps 6c and 6d, [`src/iterate.zig`](../src/iterate.zig)); the forwarder still sends the full QNAME upstream ([`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig); the "forwarder sends full QNAME upstream" test in [`test/integration/pipeline_test.zig`](../test/integration/pipeline_test.zig)). RFC 7816 adds no obligation beyond the one RFC 9156 already states. |
| Resolver priming and a local root zone (RFC 8109 / 9609 / 8806) | `partial` | RFC 8109 (BCP 209) and its successor RFC 9609 (BCP 209) describe the priming query a recursive resolver sends to fill its cache with the root zone's NS RRset and addresses; RFC 8806 (Informational) describes serving a copy of the root zone locally to a resolver. Priming is implemented on the iterative path: the walk starts from the built-in InterNIC root hints, sends the `. NS` priming query to the first hint that answers, keeps only the addresses the NS RRset names (an off-bailiwick additional record cannot add a server), and reuses the list until the shortest TTL of the answer expires; a failed priming query leaves the hints in place (`rootServers`, `primeRootServers`, `adoptRootServers` in [`src/resolver.zig`](../src/resolver.zig), test "RFC 9609 section 2: a priming answer fills the root server list"). Not implemented: a local root zone (RFC 8806, Informational) and a configuration directive naming alternate root servers; the hints are compiled in. RFC 8109 is obsoleted by RFC 9609, so the two priming documents are one claim. |
| NSEC3 parameter guidance (RFC 9276) | `supported` | RFC 9276 (BCP 236) gives guidance for choosing NSEC3 iteration and salt parameters and updates RFC 5155. The requirement of section 3.1 is implemented: "If NSEC3 must be used, then an iterations count of 0 MUST be used to alleviate computational burdens." A zone whose own apex NSEC3PARAM states another count, and a `dnssec-chain nsec3` directive that names one, are refused by the signer rather than published ([`src/zone/signer.zig`](../src/zone/signer.zig), the `InsecureChainParameters` refusal), and the same refusal is what a salted chain gets, proved by the test "RFC 9276 section 3.1: the signer refuses an NSEC3 chain that states a salt" in the same file and by the directive's own shape in [`src/config/parser.zig`](../src/config/parser.zig) ("config parser takes one signed chain and refuses every other shape", where a salt is refused and the empty salt is accepted). The rule of section 3.2 is implemented too: an answer proven by a chain whose iteration count is above 0 is not secure but insecure, and it carries the Extended DNS Error 27 that section 6 assigns to that answer ([`src/dns/validate.zig`](../src/dns/validate.zig), [`src/dns/dnssec.zig`](../src/dns/dnssec.zig)); a chain with 0 iterations is signed, served, and validated exactly as before. The recommendation of section 3.1 that an operator should not use a salt is enforced in the signer: a `dnssec-chain nsec3` directive that names a salt is refused at parse time, and a zone whose own apex NSEC3PARAM states a salt is refused at signing with the same error a non-zero iteration count gets, while the empty salt signs, serves, and validates exactly as before; the MAY of section 3.2 to answer SERVFAIL instead of insecure is not taken, the insecure answer is the one the RFC asks for; the MUST of section 3.2 that a validator returning an insecure answer still validates the signature over the NSEC3 record is met: the signature over the NSEC3 proof is verified before the answer is called insecure, and a proof whose signature does not verify makes the answer bogus. |

## Comparison baseline

The neutral a comparable DNS server comparison source is the immutable [a comparable DNS server `SupportedRFCs.md` inventory at commit ``](), captured on 2026-08-12. It is evidence for comparison scope, not a defect list. We do not infer that a comparable DNS server lacks a feature or RFC because a row is absent from this matrix or outside ZIMDNS's profile.

That inventory has two tables, and the second is not a DNS table. It is headed "The following is a list that the DHCP Server included with the a comparable DNS server DNS Server implements" and lists exactly eight RFCs: [RFC 2131](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2131) "Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol"; [RFC 2132](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2132) "DHCP Options and BOOTP Vendor Extensions"; [RFC 3396](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3396) "Encoding Long Options in the Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCPv4)"; [RFC 3397](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3397) "Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCP) Domain Search Option"; [RFC 3442](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3442) "The Classless Static Route Option for Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCP) version 4"; [RFC 4702](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4702) "The Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCP) Client Fully Qualified Domain Name (FQDN) Option"; [RFC 5417](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5417) "Control And Provisioning of Wireless Access Points (CAPWAP) Access Controller DHCP Option"; and [RFC 5859](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5859) "TFTP Server Address Option for DHCPv4". All eight are `out of scope` for ZIMDNS by design, not missing: ZIMDNS is a DNS-only server with no DHCP, DHCP-option, TFTP, or CAPWAP component, no configuration directive for one, and no wire code for any of these protocols, so an absence on this side of the comparison means scope, never a gap. This is the same kind of exclusion the DNR row records above, and it is recorded here rather than inferred so a reader of the comparison cannot mistake the boundary for a shortfall. Verified on 2026-10-05: the eight numbers and titles above were checked against the RFC Editor, and the pinned 2026-08-12 copy of `SupportedRFCs.md` is byte-identical (6021 bytes) to that file's `master` on the same date, so both tables are still exactly as captured.

### Coverage of the pinned inventory (2026-11-03)

Measured on 2026-11-03 against the pinned inventory captured on 2026-10-22 at commit ``: of the 73 RFCs in that inventory's DNS table, this matrix records a status for 60 — 31 `supported`, 28 `partial`, 1 `planned`, the last being roadmap intent rather than a user-facing capability — and an explicit exclusion for 13 — 12 `not-applicable`, 1 `out of scope`. The five counts below are RFC numbers, not matrix rows: each of the 73 RFCs appears in exactly one of them, while the ledger itself holds 53 rows in the two normative tables above and 22 in the deferred table, because one row can cover several RFCs and a row can cover RFCs the pinned inventory does not list. The DNS table holds 74 rows: those 73 RFCs and one expired IETF draft, `draft-vixie-dnsext-dns0x20`, which asks for 0x20 QNAME randomization; the DNS name case handling row above records that randomization as implemented in the `query` client only, behind the operator's `--0x20` flag, and nowhere else. Each RFC is listed once below, under the status this matrix records for it; where a document is obsoleted, the note names the successor that carries the claim.

| Status | Count | RFCs in the pinned DNS table |
|---|---:|---|
| `supported` | 31 | 1183, 2308, 2782, 3123, 3225, 3596, 4033, 4255, 4509, 4592, 5782, 6303, 6594, 6605, 6672, 6698, 6761, 6840, 7218, 7314, 7479, 7553, 7793, 7858, 8375, 8484, 8767, 9103, 9156, 9276, 9460 |
| `partial` | 28 | 1034, 1035, 1982, 1995, 1996, 2136, 3597, 4034, 4035, 5155, 5452, 5702, 5936, 6147, 6781, 6891, 7766, 7830, 7871, 8080, 8109, 8806, 8914, 8945, 8976, 9432, 9520, 9609 |
| `planned` | 1 | 9250 |
| `not-applicable` | 12 | 1886, 2845, 2915, 4635, 4697, 5891, 5966, 6844, 7129, 7671, 7816, 8709 |
| `out of scope` | 1 | 7583 |
| **Total** | **73** | the pinned DNS table's full contents, each RFC listed once |

Seven of the twelve `not-applicable` entries are documents the RFC Editor records as obsoleted (superseded) — RFC 1886 (obsoleted by RFC 3596), RFC 2845 (RFC 8945), RFC 2915 (RFC 3403), RFC 4635 (RFC 8945), RFC 5966 (RFC 7766), RFC 6844 (RFC 8659), and RFC 7816 (RFC 9156) — and each successor has a row of its own here: RFC 3596 `supported`, RFC 3403 `supported`, RFC 8945 `partial`, RFC 7766 `partial`, RFC 8659 `supported`, and RFC 9156 `supported`. RFC 8109 is obsoleted by RFC 9609, and the priming claim lives in the `partial` resolver-priming row above, which implements the RFC 9609 priming query and leaves the RFC 8806 local root zone and a configured hint list open. The remaining five `not-applicable` entries are scope decisions rather than obsoleted documents: RFC 4697 (iterative-resolver advice), RFC 5891 (IDNA conversion), RFC 7129 (Informational commentary on the NSEC and NSEC3 denial-of-existence mechanisms), RFC 7671 (DANE operational guidance binding TLS clients and servers), and RFC 8709 (SSH key algorithms).

RFC 9520 ("Negative Caching of DNS Resolution Failures", a Standards Track document that updates RFC 2308, RFC 4035, and RFC 4697) is `partial` here. What the profile implements is what RFC 9520 §2 leaves intact: NXDOMAIN and NODATA are not resolution failures, so they are cached under the RFC 2308 §3/§5 negative-TTL rule and cut below a cached denial by the RFC 8020 §3.2 descendant rule, and the §3.1 retry bound is met, because the forwarder makes two UDP attempts per upstream and retries a truncated answer over TCP, which §3.1 allows as a different transport. §3.2's MUST is now implemented — "Resolvers MUST implement a cache for resolution failures": an upstream SERVFAIL, an upstream REFUSED, an upstream FORMERR, and an unreachable upstream are held in a bounded 64-entry failure cache beside the answer cache, the next client for the same question is answered from it with the same RCODE and without a new upstream query, the entry expires on its own after the operator's `minimum-failure-cache-time` seconds (default 5, inside §3.2's one-second floor and five-minute ceiling) for the first failure of the question and after twice that time for a failure that keeps repeating, up to the RFC's five-minute ceiling, and a cached failure is never answered as an NXDOMAIN or a NODATA and never sets AD. §3.4's MUST to cache DNSSEC validation failures is implemented as well: a validation failure is held in the same bounded failure cache with the SERVFAIL RCODE and the RFC 8914 DNSSEC-bogus code the validator produced, and the next client for the same question is answered from it with the same RCODE and the same code and without a new upstream query. §3.2's backoff SHOULD is implemented: "Resolvers SHOULD employ an exponential or linear backoff algorithm to increase the cache duration for persistent resolution failures", so the time a failure is held doubles with every consecutive failure of the same question, from the operator's `minimum-failure-cache-time` seconds up to the RFC's five-minute ceiling, and a question that resolves again starts again at the operator's minimum. What is still open is RFC 4035 §4.7's restriction, which §3.4 keeps with its "with some restrictions" wording, that a resolver SHOULD answer from a cache of validation failures only after the number of failures for that question exceeds a threshold; the negative answers and cache row above records the same boundary.

RFC 4033 ("DNS Security Introduction and Requirements", a Proposed Standard that obsoletes RFC 2535 and updates RFC 1034) has no row of its own: the DNSSEC authoritative serving minimum row records that it defines no wire format, encoding, or message-processing rule, so it contributes no implementable requirement there. Its status follows the DNSSEC signing/validation row in the deferred table, which is `supported` for the signing and validation subset — zone signing is operator-reachable through `dnssec-key-file` and chain validation runs on the forwarding path. The gaps of the signing and validation subset itself stay with the authoritative zone signing row (Ed448 and the parent side of the RFC 6781 section 4.1.2 key-signing key rollover) and the DNSSEC operations row (RFC 5011 automated rollover and the two RFC 9904 algorithm deviations, RSA/SHA-1 (5 and 7) validation and Ed448 (16) validation).

This matrix cites 125 four-to-five-digit RFC numbers; the two three-digit citations are RFC 952 ("DoD Internet Host Table Specification") and RFC 959 (FTP), both named in the RFC 1123 row as background it does not cover — RFC 952 holds the label character syntax RFC 1123 §2.1 leaves open, and RFC 959 is the FTP requirements RFC 1123 §4.1.2 corrects — so neither is a covered RFC. Of the 125, 73 are in the pinned inventory's DNS table — every one of them — and 52 are not:

| Where the RFC sits in the pinned inventory | Count | RFCs |
|---|---:|---|
| In the inventory's second table — the DHCP server's — and cited only in the exclusion record above | 8 | 2131, 2132, 3396, 3397, 3442, 4702, 5417, 5859 |
| In neither a comparable DNS server table | 44 | 1010, 1122, 1123, 1918, 2104, 2181, 2535, 3152, 3403, 3986, 4253, 4343, 4868, 5001, 5011, 5735, 5737, 6052, 6598, 6604, 7828, 7873, 8020, 8078, 8145, 8198, 8482, 8499, 8624, 8657, 8658, 8659, 8805, 9018, 9210, 9461, 9462, 9463, 9471, 9499, 9567, 9619, 9660, 9904 |
| Total cited but absent from the inventory's DNS table | 52 | |

The eight DHCP numbers are excluded by design for the reason recorded above: ZIMDNS is a DNS-only server with no DHCP, DHCP-option, TFTP, or CAPWAP component, no configuration directive for one, and no wire code for any of these protocols, so their absence on this side of the comparison is scope and never a gap. The 44 in neither table are RFCs this matrix names and accounts for — among them RFC 2104 (HMAC key refresh), RFC 8020 (NXDOMAIN scope), RFC 8482 (minimal ANY answers), RFC 9471 (referral glue and TC), RFC 9619 (OPCODE 0 question count), and RFC 9660 (ZONEVERSION) are implementation rows, while RFC 9461 and RFC 9462 (encrypted-resolver discovery) have implementation rows (the publication half) and RFC 9567 (DNS Error Reporting) is an implementation row — so the pinned inventory is not a ceiling on what this matrix accounts for.

On 2026-10-21 the `master` copy of `SupportedRFCs.md` was fetched again and is still byte-identical to the pinned 2026-08-12 capture (6021 bytes), so the 2026-08-12 verification of that copy still holds as of this date. That is an observation about the pinned inventory on that date, not a re-claim about `master`; the earlier statement in this section stands as it was written.

## Review rule

Before a release or a status change, run the linked unit, golden, and socket-integration tests, update this dated matrix, and ensure README and the plans use the same status vocabulary. A row remains `partial` whenever any named subset or transport evidence is missing.
